Psychiatric Disorders, Chapter 6

Final Textbook 3, Psychiatric Disorders, full-length edition: chapter 6 of 11. The book goes up chapter by chapter; the A4 colour and black and white PDFs come once all 11 chapters are in.

Chapter 6 Anxiety and Obsessive-Compulsive and Related Disorders

In this chapter
  1. 6.1 Fear, Anxiety and the Neuroses: Classifying the Anxiety Disorders: tell normal fear and anxiety apart from an anxiety disorder, trace the idea of neurosis from Cullen to DSM-5, and compare how DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11 group these disorders and time their symptoms.
  2. 6.2 Panic Attacks, Panic Disorder and Agoraphobia: recognise a panic attack and its thirteen symptoms, and diagnose panic disorder and agoraphobia and tell each from its look-alikes.
  3. 6.3 Specific Phobia, Social Anxiety Disorder and the Names of the Phobias: describe the five types of specific phobia and the fear of scrutiny in social anxiety disorder, with their course, and decode the Greek names of the phobias.
  4. 6.4 Generalised Anxiety Disorder, Separation Anxiety Disorder and Selective Mutism: distinguish the excessive, uncontrollable worry of generalised anxiety disorder from normal worry, and diagnose the two anxiety disorders that usually begin in childhood.
  5. 6.5 Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder: The Clinical Picture: define obsessions and compulsions, apply the DSM-5-TR criteria and specifiers, and tell OCD apart from worry, delusion and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder.
  6. 6.6 Body Dysmorphic Disorder, Hoarding and the Body-Focused Repetitive Behaviours: describe body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder, trichotillomania and excoriation disorder, and explain why DSM-5 and ICD-11 group them with OCD.
  7. 6.7 The Biology of Anxiety and Obsessions: evaluate the genetic, temperamental, neurochemical and brain-circuit evidence on the anxiety disorders and OCD, including Klein's suffocation false alarm theory and PANDAS.
  8. 6.8 Learning, Cognition and Conflict: Psychological Explanations: evaluate the conditioning, cognitive and psychodynamic explanations of phobias, panic, worry and obsessions, and bring them together in Barlow's triple vulnerability theory.
  9. 6.9 Drug and Physical Treatments: compare the benzodiazepines, buspirone, antidepressants and other drugs used for these disorders, outline neurosurgery and deep brain stimulation for OCD, and place the psychotherapies alongside them.

Weightage. 32 of the 411 questions mapped in this book (7.8%) test this chapter, 17 of them from real past papers. 2 of its 9 sections are High yield.

In the first days of January 1908, in Vienna, a little boy of four and three-quarters began to be afraid of the street. On 7 January he set off as usual with his nursemaid for the Stadtpark, the city park, and on the way he started to cry and begged to be taken home. The next day his mother took him herself to the gardens of Schönbrunn, where he loved to go. He cried, did not want to leave the house, and was plainly frightened the whole way. On the way back, after much internal struggling, he told her what was wrong: "I was afraid a horse would bite me." That evening he wept again and said, "The horse will come into the room." In the Vienna of 1908 there was no escaping horses. They pulled the buses, the carts and the heavily loaded furniture vans, and the boy, whom the world knows as Little Hans, was most frightened of the bus horses and of horses straining at heavy loads, which he thought might fall down. At its worst he would go no further than the front door of the house. He called his fear "the nonsense".

His father, the music critic Max Graf, and his mother were both among the closest followers of Sigmund Freud, and since before Hans turned three the father had been noting down the boy's sayings for Freud. Now he recorded everything Hans said, did and dreamed about horses, and posted the reports to Freud. When Hans noticed him writing and asked why, his father explained that it was going to a Professor who could take away the nonsense. Freud guided the treatment through the father and saw the boy as a patient only once, for a short consultation in his rooms on the afternoon of 30 March 1908. Hearing that Hans was especially bothered by the things horses wear in front of their eyes and by the black round their mouths, Freud asked him, half in jest, whether his horses wore spectacles, whether his father did (the boy said no, against all the evidence), and whether the black round the mouth might be a moustache. Freud had seen in the horse a disguised father. On the way home Hans asked, "Does the Professor talk to God, as he can tell all that beforehand?" Yet Hans had also told his father something much simpler. Out shopping with his mother one day, he had watched a horse pulling a bus fall down in the street and lie there kicking, and he had been terribly frightened. "That was when I got the nonsense," he said, and his mother confirmed that the fear had broken out straight afterwards.

Freud published the case in 1909 as "Analysis of a Phobia in a Five-Year-Old Boy". In the same year he published a second famous case, a young man tormented by obsessional fears, known as the Rat Man, whom you will meet in Section 6.8. Half a century later Little Hans became a battleground. In 1960 the behaviour therapists Joseph Wolpe and Stanley Rachman argued that nothing in the father's notes required a hidden fear of the father: a small child had watched a horse collapse in front of him, and the fear of horses that followed was a conditioned fear (Book 2, Section 4.1.3). And the boy himself? In the spring of 1922 a young man introduced himself to Freud as Little Hans, now "a strapping youth of nineteen". He said that he was perfectly well and suffered from no troubles or inhibitions; his parents had divorced and each had married again. When he had read his own case history, he told Freud, it all seemed strange to him: he did not recognise himself and could remember nothing of it. He was Herbert Graf (1903 to 1973), who grew up to become an opera director, for many years at the Metropolitan Opera in New York.

Notice how many stories one small boy's fear can tell. For Freud it was a story of unconscious conflict, a forbidden feeling disguised as a horse. For Wolpe and Rachman it was a story of learning, a single terrifying event that left a lasting fear. A clinician today would add a third story: a child with an anxious temperament and a sensitive alarm system, ready to fire at the first real fright. This chapter asks which of these stories fits which disorder: the panic that strikes out of nowhere, the phobia of one thing, the worry about everything, and the obsessions that drive a person to wash until the skin cracks. Remember Hans; you will return to him at the end of the chapter.

6.1 Fear, Anxiety and the Neuroses: Classifying the Anxiety DisordersLower yield · not asked yet

Sit in an examination hall in the ten minutes before the papers are handed out and you can watch anxiety at work: the jiggling knees, the dry mouths, the third trip to the water cooler. Nobody in that hall has a mental disorder because their heart is racing. Yet a few of the people in it will one day sit in a clinician's room because of anxiety: the student who cannot make herself enter the hall at all, the one who has lain awake every night for a year worrying about everything from his marks to his father's blood pressure, the one who panicked once on a crowded bus and has not boarded a bus since. Three questions run through this section and through the whole chapter. What exactly are fear and anxiety? When do they stop being useful and become a disorder? And once they are a disorder, which disorder is it? The answer to the last question has changed several times in two and a half centuries, and that history explains why the disorders of this chapter are grouped as they are today.

Fear and anxiety: one alarm, two time frames

In everyday speech the two words are used almost interchangeably, but DSM-5-TR draws a clear line between them. It defines fear as the emotional response to real or perceived imminent threat, and anxiety as the anticipation of future threat. Imagine walking down a lane in your neighbourhood when a large dog breaks its chain and runs at you, barking. Your heart pounds, your breathing quickens, blood floods your muscles, and within a second you are running or reaching for a stone. That is fear: in DSM-5-TR's words, a surge of autonomic arousal of the kind needed for fight or flight, thoughts of immediate danger ("it will bite me") and escape behaviour. Now imagine the next morning, as you approach the same lane. No dog is in sight. Yet your shoulders are tight, you scan every gate, you listen for barking, and you think about taking the longer road round. That is anxiety: muscle tension and vigilance in preparation for a danger that has not yet arrived, and cautious or avoidant behaviour. Fear says "danger now"; anxiety says "danger might come".

David H. Barlow, whose research and textbook you will meet again and again in this chapter, makes the same distinction in his own words: anxiety is a negative mood state marked by bodily tension and apprehension about the future, whereas fear is an immediate alarm reaction to danger. ICD-11 uses almost the same contrast: fear "represents a reaction to perceived imminent threat in the present", while anxiety "is more future-oriented, referring to perceived anticipated threat". The two states overlap, of course; the anxious walker will be flooded with fear if a gate creaks open. And DSM-5-TR adds a point that matters clinically: the level of fear or anxiety is sometimes reduced by pervasive avoidance. A man who never goes near the lane may report very little fear at all, because the avoidance has done its work. An absence of felt fear is not proof that there is no disorder; sometimes the avoidance is the symptom.

The physiology of fear, the emergency reaction that Walter Cannon called fight or flight, and the brain's routes to it are taught in Book 1 (Sections 11.6.1 and 11.8.2); here you need only its shape. Fear is fast, intense and tied to something present. Anxiety is slower, more diffuse and tied to something expected. One particular kind of fear response needs a name of its own, because it turns up in almost every disorder of this chapter: the panic attack, an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort that reaches its peak within minutes. Barlow describes it as a false alarm, the full fight-or-flight reaction firing when there is nothing to fight and nothing to flee. Section 6.2 takes it apart symptom by symptom.

Neither emotion is a disease. Fear keeps you alive, and anxiety keeps you prepared. Howard Liddell (1949) called anxiety "the shadow of intelligence": the price a species pays for being able to plan for a future in which things might go wrong. Moderate anxiety improves performance; too little leaves you careless, and too much leaves you paralysed. That inverted-U relation between arousal and performance is the Yerkes-Dodson law (Robert Yerkes and John Dodson, 1908), taught with its own exam trap in Book 1, Section 10.3.5. Every clinician meets both ends of the curve: the student who barely studied because she felt no anxiety at all, and the student who knew every answer but could not read the questions because his hands were shaking.

Exam trap

Fear is the response to real or perceived imminent threat (autonomic surge, thoughts of immediate danger, escape). Anxiety is the anticipation of future threat (muscle tension, vigilance, cautious avoidance). A panic attack is a kind of fear response that occurs at the wrong time. Two distractors to reject: "fear is irrational and anxiety rational" (either can be proportionate or excessive), and "anxiety always has a definite object" (anxiety is often objectless, as in the free-floating anxiety of generalised anxiety disorder, Section 6.4).

When anxiety becomes a disorder

Fear and anxiety also follow a normal developmental timetable. Around the first birthday most infants show stranger anxiety, wariness and distress at unfamiliar faces, and protest when a parent leaves the room; DSM-5-TR notes that heightened separation anxiety at this age is part of normal development and may signal that a secure attachment is forming. Many young children then pass through phases of fearing the dark, imaginary creatures or animals, and most of these fears fade on their own as the child faces them and grows. (The development of attachment and of children's fears belongs to the Developmental book.) A clinician therefore needs rules for deciding when anxiety has stopped being ordinary. DSM-5-TR gives four tests and two exclusions, and each exists for a reason, so learn the reason with the rule.

  • It is excessive or out of proportion. The fear or anxiety is out of proportion to the actual threat and to the person's sociocultural context. Who decides? The clinician, not the patient, because, as DSM-5-TR puts it, people with anxiety disorders typically overestimate the danger in the situations they fear or avoid. The clinician must also take culture into account: DSM-5-TR's own examples are that a fear of the dark may be reasonable where there is ongoing violence, and that the point at which a fear of insects becomes disproportionate is different where insects are part of the diet.
  • It persists beyond the developmentally appropriate age. A two-year-old who screams when her mother leaves is behaving normally; a ten-year-old who cannot sleep unless his mother is in the room and vomits every school morning is not.
  • It is persistent. The disorders typically last 6 months or more, and this rule separates them from transient fear or anxiety, which is often brought on by stress and passes when the stress does. DSM-5-TR calls the duration "a general guide with allowance for some degree of flexibility", and it is shorter in children for two disorders: 4 weeks for separation anxiety disorder and 1 month for selective mutism. Panic disorder has its own 1-month rule (Section 6.2).
  • It causes clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning, the "distress" and "dysfunction" of the four Ds (Book 2, Section 1.6.5).

The two exclusions apply to every anxiety disorder. The symptoms must not be attributable to the physiological effects of a substance or medication or to another medical condition (the last subsection of this section explains what is diagnosed instead), and they must not be better explained by another mental disorder (each section of this chapter has its own list of look-alikes).

Push a case through the tests and they come alive. Ritu, aged 21, is in the final year of a BSc in Pune. In the month before her university examinations she sleeps badly, her stomach churns every morning and she cannot stop picturing what will happen if she fails. Her mother wonders whether she needs a psychiatrist. Does Ritu have an anxiety disorder? Her anxiety is proportionate to a real event that matters to her; it is developmentally ordinary for a student; it began with the examination timetable, and she tells you that last year it disappeared within days of the final paper; and although it is unpleasant, it has made her study more, not less. She fails every test. Now change the facts. Suppose the worry began two years ago and has continued through holidays and good results alike; it ranges from her marks to her father's health to whether the house will be burgled; and she is now too tired and tense to attend half her lectures. The anxiety is out of proportion, persistent and impairing, and Section 6.4 will tell you what to call it.

Exam trap

Whether a fear is excessive is judged by the clinician, taking cultural context into account, because sufferers overestimate danger. The "6 months" is typical, a general guide, and it is shorter for separation anxiety disorder in the young (4 weeks) and for selective mutism (1 month). Transient, stress-induced anxiety (before an examination, after a quarrel) is not a disorder, however intense it feels.

The neuroses, from Cullen to Freud

Until 1980 most of the disorders in this chapter shared a different family name: they were neuroses. The word is much older than psychoanalysis, and examiners like to ask who coined it. It was the Scottish physician William Cullen (1710 to 1790), who taught medicine at Glasgow and then Edinburgh. In 1769, in his Synopsis Nosologiae Methodicae (a methodical classification of diseases), Cullen set up a whole class of neurosis (plural neuroses), and in his later textbook, First Lines of the Practice of Physic, he defined it as "all those preternatural affections of sense or motion" that occur without fever as part of the primary disease and that depend not on a local disease of some organ but on "a more general affection of the nervous system". (Sources differ slightly on the date, because Cullen refined the class in later editions and in First Lines; 1769 is the date usually given for the coinage.) Cullen's neuroses were a huge and mixed class. He divided them into four orders: comata (losses of voluntary movement and of the senses, such as apoplexy and palsy), adynamiae (failures of the vital functions, such as fainting), spasmi (spasms and convulsions without fever) and vesaniae (disorders of judgement, that is, insanity). At first, then, a neurosis was simply a disease of the nerves.

Over the following century the class shrank. Each time physicians found a lesion behind one of the "nervous" diseases, that disease passed to neurology or general medicine, and the word neurosis was left with the conditions for which no bodily cause could be found: hysteria, hypochondria, nervous exhaustion, the obsessions and the phobias. Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) narrowed the word again and gave it a theory. For Freud a neurosis was a disorder that arose from unconscious conflict, from the anxiety that the conflict produced and from the defences the ego raised against that anxiety. The neurotic patient suffered, sometimes terribly, but kept contact with reality and knew that something was wrong; that is what separated neurosis from psychosis.

In January 1895 Freud published a paper with a magnificent title, "On the Grounds for Detaching a Particular Syndrome from Neurasthenia under the Description 'Anxiety Neurosis'". Neurasthenia, "nervous exhaustion", was then the fashionable diagnosis for almost any vague nervous complaint (it is taught in Chapter 7). Freud argued that one group of these patients had something different, an anxiety neurosis, and his description reads like a first draft of two modern disorders. Its "nuclear symptom", he wrote, was anxious expectation: a quantity of anxiety present in a "freely floating" state (frei flottierend), ready to attach itself to any idea that came along. That is the ancestor of the free-floating worry of generalised anxiety disorder. But the anxiety could also break into consciousness suddenly, without any train of thought to call it up, as an anxiety attack: palpitations, breathlessness, sweating and the conviction of dying, of a stroke or of going mad, and sometimes only "rudimentary" attacks with one or two of these features. That is the ancestor of the panic attack and of the limited-symptom attack. Freud's theories of where anxiety comes from are taught in Section 6.8; here notice only that the clinical picture was described more than a century ago.

Fourteen years later, in the case of Little Hans, Freud gave the phobias a psychoanalytic name of their own. For phobias like Hans's, which he believed were the commonest of all the psychoneuroses and the neuroses of childhood above all, he proposed the term anxiety hysteria: a hysteria whose symptom is anxiety rather than a bodily "conversion" symptom such as a paralysis. The name has outlived the theory in one odd corner. ICD-10 still lists "anxiety hysteria" as an inclusion term under F41.8, other specified anxiety disorders.

By the middle of the twentieth century psychiatry divided the mental disorders that had no known organic cause into two great classes, the neuroses and the psychoses, and the line between them was drawn by insight and by contact with reality. The second edition of the American manual, DSM-II (1968), put the idea in a few sentences: anxiety is "the chief characteristic of the neuroses"; the neuroses, unlike the psychoses, show "neither gross distortion or misinterpretation of external reality, nor gross personality disorganization"; and neurotic patients, "however severely handicapped by their symptoms", are not classed as psychotic "because they are aware that their mental functioning is disturbed". Table 6.1 sets out the old contrast. You will still hear clinicians say "neurotic" and "psychotic" in this sense, and Indian examination notes still list the features of neurosis, so the table is worth knowing even though neither manual now uses neurosis as a diagnosis.

Table 6.1 The old divide between the neuroses and the psychoses.
FeatureNeurosisPsychosis
Contact with realityPreserved: no delusions or hallucinationsLost: delusions, hallucinations or grossly disorganised thinking
InsightPresent: the person knows something is wrong and wants reliefOften absent: the person may deny being ill
PersonalityIntact: the person remains recognisably themselvesOften grossly disorganised
How symptoms are experiencedAs unwanted and distressing (ego-dystonic, Section 6.5)As real events in the world, not as symptoms
Classic examples (old names)Anxiety, phobic, obsessional and hysterical neurosesSchizophrenia, manic-depressive psychosis
Status todayDropped from DSM in 1980 and from ICD-11Still used for the psychotic disorders (Chapter 4)
Exam trap

William Cullen coined "neurosis" in 1769, for disorders of sense and motion arising from the nervous system without fever or local disease. Freud did not coin it; he narrowed it to non-psychotic disorders of unconscious conflict, described anxiety neurosis in 1895 by separating it from neurasthenia, and called phobias anxiety hysteria (1909). In the neurosis, insight and reality testing are preserved; that is what made it "not psychotic".

From DSM-I to DSM-5-TR: how the anxiety disorders took shape

The first American manual, DSM-I (1952), borrowed Adolf Meyer's language of "reactions" (Book 2, Section 2.10.3) and grouped the neuroses as "psychoneurotic disorders": anxiety reaction, dissociative reaction, conversion reaction, phobic reaction, obsessive compulsive reaction and depressive reaction. DSM-II (1968) renamed them "neuroses": anxiety neurosis, hysterical neurosis (with conversion and dissociative types), phobic neurosis, obsessive compulsive neurosis, depressive neurosis, neurasthenic neurosis, depersonalization neurosis and hypochondriacal neurosis. Notice that the list mixes the disorders of this chapter with disorders that now live in Chapters 5 and 7. That mixture was held together by a theory, not by a shared clinical picture.

Then came the break. DSM-III (1980), produced under Robert L. Spitzer (1932 to 2015), tried to describe disorders by what could be observed and reported rather than by their presumed causes. The term neurosis was dropped, for two reasons that Barlow gives: it implied a specific but unproven psychoanalytic cause, and it was too vague, applying to almost every non-psychotic disorder. The phobias, instead of being classed under the broad heading of neurosis, were given their own place inside a new, broader group, the anxiety disorders. The old anxiety neurosis was split into two disorders, panic disorder and generalised anxiety disorder (Section 6.2 tells how a drug helped to split them). The old phobic neurosis was divided into agoraphobia (with or without panic attacks), social phobia and simple phobia. Obsessive-compulsive disorder joined the group, and so did a new diagnosis, post-traumatic stress disorder. The rest of the old neuroses were scattered: hysterical neurosis went to the somatoform and dissociative disorders, and depressive neurosis became dysthymic disorder among the mood disorders.

DSM-III-R (1987) and DSM-IV (1994) revised the criteria but kept the shape. OCD and PTSD remained anxiety disorders, and DSM-IV added acute stress disorder, PTSD-like symptoms in the first month after a trauma, to the same group. Several disorders that belong to this chapter today lived elsewhere: separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism among the disorders usually first diagnosed in infancy, childhood or adolescence, body dysmorphic disorder among the somatoform disorders, and trichotillomania among the impulse-control disorders. DSM-III-R had also folded most agoraphobia into panic disorder, as "panic disorder with agoraphobia", and DSM-IV kept that arrangement.

DSM-5 (2013) broke the group into three neighbouring chapters (Figure 6.2). The first kept the name anxiety disorders. The second, the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, gathered OCD with body dysmorphic disorder, trichotillomania and two new diagnoses, hoarding disorder and excoriation (skin-picking) disorder. DSM-5-TR explains the move by "the increasing evidence of these disorders' relatedness to one another in terms of a range of diagnostic validators" and by the clinical usefulness of grouping them. In plain terms, these disorders share repetitive thoughts and driven repetitive behaviours, and they overlap in family history, brain circuitry, course and response to treatment (Sections 6.7 and 6.9). OCD, for example, improves with serotonergic antidepressants and with exposure and response prevention but not with the benzodiazepines that calm many anxious patients. The manual still placed the new chapter immediately after the anxiety disorders, to signal that OCD remains a close neighbour. The third chapter, the trauma- and stressor-related disorders, took PTSD and acute stress disorder (with the adjustment disorders), because these diagnoses require an identifiable stressful event and because anxiety is only one of several responses to trauma, alongside anger, guilt, shame, numbing and dissociation (Chapter 7).

Two disorders moved in. Separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism left the childhood section and became anxiety disorders, and DSM-5 made it explicit that separation anxiety disorder can begin in adulthood. Agoraphobia was unhitched from panic disorder and became an independent diagnosis, so that a person who meets both sets of criteria receives both (Section 6.2). And the panic attack became a specifier that can be attached to any disorder ("social anxiety disorder, with panic attacks"; "major depressive disorder, with panic attacks"). DSM-5-TR (2022) kept this arrangement, and with it DSM-5's developmental ordering of the anxiety chapter by typical age at onset: separation anxiety disorder, selective mutism, specific phobia, social anxiety disorder, panic disorder, agoraphobia and generalised anxiety disorder, followed by substance/medication-induced anxiety disorder, anxiety disorder due to another medical condition, and the other specified and unspecified categories. The editions and their politics are told in Book 2, Sections 2.10.3 and 2.10.4; Figure 6.1 gathers the dates you need for this chapter.

Exam trap

In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR, OCD is not an anxiety disorder: it heads the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. PTSD and acute stress disorder are not anxiety disorders: they are trauma- and stressor-related. Body dysmorphic disorder is an obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, not a somatic symptom, anxiety or personality disorder. Separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism are anxiety disorders, not childhood or communication disorders. A phobia is an anxiety disorder, not a mild stress reaction, a personality disorder or a passing hallucination. And it was DSM-III (1980), not DSM-5, that dropped the term neurosis.

The anxiety disorders at a glance

With the history behind you, the modern map is easy to read. DSM-5-TR observes that the anxiety disorders tend to be "highly comorbid with each other", yet they "can be differentiated by close examination of the types of situations that are feared or avoided and the content of the associated thoughts or beliefs". That sentence holds the most useful diagnostic habit in this chapter: ask what the person fears, and what they think will happen. The child who clings at the school gate fears that something terrible will happen to her mother while they are apart. The student who freezes in his viva fears that the examiners will see him tremble and think him a fool. The woman who will not take the metro fears that she will panic and be unable to get out. The man who has worried for years about money, his children's safety and his own health has no single focus at all. Specific phobia is the one exception, and DSM-5-TR says so: in specific phobia "a specific cognition is not featured"; the fear attaches to the dog, the needle or the height itself. Figure 6.3 turns the question into a sorting key.

Table 6.2 then lays out the seven disorders in DSM-5-TR's order, with the duration each requires, when it usually begins and how common it is. Treat it as a map, not as a set of criteria: the criteria themselves are taught, group by group, in Sections 6.2 to 6.4, and the figures in the last two columns are DSM-5-TR's, drawn mostly from American and European surveys. Read the onset column from top to bottom and you will see the logic of the chapter's order: separation anxiety and selective mutism in early childhood, specific phobia in childhood, social anxiety in early adolescence, panic disorder and agoraphobia in early adulthood, and generalised anxiety disorder, with a mean age at onset of 35 years in North America, latest of all.

Table 6.2 The DSM-5-TR anxiety disorders at a glance, in the manual's order (onset and 12-month prevalence from DSM-5-TR; ICD-10 code in brackets).
DisorderWhat is feared; the core ruleDurationTypical onset12-month prevalence
Separation anxiety disorder (F93.0, a childhood disorder)Separation from attachment figures or harm to them; three or more of eight signsAt least 4 weeks in children and adolescents; typically 6 months in adultsFrom the preschool years; can begin in adulthoodAbout 4% of children (over 6 to 12 months); 1.6% of US adolescents; 0.9 to 1.9% of US adults
Selective mutism (F94.0, "elective mutism")Failure to speak where speech is expected (school) while speaking elsewhereAt least 1 month, not only the first month of schoolUsually before age 5; often noticed at school entry0.03 to 1.9% (point prevalence in clinic and school samples)
Specific phobia (F40.2)A circumscribed object or situation; fear almost always immediate; avoided or enduredTypically 6 months or moreEarly childhood; most before 10 (median 7 to 11)8 to 12% in the US; about 6% in Europe; 2 to 4% in Asia, Africa and Latin America
Social anxiety disorder (F40.1, "social phobias")Scrutiny; acting in a way, or showing anxiety, that will be negatively evaluatedTypically 6 months or moreMedian 13 years; 75% between 8 and 15About 7% in the US; 0.5 to 2% in much of the world
Panic disorder (F41.0)Recurrent unexpected panic attacks, then worry about more attacks or a maladaptive change in behaviourAt least 1 month of worry or behaviour change after an attackMedian 20 to 24 years (US)2 to 3% (US and several European countries)
Agoraphobia (F40.0)Two or more of five situations, because escape might be difficult or help unavailableTypically 6 months or moreMean 21 years; 25 to 29 without earlier panic1 to 1.7% each year
Generalised anxiety disorder (F41.1)Many events; excessive, hard-to-control worry with three or more of six symptoms (one in children)More days than not for at least 6 monthsMean 35 years (North America)2.9% of US adults; 0.9% of US adolescents

The duration column repays a second look, because the papers test it again and again. Six months is the usual rule, but it is not universal, and the exceptions have reasons. Children's lives change quickly, and a month of refusing school or of silence in the classroom is already costly, so the two childhood disorders run on shorter clocks; the selective mutism rule also excludes the first month of school, when many shy children are briefly silent in a strange new room. Panic disorder is defined not by how long the attacks have gone on but by what they do to the person afterwards: a month or more of worry, or of changed behaviour, after at least one attack.

Exam trap

Learn the clocks as a set. GAD: more days than not for at least 6 months in adults, not 1, 3 or 12 months. Specific phobia, social anxiety disorder, agoraphobia: typically 6 months, not 1 week, 1 month or 3 months. Panic disorder: 1 month of persistent worry or maladaptive behaviour change after at least one attack. Separation anxiety disorder: 4 weeks in children and adolescents, typically 6 months in adults. Selective mutism: 1 month, not limited to the first month of school. "Typically 6 months" applies to separation anxiety disorder only in adults.

The same disorders in ICD-10 and ICD-11

The World Health Organization's classifications tell the same story with a different ending. ICD-10, approved by the World Health Assembly in 1990 and published with its clinical descriptions and diagnostic guidelines for mental disorders in 1992, never gave up the neurotic umbrella. Its block F40 to F48 is called neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders, and its contents are, more or less, everything that was once a neurosis:

  • F40, phobic anxiety disorders, in which anxiety is evoked only, or mainly, in well-defined situations that are not currently dangerous: F40.0 agoraphobia (F40.00 without and F40.01 with panic disorder), F40.1 social phobias and F40.2 specific (isolated) phobias.
  • F41, other anxiety disorders, in which the anxiety is not restricted to any particular situation: F41.0 panic disorder, subtitled "episodic paroxysmal anxiety"; F41.1 generalised anxiety disorder; F41.2 mixed anxiety and depressive disorder (Chapter 5); F41.3 other mixed anxiety disorders; and F41.8 other specified anxiety disorders, which still includes "anxiety hysteria".
  • F42, obsessive-compulsive disorder, with subtypes for predominantly obsessional thoughts or ruminations (F42.0), predominantly compulsive acts (F42.1) and mixed obsessional thoughts and acts (F42.2).
  • F43, reaction to severe stress, and adjustment disorders (acute stress reaction, PTSD and the adjustment disorders) and F44, dissociative [conversion] disorders, both taught in Chapter 7.
  • F45, somatoform disorders, including hypochondriacal disorder (F45.2), under which ICD-10 also files non-delusional dysmorphophobia and nosophobia, the fear of having a disease (Chapter 7 and Section 6.6).
  • F48, other neurotic disorders: neurasthenia (F48.0), depersonalisation-derealisation syndrome (F48.1) and other specified neurotic disorders (F48.8), whose inclusion list names psychasthenia and Dhat syndrome (Chapters 1 and 7).

There are no codes F46 or F47; the block simply stops at F48. ICD-10 also settles the relationship between phobia and panic the opposite way round from DSM-IV. "A panic attack occurring in an established phobic situation is regarded as an expression of the severity of the phobia, which should be given diagnostic precedence", and panic disorder is to be the main diagnosis "only in the absence of any of the phobias listed in F40". A woman with agoraphobia who also has panic attacks therefore receives F40.01, agoraphobia with panic disorder, rather than a panic disorder code. ICD-10 adds a second hierarchy: panic disorder should not be the main diagnosis if the person had a depressive disorder when the attacks began, because the attacks are then probably secondary to the depression.

The ICD-10 codes also solve a small puzzle in DSM-5-TR. The American manual prints an ICD-10-CM code (the United States' version of ICD-10) beside every diagnosis, and some of these codes betray where a disorder used to live. Separation anxiety disorder carries F93.0, from ICD-10's "emotional disorders with onset specific to childhood". Selective mutism carries F94.0, from the "disorders of social functioning with onset specific to childhood and adolescence", where ICD-10 called it elective mutism. Body dysmorphic disorder carries F45.22, a somatoform code, and trichotillomania F63.3, from ICD-10's "habit and impulse disorders"; Section 6.6 explains both moves.

ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in May 2019 and in effect from 1 January 2022, finally abandoned the neurotic block. Its chapter on mental, behavioural or neurodevelopmental disorders contains a grouping of anxiety or fear-related disorders (codes 6B00 to 6B0Z): generalised anxiety disorder (6B00), panic disorder (6B01), agoraphobia (6B02), specific phobia (6B03), social anxiety disorder (6B04), separation anxiety disorder (6B05) and selective mutism (6B06), with other specified (6B0Y) and unspecified (6B0Z) categories. Like DSM-5, ICD-11 moved separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism out of the childhood section and into this grouping. Its definition echoes DSM-5-TR: fear is a reaction to perceived imminent threat in the present, anxiety is more future-oriented, and "a key differentiating feature" among these disorders is their "disorder-specific foci of apprehension". Like DSM-5, too, ICD-11 gives OCD a grouping of its own, the obsessive-compulsive or related disorders (6B20 to 6B2Z), whose membership differs from DSM's in ways that Section 6.6 explains, and it places PTSD among the "disorders specifically associated with stress" (Chapter 7). Anxiety caused by a drug is coded with the drug in the substance section (alcohol-induced anxiety disorder, for example, is 6C40.71), and anxiety caused directly by a medical illness is called secondary anxiety syndrome (6E63).

Where the systems still differ is in their clocks. DSM-5-TR counts: 6 months, three of six symptoms, two of five situations. ICD-11 prefers clinical description and flexible language. For generalised anxiety disorder it asks for marked symptoms of anxiety that "persist for at least several months, for more days than not", with no fixed minimum number of accompanying symptoms. ICD-10's guidelines had been looser still, asking for anxiety on most days "for at least several weeks at a time, and usually several months" (Section 6.4 compares the three). Table 6.3 shows where each disorder of this chapter lives in the three systems.

Table 6.3 Where the disorders of this chapter live in DSM-5-TR, ICD-10 and ICD-11.
DisorderDSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-10 (1992)ICD-11 (in effect 2022)

AgoraphobiaAnxiety disorders (F40.00), independent of panic disorderF40.00 without, F40.01 with panic disorder6B02, anxiety or fear-related

Post-traumatic stress disorderTrauma- and stressor-related (F43.10)F43.1, reaction to severe stress6B40, disorders specifically associated with stress
Exam trap

The ICD-10 block is F40 to F48, "neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders" (some exam notes wrongly write F40 to F49). OCD left the anxiety disorders in DSM-5, but in ICD-10 it is still F42, inside the neurotic block next to the anxiety disorders; ICD-11, like DSM-5, gives it a grouping of its own. So a contamination-washing patient is diagnosed with OCD in every system, never with GAD, illness anxiety or specific phobia, whatever chapter OCD sits in. ICD-11 asks for GAD symptoms over "at least several months", not a fixed 6 months; one practice key's explanation says ICD-11 "also uses 6 months", which is wrong. The 6-month rule is DSM-5-TR's. And in ICD-10 a phobia takes precedence over panic: panic disorder with agoraphobia is coded as agoraphobia (F40.01).

Who develops anxiety disorders?

Taken together, the anxiety disorders are among the commonest of all mental disorders, and three facts hold across the group. First, as DSM-5-TR puts it, many of them develop in childhood and tend to persist if not treated; the anxious child is very often the anxious adult who finally asks for help twenty years later. Second, most occur more often in girls and women than in boys and men, at a ratio of roughly two to one. Third, they rarely come alone: comorbidity, the presence of two or more disorders in the same person, is the rule in this chapter rather than the exception.

Key study: how often anxiety travels in company (Brown and colleagues, 2001)

Timothy A. Brown, Barlow and their colleagues asked a simple question: when a patient arrives at a specialist clinic with one anxiety or mood disorder, how often does he or she have another? They interviewed 1,127 patients at one of Barlow's centres with a semistructured diagnostic interview, using DSM-IV criteria. Of the patients whose principal diagnosis was an anxiety or depressive disorder, 55 percent had at least one additional anxiety or depressive disorder at the time of assessment; counting any time in their lives, the figure rose to 76 percent. By far the commonest additional diagnosis was major depression, which 50 percent had had at some point. Later work showed why this matters: an additional diagnosis of depression or of alcohol or drug abuse makes recovery from an anxiety disorder less likely and relapse more likely (Bruce and colleagues, 2005). The study's limit is its sample: people with several problems are more likely to seek specialist treatment, so a clinic may overstate how often disorders co-occur in the community. Even so, the conclusion has shaped treatment: the "pure" case of a single anxiety disorder is the exception, which is one reason Barlow's group later built a single unified protocol for the emotional disorders (Book 2, Section 5.6.7).

Anxiety also keeps company with physical illness. In an important population study, Jitender Sareen and colleagues (2006) found that having any anxiety disorder was uniquely and significantly associated with thyroid disease, respiratory disease, gastrointestinal disease, arthritis, migraine headaches and allergic conditions. The anxiety disorder usually began before the physical disorder, which suggests, without proving, that something about being anxious may contribute to the physical illness; and people with both had more disability and a poorer quality of life than people with the physical disorder alone. DSM-5-TR similarly notes raised rates of anxiety disorders among people with asthma, hypertension, ulcers and arthritis. The link runs in the other direction too, as the last subsection of this section shows: some illnesses produce anxiety directly.

Finally, anxiety is not a "minor" problem when it comes to suicide. DSM-5-TR states that people with anxiety may be more likely to have suicidal thoughts, to attempt suicide and to die by suicide than people without anxiety, and it names panic disorder, generalised anxiety disorder and specific phobia as the anxiety disorders most strongly associated with the step from suicidal thoughts to a suicide attempt. A clinician who asks about suicide only in depressed patients will miss some of the people most at risk.

Indian context

The National Mental Health Survey of India (2015 to 2016) diagnosed its 34,802 adults by ICD-10 criteria, so its anxiety figures are reported under the F40 to F48 heading. The neurotic and stress-related disorders were among the three commonest groups of mental disorder, with substance use and mood disorders, and about 3.5 percent of adults had one at the time of the survey; more than eight in ten of them were receiving no treatment (Book 2, Section 1.10.11). Notice that the survey's heading still carries the word "neurotic": in India you will meet the ICD-10 vocabulary in hospital records, research papers and examination questions for years to come.

Many of those untreated people are not hidden; they are sitting in the waiting rooms of general physicians. Shalini, a 38-year-old schoolteacher in Lucknow, has seen four doctors in a year. She describes attacks of thumping in the chest, a burning in the stomach and a heaviness in the head, and she sleeps badly. She has had two ECGs and an echocardiogram, all normal; she takes antacids for "gas" and a tonic for "weakness". Each doctor has told her that her heart is fine, and each reassurance has helped for about a week. Nobody has asked her what goes through her mind during the attacks, or what she has stopped doing because of them. When a clinical psychologist finally asks, the story changes. She lies awake worrying about her son's safety on his motorbike, her husband's job and her own health; she dreads the next attack; and she has stopped going to the market alone in case one strikes there. In Indian primary care anxiety very often arrives like this, described in the language of the body, and many patients expect, and receive, a tonic or an injection rather than a question about worry. The clinician's task is not to dismiss the bodily complaint but to complete the picture: first make sure that the physical causes described next have been considered, then ask about the worry, the attacks and the avoidance. (Shalini's story hints at more than one diagnosis; Sections 6.2 and 6.4 will show you how to sort them out.)

Anxiety caused by a substance or an illness, and the leftover categories

Before any anxiety disorder in this chapter can be diagnosed, the clinician must ask whether the anxiety is the direct physiological effect of something else: a drug, a medicine, a toxin or a medical illness. If it is, the diagnosis changes, and so does the treatment, because the cure for anxiety caused by an overactive thyroid gland is to treat the thyroid.

The diagnosis of substance/medication-induced anxiety disorder is made when panic attacks or anxiety dominate the clinical picture and the history, examination or laboratory findings show both that the symptoms developed during or soon after intoxication with, or withdrawal from, a substance, or after exposure to or withdrawal from a medication, and that the substance is capable of producing them. The symptoms must not be better explained by an independent anxiety disorder, and DSM-5-TR says what would suggest one: anxiety that began before the substance use, anxiety that persists for a substantial period (about 1 month) after acute withdrawal or severe intoxication has ended, or a history of anxiety episodes unrelated to substances. The disturbance must not occur only during a delirium, and it must cause distress or impairment. There is one more condition, and it is the one most often forgotten: this diagnosis is made instead of a diagnosis of intoxication or withdrawal only when the anxiety is predominant and severe enough to need clinical attention in its own right. Shakiness and dread on the second morning without alcohol are usually just part of alcohol withdrawal. The specifiers say when it began: with onset during intoxication, with onset during withdrawal, or with onset after medication use. The name of the disorder begins with the substance, so a clinician records, for example, "caffeine-induced anxiety disorder, with onset during intoxication" or "salbutamol-induced anxiety disorder, with onset after medication use". In the general population it seems rare (DSM-5-TR estimates a 12-month prevalence of about 0.002 percent in the United States), but it is far commoner in clinics. The substances themselves are taught in Chapter 10.

A diagnosis of anxiety disorder due to another medical condition (F06.4) is made when panic or anxiety is predominant and there is evidence from the history, examination or laboratory findings that it is the direct pathophysiological consequence of a medical illness; the illness is named in the diagnosis ("anxiety disorder due to phaeochromocytoma"). DSM-5-TR asks the clinician to weigh three things: a clear time relationship between the onset, worsening or remission of the illness and the anxiety; features that are atypical of an independent anxiety disorder, such as an unusual age at onset or course; and evidence in the literature that the illness can cause anxiety through a known physiological mechanism, as hyperthyroidism does. The anxiety must not be better explained by another mental disorder, above all by adjustment disorder with anxiety, in which the stressor is the illness itself and the person is distressed by its meaning or consequences (Chapter 7). DSM-5-TR gives a useful contrast: in anxiety caused by an illness there is often a prominent physical component, such as shortness of breath, whereas in adjustment disorder the anxiety concerns what the illness means. Nor is it illness anxiety disorder, in which the person worries about having an illness that is not causing the anxiety physiologically (Chapter 7). The course of the anxiety follows the course of the illness. Table 6.4 lists the physical causes DSM-5-TR names.

Table 6.4 Physical causes of anxiety named by DSM-5-TR, and the diagnosis each leads to.
SourceExamplesDiagnosis
Endocrine diseaseHyperthyroidism, phaeochromocytoma, hypoglycaemia, hyperadrenocortisolismAnxiety disorder due to another medical condition
Cardiovascular diseaseCongestive heart failure, pulmonary embolism, arrhythmia such as atrial fibrillationAnxiety disorder due to another medical condition
Respiratory illnessChronic obstructive pulmonary disease, asthma, pneumoniaAnxiety disorder due to another medical condition
Metabolic disturbanceVitamin B12 deficiency, porphyriaAnxiety disorder due to another medical condition
Neurological illnessNeoplasms, vestibular dysfunction, encephalitis, seizure disordersAnxiety disorder due to another medical condition
IntoxicationAlcohol, caffeine, cannabis, phencyclidine and other hallucinogens, inhalants, stimulants including cocaineSubstance/medication-induced anxiety disorder
WithdrawalAlcohol; opioids; sedatives, hypnotics and anxiolytics; stimulants including cocaineSubstance/medication-induced anxiety disorder
MedicationsBronchodilators and other sympathomimetics, corticosteroids, thyroid preparations, insulin, oral contraceptives, antihistamines, anticholinergics, antidepressants, antipsychotics, lithium, anticonvulsants, antihypertensivesSubstance/medication-induced anxiety disorder
ToxinsOrganophosphate insecticides, nerve gases, carbon monoxide, carbon dioxide, petrol and paint fumesSubstance/medication-induced anxiety disorder

Imran shows how the reasoning works. He is 46, a bank cashier in Hyderabad, and has always been the calm one in his family; no one in his family has had an anxiety disorder. Over two months he has become constantly nervous and irritable. His heart races, his hands shake so much that he fumbles the notes he counts, he sweats in air-conditioned rooms, he sleeps badly and, although he is eating more than usual, he has lost five kilograms. He is ashamed, convinced that he is "losing his nerve". Is this generalised anxiety disorder? Several things do not fit: the onset is sudden and late, there is no personal or family history, and the weight loss despite a good appetite and the intolerance of heat are not features of an anxiety disorder at all. His physician orders thyroid function tests, which show hyperthyroidism. If the anxiety is prominent enough to need attention in its own right, the diagnosis is anxiety disorder due to hyperthyroidism, and it settles as the thyroid is treated. Change one fact and the diagnosis changes with it. Had the symptoms begun in the week Imran started drinking six tumblers of strong filter coffee a day to get through the annual audit, the diagnosis would be caffeine-induced anxiety disorder; had they begun when he started an inhaler for asthma, it would be medication-induced.

Clinical link

Clues that anxiety may have a physical cause, from DSM-5-TR: onset at an atypical age (panic attacks beginning after about 45 should raise the question), atypical symptoms (during a "panic attack", vertigo, loss of consciousness, loss of bladder or bowel control, slurred speech or amnesia), no personal or family history of anxiety disorders, and a clear time link with an illness, a new medicine, or a change in the use of a substance. In practice, ask every new anxious patient about tea, coffee, colas and energy drinks, tobacco, alcohol and sedatives (and when the last dose was), cough and cold remedies, asthma inhalers, thyroid tablets and steroids, and check that a physician has examined them. A clinical psychologist does not order the thyroid test, but she should notice when one is needed and refer. The opposite error matters too: anxiety disorders can themselves raise the risk of heart disease, and when an anxious patient has a heart attack, the anxiety disorder is not reclassified as "due to" the heart disease.

Two leftover categories complete the chapter's list. The first, other specified anxiety disorder (F41.8), is used when symptoms characteristic of an anxiety disorder predominate and cause distress or impairment but do not meet the full criteria for any anxiety disorder (or for adjustment disorder with anxiety), and the clinician chooses to record why. DSM-5-TR's examples are limited-symptom attacks (panic-like attacks with fewer than four symptoms, Section 6.2); generalised anxiety occurring less often than "more days than not"; khyâl cap, the "wind attacks" described among Cambodians; and ataque de nervios, the "attack of nerves" described among Latin Americans (both cultural concepts are explained in Chapter 1). The second, unspecified anxiety disorder (F41.9), is used when the clinician chooses not to say why the criteria are not met, including when there is too little information for a more specific diagnosis, as in an emergency department.

Exam trap

Anxiety from caffeine, a decongestant, a bronchodilator or alcohol withdrawal is substance/medication-induced anxiety disorder, but only when the anxiety predominates and needs attention in its own right; otherwise the diagnosis is simply intoxication or withdrawal. Anxiety that is the direct physiological effect of an illness (hyperthyroidism, phaeochromocytoma, hypoglycaemia) is anxiety disorder due to another medical condition. Distress about the meaning of an illness (a new diagnosis of cancer) is adjustment disorder with anxiety; worry about having an illness that one does not have is illness anxiety disorder. Anxiety that persists for about a month after intoxication or withdrawal ends points to an independent anxiety disorder. And panic attacks caused by hyperthyroidism do not make panic disorder: Criterion C of panic disorder excludes them (Section 6.2).

6.2 Panic Attacks, Panic Disorder and AgoraphobiaMedium yield · 5 Qs

Section 6.1 drew a line between fear, the alarm that fires when danger is here now, and anxiety, the apprehension that looks towards a danger that might come. This section is about what happens when the alarm fires and there is no danger at all. The result, a panic attack, is one of the most frightening experiences a healthy body can produce, and the people who have one almost never think of it as a psychological event. They think they are dying. So they go to a hospital, and the first professional they meet is usually a cardiologist or an emergency physician, not a psychologist. We begin with one such person, take the panic attack apart symptom by symptom, and then follow the two disorders that can grow out of repeated attacks: panic disorder, which is a fear of the attacks themselves, and agoraphobia, which is a fear of being caught by them in a place with no way out.

The anatomy of a panic attack

Deepak was twenty-nine, a software engineer in Bengaluru, and by his own account a calm person. On a Sunday night, a week after his team had shipped a difficult release, he was lying on the sofa watching a film with his wife. Without warning his heart began to pound so hard that he could see his shirt move. Within a minute or two he could not get a full breath; his chest felt tight, his hands and lips tingled, and he was soaked in sweat although the fan was on. The room looked strangely flat and distant, "like a television screen". He was certain he was having a heart attack. "Call an ambulance," he told his wife, "I am going to die." By the time they reached the emergency department of a private hospital, perhaps twenty-five minutes later, the worst was over. His electrocardiogram, blood pressure and cardiac enzymes were normal. The doctor told him it was probably "gas" and stress and sent him home with an antacid. Deepak was relieved, but he was also puzzled and, underneath, frightened: if nothing was wrong with his heart, what had just happened to him?

What happened was a panic attack, which DSM-5-TR defines as an abrupt surge of intense fear or intense discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes, during which four or more of a list of thirteen symptoms occur. The word itself is old. The ancient Greeks blamed sudden, groundless terror on the god Pan, who was said to frighten travellers in lonely places with his blood-curdling cries, and "panic" has meant a sudden, overwhelming fear ever since. Each part of the modern definition does a particular job, so it is worth reading it slowly.

First, the attack is an abrupt surge. It is not a slow build of worry over an afternoon; it is a sudden jump, a crescendo. Second, it is a surge of fear or discomfort. Most people describe terror, but some, especially older adults, describe the experience as intense discomfort rather than fear, and the definition deliberately includes them. Third, and most important for examinations, the surge reaches its peak within minutes. DSM-5-TR is unusually precise about what this means: the time to peak intensity is "literally only a few minutes". Not a few seconds (a startle at a sudden noise peaks in a fraction of a second and is gone), not half an hour, and certainly not several hours. Notice what the rule measures. It fixes the time to peak, not the length of the whole attack. Deepak's attack peaked within two or three minutes, but he felt shaken for the best part of an hour. Textbooks often say that an attack typically subsides within about ten to thirty minutes, and that is a fair description of many attacks, but it is a description, not a criterion. A panic attack can fade into calm, or it can fade into a state of ongoing anxiety and then peak again.

Fourth, the surge can arise from either a calm state or an anxious state. Deepak was relaxed when his attack began, but a student already tense in an examination hall can also have one. DSM-5-TR therefore tells the clinician to time the attack from the moment of the abrupt increase in discomfort, not from the moment the person first began to feel uneasy. This is also what separates a panic attack from ongoing anxiety: the attack is discrete, it peaks within minutes, and it is usually far more intense.

Fifth, there must be four or more of the thirteen symptoms. They are easiest to remember in clusters, as Figure 6.4 shows. The heart and chest give palpitations, a pounding heart or a racing pulse, and chest pain or discomfort. Breathing gives sensations of shortness of breath or smothering, and feelings of choking. The general surge of the sympathetic nervous system (the emergency reaction described in Book 1, Section 11.8.2) gives sweating, trembling or shaking, and chills or heat sensations. The gut and the balance system give nausea or abdominal distress, and feeling dizzy, unsteady, light-headed or faint. The skin gives paraesthesias, which simply means numbness or tingling sensations, often in the fingers and around the mouth. Then come three experiences of the mind. Derealisation is a feeling that the world is unreal, as when Deepak's living room looked like a flat screen; depersonalisation is a feeling of being detached from oneself, as if watching oneself from outside or moving like a robot. (These experiences as symptoms are described in Book 2, Section 2.5.8, and depersonalisation/derealisation disorder, where they persist on their own, is taught in Chapter 7.) Last come the two fears: a fear of losing control or "going crazy", and a fear of dying.

DSM-5-TR counts eleven of the thirteen symptoms as physical and two as cognitive, and the two cognitive ones are the fear of losing control or going crazy and the fear of dying. (Derealisation and depersonalisation are clearly experiences of the mind rather than the body, but the manual counts them with the eleven.) The phrase "going crazy" is the patients' own colloquial expression, kept because it is what people actually say; it is not meant as a diagnosis or an insult. The fear of dying matters clinically, because people who report it tend to have more severe attacks and more severe panic disorder.

Two further rules complete the picture. The first concerns culture. People from different cultures notice and report different sensations during an attack, and DSM-5-TR lists some culture-specific symptoms that may be seen: tinnitus (ringing in the ears), neck soreness, headache, and uncontrollable screaming or crying. These may be part of the experience, but they do not count towards the four required symptoms. The second rule concerns attacks that do not reach four. An attack that meets every other part of the definition but has fewer than four symptoms is called a limited-symptom attack. Limited-symptom attacks are common in people with panic disorder, mixed in among their full attacks, and a history of such "fearful spells" in youth is a risk factor for later full attacks. But they do not, on their own, allow a diagnosis of panic disorder.

Is a panic attack really fear, or something else that merely resembles it? David H. Barlow (born 1942), whose Boston clinic has studied panic for decades, argues that it is fear: the same emotion, with the same urge to escape and the same bodily surge, simply firing at the wrong time. In his terms a panic attack is a false alarm. The most direct evidence comes from the rare occasions on which an attack has been caught on a laboratory recorder.

Key study: a panic attack on the recorder (Cohen, Barlow and Blanchard, 1985)

Barlow's group often recorded the physiology of patients with panic disorder while they sat quietly or practised relaxation. In one such session, reported by A. S. Cohen, Barlow and Edward B. Blanchard in 1985 in a paper on relaxation-associated panic attacks, a patient had an attack in the middle of the recording, unexpectedly from the patient's point of view and from the researchers'. The traces show a sudden, steep rise in heart rate from about the eleventh to the thirteenth minute, together with rises in muscle tension in the forehead (frontalis electromyogram) and in finger temperature. The whole surge rose and subsided within about three minutes. The conclusion: a panic attack is a genuine, measurable autonomic storm, not something the patient imagines, and it can strike while a person is relaxing. DSM-5-TR summarises the wider literature in the same terms: recordings of naturally occurring attacks show abrupt surges of arousal, usually of heart rate, that peak within minutes and subside within minutes, with faster, deeper breathing and a fall in the carbon dioxide level of the blood. The limitation is obvious: a handful of attacks caught by chance in a laboratory cannot tell us how typical they are.

Exam trap

A panic attack peaks within minutes, literally a few minutes. Distractors offer "a few seconds" (that is a startle), "30 to 60 minutes" and "several hours" (those describe ongoing anxiety or worry). The rule fixes the time to peak, not the total length, so "10 to 30 minutes" is a typical duration, not a criterion. You need four or more of thirteen symptoms; fewer is a limited-symptom attack. The two cognitive symptoms are fear of losing control or going crazy and fear of dying. Headache, tinnitus, neck soreness and screaming or crying do not count. Finally, the defining feature of a panic attack is the abrupt surge with bodily symptoms, not "persistent worry for 6 months" (generalised anxiety disorder), "avoidance of a specific feared object" (specific phobia) or "re-experiencing a traumatic event" (post-traumatic stress disorder).

Expected, unexpected and nocturnal attacks, and the panic attack specifier

Suppose a woman who is terrified of dogs has a panic attack when a street dog runs at her. Suppose a man has one on a Sunday evening on his sofa. Both are panic attacks by the definition above, but they mean very different things, and the difference is captured by two terms. An expected panic attack is one for which there is an obvious cue or trigger: the dog, the lift for a person afraid of lifts, the stage for a person afraid of public speaking, or a situation in which the person's attacks have usually happened before. An unexpected panic attack is one for which there is no obvious cue or trigger at the time it occurs; it seems to come "out of the blue", while the person is relaxing, shopping, driving on an empty road or emerging from sleep. Deepak's first attack, on the sofa, was unexpected.

Who decides which kind an attack was? The clinician does, and DSM-5-TR says how: by careful questioning about the sequence of events before the attack, combined with the person's own judgement of whether it seemed to happen for no reason. The decision is not always easy. People search hard for causes after an attack, and older adults in particular tend to explain an attack afterwards by a stressful situation ("it was the hospital visit"), even when it was unexpected at the time. Culture matters too. A Vietnamese person who panics after walking out into a strong wind may attribute the attack to the wind, because a cultural syndrome called trúng gió ("hit by the wind") links wind exposure with illness; in that person's eyes the attack was expected. The clinician has to find out what the person believes before classifying the attack. The distinction is not a technicality. Expected attacks are found in almost every anxiety disorder; recurrent unexpected attacks are the hallmark of panic disorder. In the United States and Europe about half of people with panic disorder have expected attacks as well as unexpected ones, so the presence of some expected attacks does not rule panic disorder out.

One kind of unexpected attack deserves a special name. A nocturnal panic attack is waking from sleep in a state of panic, which is different from waking up normally and then panicking. DSM-5-TR estimates that in the United States between a quarter and a third of people with panic disorder have had at least one; Barlow, citing earlier clinical studies, puts the figure higher, at about 60 percent, and notes that attacks occur more often between about 1.30 and 3.30 in the morning than at any other time. Some patients become afraid to fall asleep. Are they having nightmares? Sleep-laboratory studies say no. Nightmares and other dream activity occur in rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, which comes mostly later in the night. Nocturnal panic attacks arise in slow-wave (delta) sleep, the deepest stage of non-REM sleep, usually a few hours after the person has fallen asleep, and the patient typically begins to panic as he or she sinks into it. The best current explanation is that the physical sensations of "letting go" as one enters deep sleep are themselves frightening to a person who already fears bodily sensations (the idea of fear of fear, explained below). Because the attacks do not arise in REM sleep, there is no dream to be "repressed", whatever an older style of therapy might assume. Nocturnal panic must also be told from sleep terrors, which occur mostly in children, also in deep sleep: the child screams and may run about but does not fully wake and has no memory of the episode in the morning, whereas the person with nocturnal panic wakes fully and remembers the attack vividly. (Nightmares and sleep terrors are taught in Chapter 8.) Obstructive sleep apnoea, in which breathing stops repeatedly during sleep, can also feel like suffocation, but it has a repeated cycle of waking and falling back to sleep that nocturnal panic lacks.

Because panic attacks occur in so many conditions, DSM-5-TR does not treat a panic attack as a disorder at all. It states plainly that a panic attack is not a mental disorder and cannot be coded. Instead, when attacks occur in the course of some other disorder, the clinician adds the panic attack specifier: one writes "post-traumatic stress disorder with panic attacks", "major depressive disorder with panic attacks" or "social anxiety disorder with panic attacks". Attacks can occur with any anxiety disorder, with depressive and bipolar disorders, eating disorders, obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, personality disorders, psychotic disorders and substance use disorders, and with some medical conditions of the heart, lungs, balance system and gut. The specifier is useful because attacks are a marker of severity: in almost every disorder, people who have panic attacks have more severe symptoms, more comorbidity, a poorer response to treatment and a higher rate of suicidal thoughts and attempts. There is one exception to the specifier rule. In panic disorder the attacks are already part of the criteria, so the specifier is not added; "panic disorder with panic attacks" would say the same thing twice.

How common are panic attacks? Far more common than panic disorder. In the general population of the United States and Spain, between 9.5 and 11.2 percent of adults have at least one panic attack in a year, and the lifetime figure across nations is 13.2 percent. Attacks are more common in women, are rare before puberty, and have a mean age of onset in the United States of about 22 to 23 years. Compare those figures with the 2 to 3 percent of adults who have panic disorder in a given year (subsection 6.2.5), and the conclusion is clear: most people who have a panic attack never develop the disorder. What separates them? Barlow's answer is that those who do not go on to develop panic disorder put the attack down to the events of the moment (an argument, something they ate, a bad week) and get on with their lives. A real illustration is the American professional golfer Charlie Beljan. In late 2012, on his way to winning his first tournament on the professional tour, he had a panic attack on the course that he took to be a heart attack. With paramedics following him in a golf cart, he staggered from shot to shot, sometimes sitting down on the fairway, finished with his best round of the year and only then went to hospital in an ambulance, where the attack was identified. The attack was terrifying; what it did not do, at least in the account Barlow gives, was turn into a lasting fear of the next attack. That fear is what the next subsection is about. Table 6.5 first sets the panic attack beside the experiences that examiners place next to it.

Table 6.5 The panic attack and the experiences it is confused with.
ExperienceWhat defines itTime ruleStatus
Panic attack (full-symptom)Abrupt surge of fear or discomfort with 4 or more of 13 symptomsPeaks within minutesNot a disorder; a specifier, or part of panic disorder
Limited-symptom attackThe same surge with fewer than 4 symptomsPeaks within minutesNot enough for panic disorder
Ongoing anxietyFuture-oriented tension, vigilance and cautious avoidanceBuilds and lingers; no abrupt peakNormal, or part of an anxiety disorder (Section 6.1)
Panic disorderRecurrent unexpected attacks, then worry about them or a change in behaviour1 month or more after an attackAnxiety disorder (this section)
Generalised anxiety disorderPersistent, excessive worry about many thingsMore days than not for 6 monthsAnxiety disorder (Section 6.4)
Specific phobiaFear and avoidance of one object or situation; any attacks are expectedTypically 6 monthsAnxiety disorder (Section 6.3)
Post-traumatic stress disorderRe-experiencing a trauma; attacks cued by remindersMore than 1 monthTrauma- and stressor-related disorder (Chapter 7)
Exam trap

A panic attack is not itself a mental disorder and has no code; in any other disorder it is recorded as a specifier ("PTSD with panic attacks"), but never added to panic disorder. Unexpected means no obvious cue at the time, as judged by the clinician; expected attacks with a clear trigger belong to the phobias and other anxiety disorders. Nocturnal panic arises in slow-wave (non-REM) sleep, so it is not a nightmare, which is a REM-sleep event, and unlike a sleep terror the person wakes fully and remembers it.

Panic disorder: how it was born and how it is diagnosed

Attacks of sudden terror with a pounding heart and breathlessness were described long before anyone called them panic disorder. Freud's "anxiety neurosis" of 1895 (Section 6.1) already included anxiety attacks with palpitations, disturbed breathing, sweating and trembling, alongside a chronic state of anxious expectation. For most of the twentieth century both kinds of anxiety, the sudden attacks and the steady apprehension, were treated as degrees of one thing. What pulled them apart was, of all things, a drug.

Key study: Klein's pharmacological dissection (1959 to 1964)

In 1959 the young American psychiatrist Donald F. Klein (1928 to 2019) joined Hillside Hospital in Queens, New York, where he and Max Fink were studying the effects of imipramine, the first of the tricyclic antidepressants. Klein noticed something unexpected in a group of anxious inpatients who were not depressed, patients who would later be called agoraphobic. Imipramine stopped their apparently spontaneous attacks of terror. What it did not quickly remove was the chronic anticipatory anxiety between the attacks, the dread of the next one, or the avoidance that had grown around them; those needed psychological help and encouragement. Klein and Fink published their reaction patterns to imipramine in 1962, and in 1964 Klein described "two drug-responsive anxiety syndromes". His reasoning was bold: if a drug blocks one kind of anxiety but not the other, the two may be different conditions. He called this method pharmacological dissection (sometimes psychopharmacological dissection): using the selective effect of a drug to carve a clinical picture at its joints. The criticism is that a drug's effect is weak evidence about the nature of a disorder (aspirin relieves many different kinds of headache), and later research found that antidepressants such as imipramine also help generalised anxiety, so drug response does not divide the two as cleanly as Klein hoped. But the argument was persuasive at the time, and when DSM-III appeared in 1980 it split the old anxiety neurosis into two disorders: panic disorder, defined by the attacks, and generalised anxiety disorder, defined by chronic apprehension (Section 6.4). Klein later proposed a biological theory of why the alarm misfires, the suffocation false alarm theory, which is taught in Section 6.7.

Panic disorder, as DSM-5-TR now defines it, is recurrent unexpected panic attacks followed by a lasting fear of further attacks or a lasting change in behaviour because of them. The criteria fall into four groups, and each group exists to rule something out.

  • Criterion A: recurrent unexpected panic attacks. "Recurrent" means more than one. "Unexpected" means at least some of the attacks came with no obvious trigger. Both words matter. A single attack, however terrifying, is not panic disorder; so many people have one isolated attack in their lives that a diagnosis built on one would make a disorder of an ordinary experience. And attacks that only ever come with a trigger point to some other anxiety disorder. DSM-5-TR adds that more than one full-symptom unexpected attack is needed; a person who has only ever had limited-symptom attacks does not qualify.
  • Criterion B: the aftermath. At least one attack must have been followed by a month or more of one or both of two things. The first is persistent concern or worry about having more attacks or about their consequences: that the next one will be a heart attack, that it will make the person lose control or "go crazy", that it will happen in front of colleagues. The second is a significant maladaptive change in behaviour related to the attacks, such as avoiding exercise or unfamiliar situations so as not to bring on an attack. This criterion is the heart of the disorder. Panic disorder is not really a disorder of panic attacks; it is a disorder of what a person comes to feel and do about them.
  • Criterion C: not caused by a substance or a medical condition. Stimulants, caffeine and cannabis, withdrawal from alcohol and other depressants, an overactive thyroid gland and disorders of the heart and lungs can all produce attacks, and in such cases the diagnosis is a substance/medication-induced anxiety disorder or an anxiety disorder due to another medical condition (subsection 6.2.5).
  • Criterion D: not better explained by another mental disorder. If the attacks occur only in feared social situations, the diagnosis is social anxiety disorder; only on meeting a circumscribed phobic object or situation, specific phobia; only in response to obsessions, obsessive-compulsive disorder; only in response to reminders of a trauma, post-traumatic stress disorder; only on separation from attachment figures, separation anxiety disorder. In all these cases the attacks are expected, and the other disorder explains them. If, however, the person also has unexpected attacks and worries about them, panic disorder can be diagnosed as well.

Push Deepak through the criteria. Over the next three months he had five more attacks: two at his desk, one on the Metro, one in a traffic jam and one, worst of all, that woke him at two in the morning. At least four were full attacks, and none had an obvious trigger (Criterion A). Since the first attack he had thought about his heart every day; he had seen three cardiologists, had a treadmill test and wore a fitness band to watch his pulse, and he had stopped going to the gym and stopped drinking coffee in case either set off an attack (Criterion B, both halves). His thyroid tests were normal, he used no drugs and drank little (Criterion C). His attacks were not tied to social situations, to any one phobic object, to obsessions or to a trauma (Criterion D). Deepak has panic disorder. Notice that he still travels on the Metro and goes to the office, although with dread; he does not yet avoid two or more of the situations that define agoraphobia. That will matter in subsection 6.2.6.

Exam trap

"Panic disorder is where ..." The answer is recurrent and unexpected panic attacks (followed by a month of worry or behaviour change). The distractors each describe something else: "an individual experiences a singular panic attack" (one attack is common and is not a disorder); "excessive anxiety and worry about many different aspects of life" (generalised anxiety disorder, Section 6.4); "a fear of panicking in enclosed spaces" (a fear tied to a place: claustrophobia, a situational specific phobia, if it is one kind of place, or agoraphobia if two or more of the five agoraphobic situations are feared). Also remember the duration: the worry after an attack must last 1 month, not 6 months; panic disorder is the one adult anxiety disorder in this section without a "typically 6 months" rule.

Fear of fear: anticipatory anxiety, interoceptive avoidance and safety signals

Why does one person shrug off a panic attack while another, like Deepak, spends months watching his own pulse? In 1978 two American behaviour therapists, Alan J. Goldstein and Dianne L.
