Chambless, published "A reanalysis of agoraphobia" in the journal Behavior Therapy. Their central idea was that after a few attacks the person comes to fear not a place, an animal or a situation, but the bodily sensations of anxiety themselves. A slight quickening of the heart, a moment of breathlessness on the stairs, a wave of dizziness on standing up: each becomes a signal that the next attack may be starting, and the signal itself triggers fear. They called this fear of fear. Because the cue for the fear is inside the body, it can never be left at home, and because fear produces the very sensations that are feared, the process can feed on itself. The detailed cognitive account of how this cycle escalates into an attack, David Clark's catastrophic misinterpretation model, is taught in Section 6.8 and, as a treatment, in Book 2, Section 5.6.3; the learning account of how internal sensations come to act as conditioned cues is also Section 6.8's.

Fear of fear shows itself in three ways. The first is anticipatory anxiety: the steady, nagging apprehension between attacks about when the next one will come, where it will strike and what it will do. This is Criterion B's "persistent concern or worry", and it is often more disabling than the attacks, which may come once a month while the anticipatory anxiety is there every day. The second is interoceptive avoidance, avoidance of activities that produce internal sensations resembling the beginning of an attack. Barlow's clinic lists the typical ones: running up flights of stairs, getting involved in heated arguments, getting angry, hot and stuffy rooms, cars and shops, saunas and showers with the doors and windows closed, walking in intense heat or very cold weather, sport, aerobics, dancing and hiking, lifting heavy objects, drinking coffee or other caffeinated drinks, eating chocolate or heavy meals, sexual relations, watching horror films or exciting matches, and standing up quickly from a sitting position. Deepak's abandoned gym and his abandoned filter coffee belong here. Psychopathologists now regard this cluster as every bit as important as the more familiar avoidance of places.

The third is the use of safety behaviours, small acts meant to prevent a feared catastrophe or to make survival more likely if it comes: sitting near the exit in a cinema or on a bus, holding on to a railing, carrying a water bottle, checking the pulse, sitting down at the first sign of dizziness, keeping a mobile phone charged and a hospital's number ready. Closely related are safety signals, people or objects whose mere presence makes the person feel safe: a trusted companion who "knows what to do", a strip of tablets in the pocket that is never actually taken, the knowledge that a hospital is close by. Barlow notes that anxiety falls when a person is with a "safe" person or in a "safe" place even when that person or place could do nothing useful if disaster struck. The trouble with safety behaviours and signals is that they work too well in the short run. When the feared heart attack does not happen, the person credits the water bottle or the companion rather than learning that the attack was never dangerous, so the fear is preserved (the general idea of safety behaviours is set out in Book 2, Section 5.6.2). Figure 6.5 shows how the three strands can lead from one unexpected attack to agoraphobia.

Exam trap

"Fear of fear" (Goldstein and Chambless, 1978) belongs to panic disorder and agoraphobia. In a question that asks what the sufferer of generalised anxiety disorder experiences, "fear of fear" is a distractor; the answer is continued apprehension about unspecified things (Section 6.4). "Continual flashbacks to past events" is post-traumatic stress disorder (Chapter 7), and "a desire to check that the environment is safe" describes checking compulsions in obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5). Do not confuse fear of fear, which is fear of the sensations of anxiety, with anticipatory anxiety, which is worry about future attacks: the first drives the second.

Who develops panic disorder, how it runs, and what else it might be

In any one year about 2 to 3 percent of adults and adolescents in the United States and several European countries have panic disorder. The global lifetime prevalence is estimated at 1.7 percent, and estimates from Asian, African and Latin American countries are lower still, between 0.1 and 0.8 percent. Whether those low figures reflect a real difference or a difference in how people describe and report their distress is not settled; as you will see below, a person who experiences an attack as "heart trouble" may never be counted as a case of panic disorder at all. Women are affected about twice as often as men, and the difference is already visible before the age of 14. Panic disorder itself is rare before 14 (below 0.4 percent), rises through adolescence, perhaps after puberty, and peaks in adulthood; it becomes less common again after middle age, falling to about 1.2 percent of adults over 55 and 0.7 percent over 64, possibly because the autonomic nervous system responds less sharply with age. The median age of onset in the United States is 20 to 24 years, and across nations about 32 years. A small number of cases begin in childhood, often with "fearful spells" that are dated back to childhood only in retrospect, and onset after 55 is unusual.

Left untreated, panic disorder usually runs a chronic but waxing and waning course. Some people have bursts of frequent attacks separated by months or years of remission; others have continuous severe symptoms; only a minority recover fully without relapse within a few years. It is rarely alone. In the general population about 80 percent of people with panic disorder have had another mental disorder at some point in their lives, most often another anxiety disorder (agoraphobia above all), major depressive disorder, bipolar disorder or an alcohol use disorder. Depression comes first in about a third of those who have both; in the other two-thirds it begins with or after the panic. Some people drink or take sedatives to control their attacks, which is one route into a substance use disorder (Chapter 10). Panic disorder carries a raised rate of suicidal thoughts and behaviour, even when depression and other risk factors are allowed for; about a quarter of primary-care patients with panic disorder report suicidal thoughts, so the question must always be asked. It also produces the highest number of medical visits of any anxiety disorder, with repeated trips to emergency departments, absence from work and, for some, loss of a job or a course of study. The risk factors (temperament, anxiety sensitivity, parenting, stress, smoking and genes) are taught in Section 6.7, and the psychological theories in Section 6.8.

Culture shapes both what people fear during an attack and how they explain it. Among Latin Americans, especially from the Caribbean, ataque de nervios ("attack of nerves") resembles a panic attack, but more often includes uncontrollable shouting or crying, trembling, aggressive or suicidal behaviour and depersonalisation, and it may last longer than the few minutes of a typical panic attack. Among Cambodians, khyâl cap or "wind attacks" combine dizziness, tinnitus and neck soreness. The psychiatrist and anthropologist Devon Hinton and his colleagues showed how such beliefs feed panic among Khmer refugees in the United States: the traditional idea of kyol goeu, "wind overload", in which too much wind or gas in the body may cause blood vessels to burst, becomes the focus of catastrophic thinking during an attack, so that a sore neck or a dizzy spell on standing is read as the start of a fatal rupture. These cultural syndromes are described in Chapter 1.

Indian context

In India, as in much of Asia, a panic attack is usually experienced and reported as a bodily illness. The person, often in his or her twenties or thirties, arrives at a cardiologist's clinic or an emergency department complaining of a pounding heart, chest pain or breathlessness, or at a physician's with "gas", "acidity" or ghabrahat (a common Hindi word for agitated nervousness). Tests are normal, the patient is reassured or given an antacid or a vitamin injection, and the attacks return. Many patients have seen several doctors and had several electrocardiograms and treadmill tests before anyone mentions anxiety. This is a clinical observation rather than a measured figure, but every Indian clinician recognises it, and it is one reason why panic disorder should be asked about in any young adult with repeated normal cardiac work-ups.

Because the symptoms of a panic attack are bodily, the first step in diagnosis is always to rule out a bodily cause (Criterion C). DSM-5-TR lists the medical conditions that can cause attacks or be mistaken for them: an overactive thyroid gland (hyperthyroidism) or parathyroid gland (hyperparathyroidism); phaeochromocytoma, a rare tumour of the adrenal gland that releases bursts of adrenaline; disorders of the vestibular (balance) system; seizure disorders; and heart and lung conditions such as arrhythmias, supraventricular tachycardia, asthma and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease. Simple tests help: thyroid hormones, serum calcium for the parathyroid, a Holter monitor (a portable 24-hour electrocardiogram) for arrhythmias. Substances are the second check. Intoxication with stimulants (cocaine, amphetamines and plain caffeine) or with cannabis, and withdrawal from alcohol, barbiturates and other depressants, can all set off attacks. Two warning signs suggest a medical or substance cause rather than panic disorder: onset after the age of 45, and atypical symptoms during an attack, such as true vertigo (the room spinning), loss of consciousness, loss of bladder or bowel control, slurred speech or amnesia. A clinician who sees any of them should look harder for another cause. A substance cause does not settle the matter for good, however. If attacks continue long after intoxication or withdrawal has ended, or if they began before the drinking did, panic disorder may be present as well, and many people with panic disorder use alcohol to quieten their attacks.

The third check is other mental disorders. In the other anxiety disorders the attacks are expected: cued by social situations in social anxiety disorder, by phobic objects in specific phobia, by worry in generalised anxiety disorder, by separation in separation anxiety disorder. People with illness anxiety disorder (Chapter 7) also worry about their health, but they fear having a disease, and their anxiety is a steady preoccupation rather than a series of attacks; the person with panic disorder fears the attacks and what the attacks might do. Generalised anxiety disorder (Section 6.4) shares the chronic apprehension, but its worry is spread across many everyday matters rather than fixed on the next attack. Finally, panic attacks happen in depression, in psychotic disorders and in personality disorders, and there the specifier, not a second diagnosis, is usually the right label.

Clinical link

Explaining the diagnosis well is the first treatment. A patient told "it is nothing, only stress" leaves feeling dismissed and, often, still convinced that something has been missed. A patient told "this is a panic attack: your body's emergency alarm going off with no fire. It feels like dying, but it cannot stop your heart, and it can be treated" has been given a reason to stop searching for a heart disease. The psychological treatment with the strongest evidence for panic disorder is cognitive behaviour therapy with interoceptive exposure, in which the patient deliberately brings on the feared sensations (spinning in a chair, breathing through a straw, running on the spot) until they lose their power (Book 2, Sections 5.6.3 and 4.3; breathing retraining is Section 4.8.3). The drugs, including the antidepressants and the benzodiazepines, and the reasons for caution with the latter, are compared in Section 6.9.

Agoraphobia: the fear of being trapped

In 1871 the Berlin psychiatrist Carl Friedrich Otto Westphal (1833 to 1890), often written Karl Westphal, described three men who were seized by extreme anxiety and dread whenever they had to cross certain open squares and public places of the city. He named their condition agoraphobia, from the Greek agora, the marketplace and place of assembly at the centre of a Greek city, and phobos, fear. Barlow points out that the name fits better than it seems: the agora was a busy, bustling place, and the most stressful places for people with agoraphobia today are its modern equivalents. In India these are the shopping mall, the railway station, the crowded bazaar during a festival, the queue at a bank, the packed suburban train and the bus with its door jammed with commuters.

Nandini, thirty-four, lives in Kochi with her husband and two school-age children. Her first panic attack came four years ago on a crowded private bus stuck in traffic; she remembers thinking that if she fainted no one could reach her and the bus could not stop. More attacks followed, in a textile shop during the Onam sales and in the queue at the ration shop. Little by little she gave up the bus, then the market, then the bank. She can still go to the temple on Fridays, but only with her husband, and she stands at the back near the gate. If he is travelling, she does not go out at all; the children buy the vegetables on their way home from school, and the grocer delivers. "It is not the place," she says. "It is that if it happens there, I cannot get out, and nobody will help me. At home, if it comes, I can lie down."

Nandini's words contain the essence of the disorder. Agoraphobia is not, as its name suggests, a fear of open spaces. It is a fear of being in situations from which escape might be difficult, or in which help might not be available, if panic-like symptoms or other incapacitating or embarrassing symptoms were to occur. The organising theme is being trapped or helpless. DSM-5-TR's criteria spell this out in groups.

  • Criterion A: the situations. Marked fear or anxiety about two or more of five situations: (1) using public transport (cars, buses, trains, ships, aeroplanes); (2) being in open spaces (car parks, marketplaces, bridges); (3) being in enclosed places (shops, theatres, cinemas); (4) standing in a queue or being in a crowd; and (5) being outside the home alone. The examples are not exhaustive. The requirement of two situations is the main way to tell agoraphobia from a situational specific phobia, which is limited to one.
  • Criterion B: the reason. The person fears or avoids the situations because of thoughts that escape might be difficult or help unavailable in the event of panic-like symptoms (any of the thirteen, such as dizziness, faintness or fear of dying) or other incapacitating or embarrassing symptoms. These include vomiting and bowel symptoms, a fear of falling in older adults, a fear of incontinence and, in children, a sense of being disoriented and lost. The reason is what separates agoraphobia from a phobia of the situation itself. A person afraid of flying because the plane might crash has a specific phobia; a person afraid of flying because she might panic and could not get off has agoraphobic fear.
  • Criteria C and D: consistency and response. The situations almost always provoke fear or anxiety (someone anxious in a queue only once in five visits does not qualify), and they are actively avoided, require the presence of a companion, or are endured with intense fear. Avoidance can be behavioural (changing routes, taking a job close to home, having food delivered) or cognitive (getting through the situation by distraction), and the person may use safety behaviours such as sitting near exits.
  • Criterion E: out of proportion. The fear must be out of proportion to the actual danger and to the sociocultural context. Not wanting to go out in a cyclone, or avoiding a deserted car park in a high-crime area at night, is not agoraphobia. Culture matters here in an important way. DSM-5-TR gives the example of societies in which it is customary for women not to leave the house alone; where that is the norm, staying at home is not a symptom. An Indian clinician must therefore ask whether a woman who never goes out alone is afraid, or is simply following what her family expects.
  • Criteria F and G: persistence and impairment. The fear, anxiety or avoidance typically lasts 6 months or more, which excludes short-lived problems, and it causes clinically significant distress or impairment.
  • Criteria H and I: other explanations. If a medical condition is present (inflammatory bowel disease, Parkinson's disease), the fear must be clearly excessive for it. And the picture must not be better explained by another mental disorder: by a situational specific phobia (one situation only), social anxiety disorder (social situations only, feared because of judgement), obsessive-compulsive disorder (avoidance driven by obsessions, such as fear of contamination), body dysmorphic disorder (avoidance because of perceived flaws in appearance), post-traumatic stress disorder (avoidance of trauma reminders only) or separation anxiety disorder (fear of separation from attachment figures).

Nandini fears and avoids public transport, enclosed places, queues and crowds, and going out alone: four of the five situations. She fears them because she could not escape or get help if she panicked. She goes only with a companion, her fear is far beyond any real danger, it has lasted four years and it has shrunk her life. She has agoraphobia. She also has recurrent unexpected attacks and a lasting fear of them, so she has panic disorder too, and under DSM-5-TR she receives both diagnoses.

At its most severe, agoraphobia makes a person completely housebound, dependent on others even for food and medicine. Barlow describes Mrs M., a 67-year-old woman who had suffered from panic disorder and agoraphobia for more than 30 years and had not left her second-floor flat for 20. Even parts of her flat had become unsafe: for 15 years she had not answered her own door because she was afraid to look into the hallway, and for 10 she had not entered the half of her kitchen that overlooked the back yard. Her daughter brought groceries once a week. When the evaluating psychologist arrived, he was the first person she had seen in three weeks; she offered him coffee and homemade biscuits, and she declined treatment. A happier example is the American television cook Paula Deen, whose interest in cooking, by Barlow's account, developed during the years when severe agoraphobia kept her at home. Demoralisation, depression and the misuse of alcohol and sedatives as self-medication are common in the housebound.

Agoraphobia looks slightly different across the lifespan. In children the most feared situation is being outside the home alone, and the fear is of getting lost. In adults the fear is mostly of panic-like symptoms. In older adults the most feared situations are shops, queues and open spaces, and the commonest fear is of falling; about 10 percent of older adults with agoraphobia say their first episode came after 65. With an older person the clinician has to judge carefully whether the fear of falling is out of proportion to real frailty.

Agoraphobia as a disorder in its own right: history, numbers and look-alikes

For most of its history agoraphobia has been tied to panic, and the manuals have argued about which of the two comes first. Barlow's view, built on years of clinic work, is that most agoraphobic avoidance is simply a complication of severe unexpected panic attacks: if you have had attacks and fear another, you want to be in a safe place, or with a safe person, from which you can quickly get to a hospital or a bed. On that view agoraphobia is one way of coping with panic. Yet the avoidance, once learned, can become independent of the attacks. Mrs M. had not had an attack in years and was still housebound, because agoraphobic avoidance depends mostly on how strongly a person expects another attack, not on how many attacks he or she actually has. And epidemiological surveys, which find people in the community rather than in clinics, turned up a large group who have agoraphobia without ever having had a panic attack at all; Barlow notes that about half of the people with agoraphobia found in population surveys fit this description, though they are rarely seen in clinics. Some of them fear other unpredictable and embarrassing events: dizzy spells, loss of bladder or bowel control, falling. Those findings explain the change shown in Table 6.6. DSM-IV had a single combined disorder, panic disorder with agoraphobia, plus a residual category for agoraphobia without a history of panic disorder. DSM-5 made agoraphobia and panic disorder two independent diagnoses: agoraphobia is diagnosed irrespective of panic disorder, and a person who meets the criteria for both, like Nandini, receives both.

Table 6.6 How the manuals have joined and separated panic and agoraphobia.
ManualDiagnoses availableWhat it assumed
DSM-III (1980)Panic disorder; agoraphobia with panic attacks; agoraphobia without panic attacksAgoraphobia was the main disorder, with or without attacks as a feature
DSM-III-R (1987) and DSM-IV (1994)Panic disorder without agoraphobia; panic disorder with agoraphobia; agoraphobia without history of panic disorderAgoraphobia was usually a complication of panic disorder
ICD-10 (1992)Agoraphobia with or without panic disorder (F40.0); panic disorder (F41.0) only when no phobia explains the attacksAgoraphobia given priority over panic
DSM-5 (2013) and DSM-5-TR (2022)Panic disorder; agoraphobia: two independent diagnoses, both given when both are metEach can occur without the other
ICD-11 (in force from 2022)Panic disorder and agoraphobia as separate diagnosesAs in DSM-5

Every year about 1 to 1.7 percent of adolescents and adults worldwide meet the criteria for agoraphobia. Women are affected about twice as often as men; Barlow's clinic found that the more severe the agoraphobic avoidance, the higher the proportion of women (in his patients with panic disorder, 72 percent of those with mild agoraphobia were women, 81 percent with moderate and 89 percent with severe). He thinks the explanation is mainly cultural: it is more acceptable for women to admit fear and to avoid situations, while men are expected to "tough it out", and a large proportion of men with severe unexpected attacks cope instead by drinking heavily, which can hide the anxiety disorder behind an alcohol problem. Agoraphobia can occur in childhood, but its incidence peaks in late adolescence and early adulthood. Two-thirds of cases begin before 35, the mean age of onset is 21, and agoraphobia without preceding panic attacks or panic disorder begins later, at 25 to 29; there is a second rise in risk after 40. Panic attacks or panic disorder come before the agoraphobia in 30 percent of community cases and in more than 50 percent of clinical cases. The course is usually persistent and chronic: without treatment complete remission is rare (about 10 percent), and about a third of those who do remit relapse. About 90 percent of people with agoraphobia have another mental disorder, most often another anxiety disorder, depression, post-traumatic stress disorder or an alcohol use disorder, and about 15 percent report suicidal thoughts or behaviour. The temperament and family background of agoraphobia, and its strong genetic loading, are discussed in Section 6.7.

Agoraphobia is examined mostly through its look-alikes, so Table 6.7 lines them up. Notice that each look-alike fails one of the two tests that define agoraphobia: the number of situations (two or more of five) or the reason for the fear (being trapped without help if panic-like symptoms strike).

Table 6.7 Agoraphobia and its look-alikes.
What the person experiencesDiagnosisWhy it is not agoraphobia
Hears voices telling him what to doA psychotic symptom (Chapter 4)A hallucination, not a fear of places
Feels very nervous in a lift, and nowhere elseClaustrophobia: specific phobia, situational typeOnly one situation
Is very fearful of tall trees or tall buildingsAcrophobia (heights): specific phobia, natural environment typeFears the height itself, not being trapped
Fears being watched, judged and embarrassedSocial anxiety disorder (Section 6.3)Fears scrutiny; calm when alone
Fears spidersSpecific phobia, animal typeA single object
Avoids public places for fear of germsObsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5)Driven by contamination obsessions
Will not go out without a parentSeparation anxiety disorder (Section 6.4)Fears separation, not panic symptoms
Avoids places that recall an assaultPost-traumatic stress disorder (Chapter 7)Trauma reminders only
Stays at home, without energy or interestMajor depressive disorder (Chapter 5)No fear of panic-like symptoms
Fears public places where escape or help might be hard if panic strikesAgoraphobiaTwo or more of five situations, feared as traps
Exam trap

A person diagnosed with agoraphobia has an irrational fear of public places where escape might be difficult or help unavailable. "Hearing voices telling him or her to do things" is psychosis; "feeling very nervous in an elevator" on its own is claustrophobia, a situational specific phobia; "feeling very fearful of tall trees or buildings" is acrophobia, a natural environment phobia. In DSM-5, agoraphobia is marked fear of two or more of five situations (public transport, open spaces, enclosed places, queues or crowds, being outside the home alone), not "social scrutiny and embarrassment" (social anxiety disorder), "a single specific object such as spiders" (specific phobia) or "contamination by germs" (obsessive-compulsive disorder). It needs typically 6 months, and since DSM-5 it is diagnosed independently of panic disorder; "panic disorder with agoraphobia" is DSM-IV language. The theme is being trapped or helpless, not open space as such.

6.3 Specific Phobia, Social Anxiety Disorder and the Names of the PhobiasMedium yield · 3 Qs

John Madden (1936 to 2021) was one of the most famous figures in American sport, a championship-winning football coach who became a much-loved television commentator. His job meant covering a game in New York one Sunday and in San Francisco the next, a distance any other commentator would fly. Madden could not fly. He suffered from claustrophobia, and for years he crossed the country by train; later he travelled in a specially equipped private bus. As Barlow observes, he must have known that flying is the safest way to travel and that his fear was costing him time and money, and he even made fun of it in television advertisements. None of that knowledge made the fear go away. That combination, a fear that the sufferer may know is excessive but cannot argue away, which is reliably set off by one kind of thing and which reshapes a life around avoiding it, is the subject of this section. In panic disorder and agoraphobia (Section 6.2) the danger is felt to be inside the body. In the phobias it is felt to be outside: in a dog, a needle, a lift, or the eyes of other people.

What makes a fear a phobia

A phobia is a persistent, irrational fear of a specific object or situation: a fear that is out of proportion to the real danger, that is set off almost every time the person meets the object or situation, and that leads the person to avoid it or to endure it with dread. Each part of that sentence separates a phobia from something else. "Persistent" separates it from the passing fears that everyone has. "Out of proportion" separates it from a sensible fear (a fear of cobras in a field in Konkan in the monsoon is prudence, not phobia). "Almost every time" separates it from a fear that comes and goes. "Avoided or endured with dread" captures what makes it a problem. And "a specific object or situation" separates it from the free-floating worry of generalised anxiety disorder (Section 6.4), which attaches to almost anything.

Examiners test the definition by offering things that are not phobias. A phobia is not a mild stress reaction: the ordinary upset that follows a bad event, or the more troublesome distress of an adjustment disorder (Chapter 7), is a response to a stressor and fades as the person adapts, whereas a phobia is a lasting fear of a particular stimulus. It is not a personality disorder, which is a pervasive, inflexible pattern of inner experience and behaviour that colours almost every area of life (Chapter 11); a phobia is circumscribed, and outside its territory the person may be quite untroubled. And it is not a temporary hallucination, which is a perception without a stimulus (Chapter 2); the phobic person perceives the spider or the lift perfectly accurately and is afraid of the real thing. A phobia is an anxiety disorder. (Phobia as a sign in the mental status examination is described in Book 2, Section 2.5.5.)

Fears themselves are almost universal. In a classic community survey published in 1969, W. Stewart Agras and his colleagues asked people about intense fears and found that snakes headed the list, reported by about a quarter of the sample, followed by heights and flying; most such fears were far more common in women. Barlow's summary of later surveys is that few of the people who report intense fears qualify for a phobia, but that for roughly one person in eight a fear becomes severe enough at some point in life to earn the label. Children's fears are a special case, because many are a normal part of development (Section 6.1): infants fear loud noises and strangers, toddlers fear separation, and fears of animals and of the dark appear early and may last into the fourth or fifth year, with fears of monsters and imaginary creatures beginning around three. These fears are usually transient and only mildly impairing, and DSM-5-TR says plainly that such developmentally appropriate fears are not specific phobia.

The DSM-5-TR criteria for specific phobia turn the definition into rules. Read them in groups.

  • Criterion A: marked fear of a specific object or situation. The manual's examples are flying, heights, animals, receiving an injection and seeing blood. "Marked" means intense or severe. The feared thing is called the phobic stimulus. The fear may grow as the stimulus comes closer, may be felt in anticipation, and may take the form of a full or limited-symptom panic attack, which in this setting is an expected attack. In children the fear may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing or clinging rather than in words.
  • Criterion B: almost always and at once. The stimulus almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Someone who is anxious on only one flight in five does not have a flying phobia, and the fear comes as soon as the stimulus is met, not after a delay. The intensity may still vary with circumstances (turbulence, the presence of others, how long the exposure lasts).
  • Criterion C: avoidance or dread. The stimulus is actively avoided, or endured with intense fear. Avoidance can be obvious (a person who fears blood refusing to see a doctor) or subtle (a person who fears snakes refusing to look at pictures of anything snake-shaped), and people with long-standing phobias often arrange their lives so thoroughly around the fear (turning down a job that needs travel, moving to a place without the feared animal) that they no longer feel anxious day to day. Their avoidance itself then confirms the diagnosis.
  • Criterion D: out of proportion. The fear is out of proportion to the actual danger and to the sociocultural context. People with specific phobia often recognise that their reactions are excessive, but they also tend to overestimate the danger, so the judgement is made by the clinician, not the patient. Context counts: a fear of the dark may be reasonable where there is ongoing violence.
  • Criterion E: persistence. The fear, anxiety or avoidance is persistent, typically lasting 6 months or more. The rule separates a phobia from the transient fears that are so common, especially in children. "Typically" signals that the six months is a guide applied with clinical judgement, not a stopwatch.
  • Criteria F and G: impairment and other explanations. There must be clinically significant distress or impairment, and the fear must not be better explained by another disorder: by agoraphobia (fear of panic-like symptoms in several situations), obsessive-compulsive disorder (fear tied to obsessions, such as fear of blood because of obsessional thoughts of HIV), post-traumatic stress disorder (reminders of a trauma), separation anxiety disorder (separation from attachment figures) or social anxiety disorder (fear of social scrutiny). DSM-5-TR also excludes avoidance confined to food (consider an eating disorder) and fear that grows out of a delusion (consider a psychotic disorder).

Two changes from DSM-IV are worth knowing, because older books and many question banks still use the earlier wording. DSM-IV required that the person recognise that the fear was "excessive or unreasonable" (a requirement it waived for children); DSM-5 dropped this insight requirement and put the judgement of "out of proportion" in the clinician's hands, because the evidence showed that people with phobias tend to overestimate the danger and that older adults often put their fears down to ageing, so that relying on the patient's insight missed real cases. And DSM-IV applied its six-month duration only to people under 18; DSM-5 extended "typically 6 months" to all ages. The same two changes were made to social anxiety disorder.

Finally, phobias seldom come singly. DSM-5-TR reports that the average person with specific phobia fears three objects or situations, and about three-quarters fear more than one. Each is diagnosed and coded separately: a person who fears thunderstorms and flying receives two diagnoses, specific phobia, natural environment type, and specific phobia, situational type.

Exam trap

A phobia is best described as a persistent irrational fear of a specific object or situation. It is not a mild stress reaction (ordinary or adjustment-level distress, Chapter 7), not a personality disorder (a pervasive trait pattern, Chapter 11) and not a temporary hallucination (a perceptual symptom, Chapter 2): it is an anxiety disorder. The DSM-5 duration for specific phobia is typically 6 months, not 1 week, 1 month or 3 months. The adult no longer has to recognise that the fear is excessive; the clinician judges it out of proportion.

The five types of specific phobia

Jack D. Maser once compiled a list of named phobias from medical dictionaries and other sources, and the entries under the letter "a" alone run from acarophobia (insects or mites) to autophobia (being alone). Barlow's verdict on such lists is that they have little or no value for the study of psychopathology. What does have value is the discovery, built into DSM-IV in 1994 (which also replaced the older name "simple phobia" with "specific phobia"), that phobias fall into a small number of types that differ in their age of onset, their physiology and the way they run in families. DSM-5-TR keeps five, recorded as specifiers and coded by the phobic stimulus (Table 6.8).

The animal type covers spiders, insects, dogs, snakes, rats and mice, and the like. Such fears are common; they count as phobias only when the fear is far beyond ordinary distaste and the avoidance seriously interferes with life, as with Barlow's patients who could not read a magazine in case they turned a page and met a picture of a snake. The natural environment type covers heights, storms and water. These fears tend to cluster (a person who fears deep water is likely to fear storms too), and because each of these situations carries some real danger, some fear of them is adaptive. Barlow suggests that we may be biologically prepared to learn these fears easily, an idea, preparedness, taught in Section 6.8 (Book 1, Section 6.4.6). Animal and natural environment phobias usually begin early, at a peak age of about 7.

The blood-injection-injury type covers needles and injections, invasive medical procedures, the sight of blood, and injury. It is physiologically unique, and the next subsection is devoted to it. The situational type covers aeroplanes, lifts, enclosed places, tunnels and driving. Madden's claustrophobia was situational. Situational phobias begin later than the other types, often from the mid-teens to the mid-twenties, much like panic disorder and agoraphobia, and for a time they were thought to be closely related to those disorders. The essential difference is that the person with a situational phobia has panic attacks only in or near the phobic situation and can relax completely when it is not in prospect, whereas the person with panic disorder may be struck anywhere. Situational phobias are also the type that most often brings people to clinics, probably because avoiding driving, flying or lifts is so hard to arrange in modern life. Finally, the other type collects phobias that fit nowhere else: fear of situations that might lead to choking or vomiting, fear of contracting an illness, and, in children, fear of loud sounds or of costumed characters such as clowns. Most phobias begin in childhood, but some can begin at any age after a frightening experience; a phobia of choking, for example, almost always follows a real near-choking episode.

Table 6.8 The five types of specific phobia in DSM-5-TR.
TypeTypical phobic stimuliTypical onsetDistinctive features
AnimalSpiders, insects, dogs, snakes, rats and miceEarly childhood (peak about 7 years)Mostly women; fears of one animal type run in families
Natural environmentHeights, storms, waterEarly childhood (peak about 7 years)Fears cluster together; some danger is real; common in older adults, with fear of falling
Blood-injection-injuryNeedles, injections, invasive medical procedures, blood, injuryChildhood (average about 9 years)Vasovagal fainting; strongest family loading; women and men nearly equal
SituationalAeroplanes, lifts, enclosed places, tunnels, drivingLater: mid-teens to mid-twentiesOverlaps agoraphobia; attacks only in the situation; most often seen in clinics
OtherChoking, vomiting, contracting illness; in children loud sounds, costumed charactersAny age; often after a frightening eventFear of choking usually follows a near-choking episode

Two boundaries need care. The first is between a situational phobia and agoraphobia, which share several situations (aeroplanes and lifts overlap with agoraphobia's "public transport" and "enclosed places"). DSM-5-TR gives two tests. One is the count: a person who fears only one of the agoraphobic situations has a specific phobia, situational type, whereas a person who fears, for example, aeroplanes, lifts and crowds, touching two of agoraphobia's five situations, is likely to have agoraphobia. The other is the reason: if the situation is feared because it might directly cause harm (the plane might crash, the lift cable might snap, the dog might bite), the diagnosis leans towards specific phobia; if it is feared because escape or help would be hard if panic struck, it leans towards agoraphobia. The second boundary is with panic disorder. People with a specific phobia may panic when they meet the phobic stimulus, but those attacks are expected; only if they also have unexpected attacks, and worry about them, is panic disorder added.

Exam trap

Know the type of every common phobia. Claustrophobia (enclosed spaces, lifts) and fear of flying are situational; acrophobia (heights), fear of storms and fear of water are natural environment; fear of needles, blood or injury is blood-injection-injury; fear of choking or vomiting is other. Situational phobias begin later than the other types. One feared situation is a specific phobia; two or more of agoraphobia's five, feared because escape would be hard, is agoraphobia.

Blood-injection-injury phobia: the phobia that makes people faint

Farhan, nineteen, a first-year engineering student in Hyderabad, came to the college counsellor after an embarrassing morning. A blood test was required for his hostel medical. He had felt the familiar dread for a week, and as the needle went in he grew hot, then cold and sweaty; the room went grey and he slid off the chair. He came round on the floor with his legs raised and a nurse fanning him. It had happened before: at twelve, watching a cousin's cut being stitched; at fifteen, at a school vaccination drive, which he had avoided ever since by forging a note. He had not been to a dentist in six years despite a painful tooth, he skipped any film his friends warned was "bloody", and he dreaded the thought that a relative might one day need him in a hospital. "My father is the same," he said. "He cannot even watch the cricket when a player is bleeding."

Farhan's story shows what makes the blood-injection-injury type unlike every other phobia. In almost all phobias, meeting the feared object produces a surge of sympathetic arousal: heart rate and blood pressure rise and stay up while the fear lasts. Many people with these phobias, and with panic attacks, say that they feel they are about to faint, but they almost never do, precisely because their blood pressure is rising, not falling. People with blood-injection-injury phobia show a different pattern, called a diphasic vasovagal response because it has two phases. In the first phase, the sight of blood or a needle produces a brief acceleration of heart rate and a rise in blood pressure. In the second, the body overcorrects: heart rate slows, the blood vessels widen and blood pressure drops sharply. Less blood reaches the brain, the person feels light-headed and queasy, vision greys, and he or she may faint. The faint is a vasovagal syncope ("syncope" means a swoon caused by low blood pressure in the head; "vasovagal" refers to the blood vessels and the vagus nerve, which slows the heart). Barlow suggests that the overcorrection comes from an overreactive sinoaortic baroreflex arc, the reflex that normally brings blood pressure back down after a sudden rise, and that the tendency to overcorrect is inherited. Figure 6.6 shows the difference schematically.

Other features follow from this unusual physiology. Blood-injection-injury phobia runs in families more strongly than any other phobia, which fits the idea that what is inherited is the strong vasovagal tendency; the phobia then develops as a fear of having the response (the family figures are given in Section 6.7). Unlike the other types, it affects women and men nearly equally, and its average age of onset is about 9 years. Yet the reflex is not the whole story: many people with a strong fainting tendency never become phobic, because they cope (some learn on their own to tense their muscles when they see blood), and some people with little fainting tendency develop the phobia anyway.

The costs are out of all proportion to how trivial the phobia sounds. People with blood-injection-injury phobia are often reluctant to seek medical care even when they need it. They miss vaccinations, put off blood tests that would detect diabetes or anaemia, avoid the dentist until a tooth must be extracted, and refuse to donate blood. A pregnant woman with the phobia may dread the repeated blood tests and examinations of antenatal care, and some avoid them. In a country that depends on mass immunisation drives and on blood tests for screening, this matters.

Key study: applied tension (Öst and Sterner, 1987)

Ordinary exposure therapy has a problem with this phobia: a patient who faints during exposure learns nothing except that the feared thing makes him faint. The Swedish psychologist Lars-Göran Öst and Ulf Sterner described a solution in 1987 in Behaviour Research and Therapy, which they called applied tension. The patient learns to tense the large muscles of the arms, legs and trunk, holding the tension until a feeling of warmth rises in the face, then releasing it without relaxing completely, and repeating the cycle. Tensing the muscles quickly raises blood pressure, which counteracts the second, fainting phase of the vasovagal response. The patient also learns to recognise the very first signs of a fall in blood pressure and to apply the tension at once, and then practises it while looking at pictures of wounds, watching films of surgery and finally having blood taken, so that the exposure can be completed without fainting. The broader lesson is that a treatment built on the physiology of a particular phobia works better than a general one. Öst and his colleagues later showed that many specific phobias, blood phobia among them, can be treated in a single intensive session of about 2 to 6 hours of guided exposure, followed by practice at home (one of their trials, published in 2001, treated children and adolescents), and Barlow notes that after such treatment the vasovagal tendency itself lessens considerably. Exposure therapy as a method is taught in Book 2, Section 4.3.

Exam trap

Blood-injection-injury phobia shows a diphasic vasovagal response: a brief rise in heart rate and blood pressure, then a drop, and possibly fainting. In every other phobia, and in panic attacks, blood pressure rises and the person feels faint but almost never faints; so "fainting" in a question points to blood-injection-injury phobia. The treatment designed for it is applied tension (tensing, not relaxing, the muscles), because relaxation would lower blood pressure further. It runs in families more strongly than any other phobia, begins at about 9, and affects the sexes nearly equally.

Who develops specific phobia, and how it runs

Specific phobia is one of the most common of all mental disorders. In the United States the 12-month prevalence is about 8 to 12 percent; in Europe it is similar or somewhat lower (about 6 percent), and in Asian, African and Latin American countries it is generally 2 to 4 percent. Among children across countries the average is about 5 percent (range 3 to 9 percent); among adolescents aged 13 to 17 in the United States it reaches about 16 percent; among older people it falls to about 3 to 5 percent, perhaps because the fears fade to a level below the threshold. Women are affected about twice as often as men across the types, with the blood-injection-injury type the exception. (Barlow gives a more lopsided ratio of about 4 to 1; the DSM-5-TR figure of about 2 to 1 is the safer one to quote.)

Specific phobia usually begins in early childhood, most cases before the age of 10, with a median age of onset between 7 and 11 and a mean of about 10; Barlow notes that its onset is earlier than that of any anxiety disorder except separation anxiety disorder. Phobias that begin in childhood often wax and wane during childhood and adolescence, but those that persist into adulthood rarely remit without treatment. How do they begin? Sometimes after a frightening event with the object (being bitten by a dog, being stuck in a lift); sometimes after watching someone else go through one (seeing someone drown); sometimes after an unexpected panic attack in the situation that will later be feared; sometimes after being told about danger (extensive news coverage of a plane crash). These correspond to Stanley Rachman's three pathways to fear (direct conditioning, vicarious learning and information), which are explained in Section 6.8. But many people simply cannot remember how their phobia began.

Phobias are often dismissed as harmless, and DSM-5-TR's figures show why that is a mistake. People with specific phobia show impairment in work, relationships and quality of life comparable to people with other anxiety disorders, and the impairment grows with the number of things feared. Specific phobia is linked to suicidal thoughts and attempts; one large ten-year German study of young people aged 14 to 24 estimated that 30 percent of first suicide attempts were attributable to specific phobia. Because it begins so early, it is usually the first disorder in a person's history, and it raises the risk of later anxiety, depressive, substance-related and somatic symptom disorders and of personality disorders, particularly dependent personality disorder. In older people the most common phobias are of the natural environment and of falling, which can shrink mobility and independence. The biological and psychological causes of the phobias are taught in Sections 6.7 and 6.8, and their drug treatment (little needed, since exposure works so well) in Section 6.9.

The names of the phobias

Indian entrance papers love to ask for the name of a phobia, and the names look forbidding until you see how they are built. Almost all of them join a Greek word (occasionally a Latin one) for the feared thing to the Greek phobos, fear. Learn the root and the name decodes itself: algos is pain, so algophobia is the fear of pain; nosos is disease, so nosophobia is the fear of disease; kyon (genitive kynos) is dog, so cynophobia is the fear of dogs; haima is blood, so haematophobia (also written hemophobia or haemophobia) is the fear of blood. The same roots run through ordinary medical words, which is the best way to remember them: analgesic (a painkiller) contains algos; nosology (the classification of diseases) contains nosos; haemoglobin and haemorrhage contain haima; Acropolis, the "high city" of Athens, contains akros, high; thanatology (the study of death) contains thanatos. Table 6.9 gives the names that appear in the papers, with their roots and with where each would go in DSM-5-TR.

Table 6.9 The names of the phobias, their roots and their place in DSM-5-TR.
NameFear ofRootWhere it goes in DSM-5-TR
AcrophobiaHeightsGreek akron, peak, summit (akros, highest)Specific phobia, natural environment
AerophobiaFlying (literally air; in older lists, draughts)Greek aer, airSpecific phobia, situational
AgoraphobiaPublic places where escape is hard (literally the marketplace)Greek agora, marketplace, place of assemblyAgoraphobia, a separate disorder
AlgophobiaPainGreek algos, painSpecific phobia (often blood-injection-injury when it is the pain of procedures)
ArachnophobiaSpidersGreek arachne, spiderSpecific phobia, animal
AstraphobiaThunder and lightningGreek astrape, lightningSpecific phobia, natural environment
ClaustrophobiaEnclosed spacesLatin claustrum, a shut-in place (claudere, to shut)Specific phobia, situational
CynophobiaDogsGreek kyon, kynos, dogSpecific phobia, animal
GlossophobiaPublic speakingGreek glossa, tongueSocial anxiety disorder, performance only
HaematophobiaBloodGreek haima, bloodSpecific phobia, blood-injection-injury
HydrophobiaWater (also the old name for rabies)Greek hydor, waterSpecific phobia, natural environment
MysophobiaDirt and germsGreek mysos, uncleannessSpecific phobia; tell from obsessive-compulsive disorder
NosophobiaDiseaseGreek nosos, diseaseOverlaps illness anxiety disorder (Chapter 7)
NyctophobiaDarkness, nightGreek nyx, nyktos, nightSpecific phobia if it persists; a normal fear in young children
OphidiophobiaSnakesGreek ophis, serpentSpecific phobia, animal
ThanatophobiaDeathGreek thanatos, deathDeath anxiety; not a DSM category
TrypanophobiaNeedles and injectionsGreek trypanon, borer, augerSpecific phobia, blood-injection-injury
XenophobiaStrangers or foreignersGreek xenos, stranger, foreignerUsually a prejudice, not a clinical phobia

A few entries in the table need a word of explanation. Agoraphobia is the one name in the list that DSM-5-TR uses as the name of a disorder, and its literal meaning, "fear of the marketplace", is itself examined; Subsection 6.2.6 explains why the real theme is being trapped rather than open space. Claustrophobia is the only common name with a Latin root, from claustrum, an enclosed place, the word that also gives "cloister". Aerophobia is built from aer, air, and older lists such as Maser's define it as a fear of draughts and air currents; in modern use it means fear of flying (aviophobia, from the Latin avis, bird, is the other name). Hydrophobia is the name the papers give for a fear of water, but you should know that in medicine hydrophobia is also the old name for rabies, in which attempts to drink set off painful spasms of the throat, so that the patient comes to dread water; modern writers often prefer aquaphobia for the phobia. Mysophobia, from mysos, uncleanness, was coined in 1879 by the American neurologist William Alexander Hammond (1828 to 1900) while describing a patient who washed the hands again and again; what Hammond was describing would today usually be called obsessive-compulsive disorder. That history is a warning. A fear of germs that comes with intrusive thoughts of contamination and with repetitive washing to neutralise them is obsessive-compulsive disorder (Section 6.5), not a phobia; a fear of dirt that is simply avoided, without obsessions or rituals, may be a specific phobia. Xenophobia today usually means hostility to foreigners, which is a social attitude (the Social Psychology book), not a psychiatric disorder; and the wary distress of an eight-month-old baby on meeting a stranger is normal stranger anxiety (Section 6.1), not a phobia at all. Other names you may meet are built the same way: ailurophobia (cats, Greek ailouros), apiphobia (bees, Latin apis) and autophobia (being alone, Greek autos, self).

The clinical point is the one Barlow makes about Maser's list: these names are not diagnoses. A clinician never writes "trypanophobia" in a case record. DSM-5-TR asks only for the type, so the names are translated: trypanophobia and haematophobia become specific phobia, blood-injection-injury type; claustrophobia and aerophobia, situational type; acrophobia, astraphobia and hydrophobia, natural environment type; cynophobia, arachnophobia and ophidiophobia, animal type. Glossophobia is not a specific phobia at all, because the fear is of being judged while speaking; it is social anxiety disorder with the performance only specifier (subsection 6.3.6). And nosophobia, a fear of having or catching a disease, may be a specific phobia of the "other" type (fear of contracting an illness) or, when the person is preoccupied with already having a serious illness, illness anxiety disorder (Chapter 7).

Exam trap

The specific phobia of pain is algophobia (algos, pain, as in analgesic). The distractors are other real names: nosophobia is fear of disease (nosos, as in nosology), cynophobia fear of dogs (kyon, kynos) and haematophobia fear of blood (haima, as in haemoglobin). Decode any unfamiliar name by its root. Agoraphobia literally means fear of the marketplace; claustrophobia (Latin claustrum) is enclosed spaces; acrophobia is heights; nyctophobia is darkness; astraphobia is thunder and lightning; mysophobia is dirt and germs; trypanophobia is needles; glossophobia is public speaking.

Social anxiety disorder: the fear of scrutiny

Prakash, twenty-three, is in the final year of an MSc in chemistry in Mysuru. He is a good student on paper, and he is in trouble. He has not given a single seminar this year; twice he reported sick on the day. In practical classes he cannot pour from a burette while the instructor watches, because his hands shake and he is sure everyone can see. He eats in his hostel room rather than the mess, because he cannot lift a glass of water steadily with people around him. His viva voce is in six weeks, and he has been rehearsing answers aloud every night, word for word. At home in his village it is worse in a different way. His parents have arranged a bride-viewing visit, and for a month he has been dreading the moment when he must sit in the girl's family's front room, hold a cup of tea in front of her father and uncles, and make conversation. "They will see my hand shaking. My voice will go. They will think I am weak, or that something is wrong with me, and my parents will be shamed." He remembers when it began: in Class 8 he was asked to read a passage aloud, stumbled, and the class laughed. Yet with his two closest friends, at his own pace, Prakash is relaxed and funny.

Prakash has social anxiety disorder, the disorder called social phobia until DSM-5 renamed it. Its essence, in DSM-5-TR's words, is a marked fear or anxiety about social situations in which the person may be scrutinised by others. Notice the word: scrutiny. The person with social anxiety disorder does not fear an object, as in a specific phobia, or being trapped, as in agoraphobia. He fears the eyes of other people, and what they will conclude. The criteria again fall into groups.

  • Criterion A: the situations. Marked fear or anxiety about one or more social situations involving possible scrutiny. DSM-5-TR gives three families: social interactions (having a conversation, meeting unfamiliar people), being observed (eating or drinking in front of others), and performing in front of others (giving a speech). In children the anxiety must occur in peer settings and not just with adults, because many children are shy with adults in a way that is entirely normal.
  • Criterion B: the feared outcome. The person fears that he or she will act in a way, or show anxiety symptoms, that will be negatively evaluated: that it will be humiliating or embarrassing, will lead to rejection, or will offend others. The feared symptoms are typically the visible ones (blushing, trembling, sweating, stumbling over words, staring). Each leads to its own avoidance: a person who fears trembling may avoid drinking, eating, writing or pointing in public; one who fears sweating may avoid shaking hands or eating spicy food; one who fears blushing may avoid bright lights and intimate topics. Blushing is the hallmark physical sign of the disorder. Some people, more often men, fear and avoid urinating in a public toilet when others are present, called paruresis or "shy bladder".
  • Criteria C and D: consistency and response. The social situations almost always provoke fear (occasional nervousness does not count), and they are avoided or endured with intense fear. In children the fear may show as crying, tantrums, freezing, clinging, shrinking or failing to speak. Avoidance can be extensive (not going to parties, refusing school) or subtle: over-preparing a speech, as Prakash does, diverting attention to others, limiting eye contact, keeping the hands in the pockets. Anticipatory anxiety can begin weeks before the event.
  • Criterion E: out of proportion. The fear is out of proportion to the actual threat of being negatively evaluated, and to the sociocultural context. If the person is actually being bullied, the anxiety may be realistic. Because people with the disorder overestimate the consequences of social situations, the clinician makes the judgement. Culture matters: in some cultures, behaviour that would look socially anxious elsewhere is the correct way to behave. In many Indian families a young person who lowers his eyes, speaks softly and says little in front of elders is showing respect, not symptoms.
  • Criteria F and G: persistence and impairment. The fear typically lasts 6 months or more, which separates the disorder from the passing social fears of childhood and adolescence, and it must interfere significantly with routine, work or study, or relationships, or cause marked distress. DSM-5-TR's example is precise: a person who is afraid of public speaking does not have the disorder if speaking is never required at work or in class and the fear does not distress him, but if he avoids or is passed over for the job or course he really wants because of it, the impairment criterion is met.
  • Criteria H, I and J: other explanations. The fear is not due to a substance or medical condition, not better explained by another mental disorder such as panic disorder, body dysmorphic disorder or autism spectrum disorder, and if a medical condition that might draw attention is present (Parkinson's disease, obesity, disfigurement from burns or injury), the fear is clearly unrelated to it or excessive.

DSM-5-TR offers one specifier. Performance only is used when the fear is restricted to speaking or performing in public. It suits musicians, dancers, actors, athletes and people whose work or study requires regular presentations, and it describes people who do not fear or avoid ordinary social situations at all. A young Bharatanatyam dancer who is relaxed and talkative at parties but trembles, feels sick and fears her memory will go blank before every performance fits this specifier; so does the student who is at ease in the canteen but cannot face the viva. Barlow gives sporting examples: professional baseball players who suddenly could not make a routine throw to first base when they had time to think about it, and the actress Scarlett Johansson, who stayed away from the Broadway stage for years because of performance anxiety. DSM-5 created this specifier in place of DSM-IV's "generalised" specifier, which had marked the opposite end, fear of most social situations. Glossophobia, the fear of public speaking (subsection 6.3.5), is the most common performance fear.

Several associated features help recognise the disorder in an interview. People with social anxiety disorder may be unassertive or excessively submissive, may hold their bodies rigidly, avoid eye contact or speak very softly, and disclose little about themselves. They may choose jobs without social contact, live at home longer, and delay marriage. Many drink before a social event to steady themselves, and a few go on to an alcohol use disorder. Older adults with the disorder may fear that a tremor, a failing memory for names or a hearing problem will be noticed.

Clinical link

Social anxiety disorder is among the most under-treated of all disorders. DSM-5-TR reports that in high-income countries only about half of the people who have it ever seek treatment, and those who do typically wait 15 to 20 years. The disorder itself keeps people out of the clinic, because seeing a professional means meeting a stranger who will evaluate you. The treatments with good evidence are exposure (Book 2, Section 4.3), cognitive therapy built on the Clark and Wells model of social anxiety, which explains how self-focused attention and safety behaviours keep the fear alive (Book 2, Section 5.6.4, and Section 6.8 of this chapter), and social skills training where skills are genuinely lacking (Book 2, Section 4.7). The drugs, including the beta blockers used for performance anxiety, are compared in Section 6.9.

Social anxiety disorder: who has it, how it runs, and what it is not

The 12-month prevalence of social anxiety disorder in the United States is about 7 percent. Using the same interview, most of the rest of the world reports much lower figures, clustering around 0.5 to 2 percent, with a median of 2.3 percent in Europe; prevalence appears to be rising in the United States and in East Asian countries. Among young adolescents (13 to 17) the rate is roughly half the adult rate, and it falls after 65, to between 2 and 5 percent of older adults in North America, Europe and Australia. In the general population women have the disorder more often than men, with odds ratios between 1.5 and 2.2, and the gap is widest in adolescents and young adults. In clinical samples, however, the numbers of men and women are equal or men are slightly more numerous, probably because social expectations push men to seek help when the disorder blocks a career or marriage. (Barlow's text puts the sex ratio close to 50:50; DSM-5-TR's figures, which keep community and clinic samples apart, are the safer ones to quote.)

The disorder begins early. The median age of onset in the United States is 13 years, and three-quarters of people with it date its onset to between 8 and 15. It may grow slowly out of childhood shyness or social inhibition, or it may follow a stressful or humiliating experience, such as being bullied or, like Prakash, being laughed at while reading aloud. A first onset in adulthood is relatively rare and usually follows a humiliating event or a change of role that brings new social demands (a promotion, marriage into a family of a different social standing). The course is variable, short and fluctuating for some, chronic for others, and among people who reach clinics it tends to be particularly persistent; DSM-5-TR notes that a fear of dating may fade after marriage and return after divorce. Social anxiety disorder commonly precedes the other disorders that accompany it: other anxiety disorders, major depressive disorder (chronic isolation can lead to depression) and substance use disorders, especially alcohol used as self-medication. It is frequently comorbid with avoidant personality disorder. Its consequences include leaving school early, lower employment, income and quality of life, fewer and less supportive friendships, and, particularly among men, remaining single. The roles of temperament (behavioural inhibition, studied by Jerome Kagan) and genes are taught in Section 6.7, and the evolutionary preparedness to fear angry and critical faces, with the cognitive model, in Section 6.8.

Culture shapes the fear itself. In Japan and Korea a syndrome called taijin kyofusho ("fear of interpersonal relations") centres not on embarrassing oneself, as social anxiety disorder usually does, but on offending or making other people uncomfortable: the fear that one's gaze, one's blushing, one's facial expression or one's body odour is upsetting to others ("my gaze upsets people, so they look away and avoid me"). DSM-5-TR notes that the fear is sometimes held with delusional intensity, and that while many presentations meet the criteria for social anxiety disorder, others fit body dysmorphic disorder or delusional disorder. Barlow reports that among Japanese sufferers men outnumber women, by about 3 to 2. The form centred on body odour resembles olfactory reference disorder, which is described with the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders in Section 6.6, and the cultural syndromes in general are taught in Chapter 1. DSM-5-TR adds that in strongly collectivist cultures a fear of offending others may be the main fear even in people diagnosed with social anxiety disorder.

Indian context

Indian settings put social evaluation at the centre of many life events. The viva voce and the group discussion in admissions and job interviews, reading aloud or answering questions in class, the arranged-marriage "bride-viewing" or "girl-seeing" visit where a young man or woman is watched and assessed by a whole family, speaking at a family function, and even the morning school assembly can all be the situations a socially anxious person dreads. A clinician should keep two things apart: modesty, reticence and deference before elders, which are culturally expected and are not symptoms (Criterion E), and a fear of being judged that is out of proportion, is felt almost every time, and makes the person avoid or dread what he or she needs to do. Prakash, who has turned down two seminar slots and is planning to miss his own viva, has crossed that line.

Because social anxiety is woven into so many other conditions, the differential diagnosis is examined heavily, and DSM-5-TR spends unusual care on it. The most important comparisons are set out in Table 6.10. Two need a further word. Ordinary shyness (social reticence) is a common personality trait and, by itself, is not pathological; some societies even value it. Only about 12 percent of people in the United States who call themselves shy meet the criteria for social anxiety disorder, and the diagnosis is made only when the shyness causes significant impairment and all the criteria are met. Avoidant personality disorder (Chapter 11) is the closest neighbour. It shows a broader pattern of avoidance, more impairment, a strongly and pervasively negative view of oneself, a sense that rejection means one is worthless, and a feeling of not fitting in that dates from early childhood. The two often occur together: social anxiety disorder is more often comorbid with avoidant personality disorder than with any other personality disorder, and avoidant personality disorder is more often comorbid with social anxiety disorder than with any other anxiety disorder.

Table 6.10 Social anxiety disorder and the conditions it is confused with.
ConditionWhat the person fears or doesHow it differs from social anxiety disorder
Normal shynessQuiet and reserved with strangersA trait; no significant impairment
Avoidant personality disorder (Chapter 11)Pervasive inferiority, fear of rejection, avoidance from early lifeBroader avoidance and a global negative self-view; often coexists
Agoraphobia (Section 6.2)Being trapped without help if panic strikesFear of escape being hard, not of judgement; anxious even when alone
Panic disorder (Section 6.2)Unexpected attacks "out of the blue"In social anxiety, attacks come only in social situations, and the shame matters more than the attack
Specific phobia (this section)Embarrassment only about one thing, such as fainting at a blood testNo fear of evaluation in other social situations
Body dysmorphic disorder (Section 6.6)A perceived flaw in appearanceSocial fear comes only from the belief about appearance
Selective mutism (Section 6.4)Silence in some settingsNo fear in situations needing no speech, such as play
Autism spectrum disorder (Chapter 3)Social communication deficitsSocial anxiety disorder has adequate social skills and relationships
Major depressive disorder (Chapter 5)Withdrawal from lost interest; feels worthless and unlikeableSocial anxiety fears that particular behaviours or symptoms will be judged
Separation anxiety disorder (Section 6.4)Being away from attachment figuresAt ease socially when the attachment figure is present
Generalised anxiety disorder (Section 6.4)Worries about relationships and many other mattersWorry is not focused on being evaluated
Exam trap

Social anxiety disorder is fear of social scrutiny and negative evaluation: being humiliated, embarrassed, rejected or offending others. It is not fear of one object (specific phobia) or of being trapped (agoraphobia). Its duration, like that of specific phobia and agoraphobia, is typically 6 months, not 1 week, 1 month or 3 months. In children the anxiety must appear with peers, not only with adults. The only specifier is performance only (fear restricted to speaking or performing in public), which replaced DSM-IV's "generalised" specifier. And remember that a phobia, social or specific, is an anxiety disorder.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. DSM-5-TR says a panic attack reaches its peak within , which means literally only a few of them.
  2. Of the thirteen panic attack symptoms, eleven are physical and are cognitive: fear of losing control or going crazy, and fear of dying.
  3. An attack meeting every other criterion but with fewer than four symptoms is a attack.
  4. Nocturnal panic attacks arise in sleep, which is why they are not nightmares.
  5. Panic disorder requires at least one attack to be followed by or more of persistent worry or maladaptive change in behaviour.
  6. In 1978 Goldstein and Chambless described the fear of the bodily sensations of anxiety as "fear of ".
  7. The term agoraphobia was coined in 1871 by the German psychiatrist Carl (Karl) .
  8. Agoraphobia requires marked fear of at least of five situations.
  9. Tensing the muscles during exposure to stop the fall in blood pressure of blood-injection-injury phobia is called applied .

Answers: (1) minutes, (2) two, (3) limited-symptom, (4) slow-wave (deep non-REM, delta), (5) 1 month, (6) fear, (7) Westphal, (8) two, (9) tension, (10) algophobia.

6.4 Generalised Anxiety Disorder, Separation Anxiety Disorder and Selective MutismMedium yield · 4 Qs

Sunita is 44. She teaches mathematics at a girls' school in Nagpur and takes tuition classes at home in the evenings. She arrived at the psychiatry outpatient department of a medical college hospital holding a letter from her family physician: "Tension headaches, poor sleep, epigastric discomfort for two years. ECG, thyroid function and endoscopy normal. ?Anxiety. Kindly evaluate." When the psychologist asked what she worried about, she laughed. "What do I not worry about?" Then the list came out, one item tumbling over the next. Her husband rides his scooter on the ring road, and every evening, from half past six until she hears the gate, she pictures the trucks. Her daughter's Class 12 board examinations are four months away. The bank may raise the interest on their home loan. Her mother's sugar is not well controlled. She may have offended a neighbour by forgetting to invite her to a function. The milk might boil over while she checks the students' notebooks. "When one thing is settled, another one comes and sits in its place. I know most of it is useless. My husband says I borrow trouble from tomorrow. But I cannot switch it off." Her neck and shoulders, she said, feel "like stone"; she is tired by noon, snaps at her students and her daughter, loses the thread while marking papers, and lies awake going over the next day. She has never had a sudden attack of terror, she performs no rituals, and although she is weary she is not chiefly sad. "I have been a tension type since school," she said. "These last two years it is too much."

Sunita has generalised anxiety disorder (GAD), the most ordinary looking of the anxiety disorders and, for most of the history of psychiatry, the hardest to pin down. A spider phobia has a spider; panic disorder has its attacks. GAD has no single object and no dramatic event. Its core is a mental activity that everyone engages in, worry, carried on too much, about too many things, for too long, and with too little control. This section first teaches GAD, from its history to its course, and then turns to two anxiety disorders that usually begin in childhood: separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism. The causes of all three are taught in Sections 6.7 and 6.8 and their drug treatment in Section 6.9; here the task is to recognise them and to tell them from their look-alikes.

From free-floating anxiety to a diagnosis

Chronic, diffuse anxiety is one of the oldest complaints in medicine. Eighteenth- and nineteenth-century physicians used the word pantophobia ("fear of everything") for patients who seemed afraid of all things at once, and the American neurologist George Miller Beard, who popularised neurasthenia from 1869 (a diagnosis taught in Chapter 7), listed "pantaphobia", a fear of everything, among its many forms. The decisive description came from Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939). In 1895 he carved an "anxiety neurosis" out of neurasthenia (Section 6.1 tells that story) and named as its central symptom anxious expectation: a constant readiness to expect the worst. His example could have come from Sunita's clinic. A woman who suffers from anxious expectation, he wrote, will think of pneumonia every time her husband coughs with a cold, and in her mind's eye will see his funeral go past. Freud described this state as a quantum of anxiety "in a freely floating state", ready to attach itself to any idea that came along, and from that phrase came the term free-floating anxiety: anxiety that is not bound to any particular object or situation but drifts from one concern to the next.

For decades afterwards clinicians assumed, as Barlow and Durand put it, that people who were generally anxious had simply not yet focused their anxiety on anything specific. The first American manuals followed that view. DSM-I (1952) had an "anxiety reaction" for diffuse anxiety that was neither tied to particular situations, as in the phobic reactions, nor controlled by a particular defence, and DSM-II (1968) renamed it "anxiety neurosis", a single category that covered both chronic apprehension and sudden attacks. The split came with DSM-III in 1980. Partly because the attacks and the chronic anxiety responded differently to the antidepressant imipramine (Section 6.2 tells how Donald Klein's observation helped separate them), anxiety neurosis was divided into panic disorder and generalised anxiety disorder. GAD was born.

It was a weak birth. DSM-III asked for only one month of generalised, persistent anxiety, with symptoms from three of four broad groups (motor tension, autonomic hyperactivity, apprehensive expectation, and vigilance and scanning). So loose a definition caught almost everyone who was anxious. In 1986 David H. Barlow and his colleagues pointed out that DSM-III's GAD was largely a residual diagnosis: patients with other anxiety disorders nearly always met its criteria too, so the label added little. They also made a suggestion that shaped everything that followed. There might be two kinds of "generalised anxiety": a residual kind, the apprehension that accompanies any anxiety disorder, and a qualitatively different syndrome of chronic worry that deserved its own name.

The revisions moved steadily in that direction. DSM-III-R (1987) raised the duration to six months and put worry at the centre: "unrealistic or excessive anxiety and worry (apprehensive expectation) about two or more life circumstances", with at least six of eighteen listed symptoms. DSM-IV (1994) emphasised that the worry is hard to control, cut the eighteen symptoms to six (research had shown that the bodily profile of GAD differs from that of the other anxiety disorders, so most of the autonomic items of the older list, such as a racing heart, were dropped), required clinically significant distress or impairment, and absorbed the childhood diagnosis of overanxious disorder into GAD, which is why the modern criteria carry a note about children. DSM-5 (2013) changed almost nothing. A review led by Gavin Andrews and colleagues (2010) had proposed renaming the condition "generalised worry disorder" and adding a criterion for the behaviours that worry produces (avoidance, over-preparation, procrastination, reassurance seeking), and others argued that GAD is so close to major depression that both should be grouped as "distress disorders". Neither proposal was adopted, and DSM-5-TR (2022) keeps the DSM-IV definition. The lesson of this history is the one Barlow drew in 1986: what makes GAD a disorder in its own right is not anxiety in general but a particular way of worrying.

Exam trap

GAD as a named diagnosis dates from DSM-III (1980), when anxiety neurosis was split into GAD and panic disorder. Its first duration rule was one month; the six-month rule and the central place of worry date from DSM-III-R (1987); the six symptoms and the emphasis on uncontrollable worry from DSM-IV (1994). "Free-floating anxiety" goes back to Freud's 1895 description of anxious expectation in anxiety neurosis. Do not credit Freud with the word neurosis itself, which was William Cullen's (Section 6.1).

The criteria for generalised anxiety disorder, group by group

The DSM-5-TR criteria make sense once you see that each one answers a question a sceptic would ask: everyone worries, so what makes this worry a disorder? The answers come in four groups: what the worry is like (criteria A and B), what comes with it (C), what it costs (D), and what else it might be (E and F).

The worry itself (A and B). Criterion A asks for excessive anxiety and worry, which DSM-5-TR glosses as apprehensive expectation, occurring more days than not for at least six months, about a number of events or activities such as work or school performance. Each phrase does work. Thomas D.
