Borkovec and his colleagues in 1983 defined worry as a chain of thoughts and images, loaded with negative feeling and relatively uncontrollable, that represents an attempt at mental problem solving on an issue whose outcome is uncertain but might be bad. Notice that worry is mostly verbal ("What if the bus is late? Then I will miss the train. Then ...") and points to the future. "Excessive" means that the intensity, duration or frequency of the worry is out of proportion to the actual likelihood or impact of the feared event: Sunita's husband is a careful rider on a road he has used for twenty years, yet she pictures his death every evening. "More days than not" is a deliberate softening; the worry need not be present every single day, but it must be the usual state of affairs. "A number of events or activities" is what makes the disorder generalised: one dominant fear points to a different diagnosis. Criterion B adds that the person finds the worry difficult to control. This is the heart of the matter. People with GAD usually know they worry too much; what they cannot do is stop, put the worry aside when something more pressing comes up, or keep it from intruding on the task in front of them.

The company the worry keeps (C). The anxiety and worry must come with at least three of six symptoms, at least some of them present more days than not over the same six months (Figure 6.7). The six are restlessness or feeling keyed up or on edge; being easily fatigued; difficulty concentrating or the mind going blank; irritability; muscle tension; and sleep disturbance (difficulty falling or staying asleep, or restless, unsatisfying sleep). Look at the list and you will see that it describes a mind and body held in a state of readiness for a threat that never quite arrives: the muscles braced, the attention divided, the temper short, the sleep shallow, and the person exhausted by the effort. Sunita had all six. For children only one of the six is required, a rule that Barlow's group tested in children and adolescents and found to work (Tracey, Chorpita, Douban and Barlow 1997).

The cost (D). The anxiety, worry or physical symptoms must cause clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. This is the threshold that separates a worrier from a patient (the four Ds of abnormality are taught in Book 2, Section 1.6.5). Sunita was still teaching, but she was snapping at her students, losing her place in her marking and spending her evenings in dread; that was enough.

The exclusions (E and F). Criterion E rules out anxiety that is the physiological effect of a substance or medication (heavy coffee drinking produces caffeine-induced anxiety disorder; withdrawal from alcohol or benzodiazepines produces anxiety too, Chapter 10) or of another medical condition (DSM-5-TR names hyperthyroidism, and also pheochromocytoma, a hormone-secreting tumour of the adrenal gland). This is why Sunita's physician checked her thyroid before referring her (the medical mimics of anxiety are listed in Section 6.1). Criterion F rules out worry that belongs to another mental disorder. DSM-5-TR's list is long, but it follows one logic: in each case the worry has a single home. It may be worry about having panic attacks (panic disorder), about negative evaluation (social anxiety disorder), about contamination or other obsessions (obsessive-compulsive disorder), about separation from attachment figures (separation anxiety disorder), about reminders of a trauma (posttraumatic stress disorder), about gaining weight (anorexia nervosa), about physical complaints (somatic symptom disorder), about perceived flaws in appearance (body dysmorphic disorder), about having a serious illness (illness anxiety disorder), or about the content of delusions (schizophrenia or delusional disorder). GAD is what remains when the worry has no single home and wanders across the whole of life.

Exam trap

The DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR duration for GAD in adults is at least 6 months, more days than not. The wrong options all come from somewhere: 1 month was DSM-III's original GAD rule and is today the rule for panic disorder's period of concern and for selective mutism; 3 months is the window within which an adjustment disorder must begin after its stressor (Chapter 7); 12 months belongs to no anxiety disorder. The defining symptom of GAD is persistent, excessive worry that is hard to control. Mania and mood swings are mood symptoms (Chapter 5) and hallucinations are psychotic symptoms (Chapter 4); none is a criterion of GAD. Count carefully too: three of six symptoms in adults, but only one in children.

What GAD worry is like, and how it differs from ordinary worry

Ask people with GAD what they worry about and you will hear the ordinary business of life. DSM-5-TR lists job responsibilities, health and finances, the health of family members, misfortune to their children, and minor matters such as household chores or being late for appointments. The minor matters are the giveaway. When people with anxiety disorders were asked "Do you worry excessively about minor things?", every one of those with GAD said yes, against about half of those with other anxiety disorders (Barlow 2002, reported by Barlow and Durand), a difference large enough to be statistically significant. Large events quickly become the focus too, and the focus never stays put: as soon as one concern is settled another takes its place, which is exactly what Sunita meant when she said the next worry "comes and sits in its place".

The content shifts with age, though the disorder itself looks much the same across the lifespan. Children and adolescents with GAD worry mainly about their competence and the quality of their performance at school or in sport, even when nobody is evaluating them; they may worry about being on time, or about catastrophes such as earthquakes or nuclear war. They tend to be over-conforming, perfectionistic and unsure of themselves, redo work that is not perfect, and seek reassurance again and again ("Ma, will I pass? Are you sure?"). Older adults worry most about health, and in the frail elderly a fear of falling may shrink daily life to a single room.

Where, then, is the line between Sunita and every other mother in Nagpur who worries about a daughter's board examinations? DSM-5-TR draws it with three features, summarised in Table 6.11. First, GAD worries are excessive and interfere with functioning, whereas everyday worries feel manageable and can be put off when something more pressing arises. Second, GAD worries are more pervasive, pronounced and distressing, last longer, and often arise with no precipitating event; and the wider the range of life circumstances a person worries about, the more likely the diagnosis. Third, ordinary worries rarely come with physical symptoms such as restlessness and feeling keyed up.

Table 6.11 Everyday worry and the worry of generalised anxiety disorder (after DSM-5-TR).
FeatureEveryday worryWorry in GAD
SizeIn proportion to the real likelihood and impact of the eventExcessive: out of proportion to the likelihood or impact
ControlManageable; can be postponed when something more pressing comes upHard to control; intrudes on the task in hand
RangeOne or two live concernsMany areas of life, including minor matters; hops from topic to topic
Trigger and durationUsually set off by a real event; fades when it is resolvedOften no precipitant; more days than not for at least 6 months
BodySeldom accompanied by physical symptomsMuscle tension, restlessness, fatigue, poor sleep
CostLittle interference with work, study or relationshipsClinically significant distress or impairment

The bodily side of GAD holds a surprise. You might expect a person who worries all day to have a heart that races all day. It does not. Panic is a surge of the sympathetic nervous system: palpitations, sweating, trembling, breathlessness. GAD, by contrast, is marked by muscle tension, mental agitation, fatigue, irritability and poor sleep. When researchers measured how people respond to a stressor, those with GAD showed smaller responses than people with other anxiety disorders on most physiological measures, including heart rate, blood pressure, skin conductance and breathing rate (Borkovec and Hu 1990). For this reason they have been called autonomic restrictors (Barlow and colleagues 1996; Thayer, Friedman and Borkovec 1996). The one physiological measure that consistently separates people with GAD from non-anxious people is muscle tension: they are chronically tense. DSM-5-TR adds trembling, twitching, shakiness and aching muscles, sweating, nausea and diarrhoea, an exaggerated startle response, and frequent stress-related conditions such as irritable bowel syndrome and headaches, while noting that racing heart, shortness of breath and dizziness are less prominent than in panic disorder. Why should worry go with restricted arousal but tense muscles? Borkovec's answer, that verbal worry is a way of avoiding the vivid images that would trigger real fear, is a theory of cause and is taught in Section 6.8.

Culture shapes what the clinician first sees. DSM-5-TR notes that in some cultural contexts the bodily symptoms dominate the presentation and in others the cognitive ones do, and that the topics people worry about are culturally specific, so a clinician must judge excessiveness against what is usual in the person's setting. Sunita's route to the clinic, through two years of headaches, "gas" and sleeplessness taken to a general practitioner, is typical. Barlow and Durand report that only about 10 percent of patients at specialist anxiety clinics have GAD, against 30 to 50 percent with panic disorder, largely because people with GAD take their complaints to primary care doctors, where they are found in large numbers.

Exam trap

A question asks what the sufferer of GAD experiences, and the keyed answer is continued apprehension about unspecified things: free-floating apprehension that is not tied to one object or situation. Do not misread "unspecified" as "without content" (Sunita can list her worries); it means that no single object or theme defines the anxiety. The distractors each name another disorder. "Fear of fear" (Goldstein and Chambless 1978) is the fear of one's own bodily sensations in panic disorder and agoraphobia (Section 6.2). Continual flashbacks to past events are re-experiencing in PTSD (Chapter 7). A desire to check that the environment is safe is best read as the checking of OCD (Section 6.5), although it could also describe the hypervigilance of PTSD; either way it is not GAD, whose worry brings no ritual.

Generalised anxiety disorder in ICD-10 and ICD-11

The two editions of the WHO classification handle GAD a little differently from DSM, and examiners like the differences. ICD-10 places generalised anxiety disorder (F41.1) among the "other anxiety disorders" (F41), next to panic disorder (F41.0) and mixed anxiety and depressive disorder (F41.2, taught in Chapter 5), and its category explicitly includes the older labels anxiety neurosis, anxiety reaction and anxiety state while excluding neurasthenia. ICD-10's clinical guidelines describe anxiety that is generalised and persistent and "free-floating", and ask for primary symptoms of anxiety on most days for at least several weeks at a time, and usually several months. The symptoms are grouped under three headings: apprehension (worries about future misfortunes, feeling on edge, difficulty concentrating), motor tension (restless fidgeting, tension headaches, trembling, inability to relax) and autonomic overactivity (light-headedness, sweating, a fast heart or fast breathing, epigastric discomfort, dizziness, dry mouth). ICD-10's separate research criteria are stricter: at least six months of prominent tension, worry and apprehension about everyday events, with at least four symptoms from a list of twenty-two, one of them autonomic. ICD-10 also did not allow the diagnosis when the full criteria for phobic anxiety, panic, obsessive-compulsive or hypochondriacal disorder were met.

ICD-11 (code 6B00, in the grouping "anxiety or fear-related disorders") describes marked symptoms of anxiety that persist for at least several months, for more days than not, shown either as general apprehension (which it calls "free-floating anxiety") or as excessive worry about multiple everyday events, most often family, health, finances, and school or work. Worry about everyday events was thus added as an alternative to general apprehension. The accompanying symptoms (muscle tension or motor restlessness, sympathetic overactivity, nervousness, difficulty concentrating, irritability, sleep disturbance) are listed without a fixed count, the symptoms must cause significant distress or impairment, and, unlike ICD-10, ICD-11 allows GAD to be diagnosed alongside other mental disorders.

Exam trap

Only DSM-5-TR (and ICD-10's research criteria) fixes the duration of GAD at 6 months. The ICD-10 clinical guidelines say "at least several weeks at a time, and usually several months"; ICD-11 says "at least several months", more days than not, with no fixed number of months and no fixed symptom count (the practice-mock explanation that says otherwise is flagged in Section 6.1). If a question asks for the DSM-5-TR minimum, the answer is 6 months; if it asks what ICD-11 requires, the answer is several months.

Who develops generalised anxiety disorder, and what becomes of it

DSM-5-TR gives a 12-month prevalence of 2.9 percent among adults and 0.9 percent among adolescents in the United States community; around the world the mean 12-month figure is 1.3 percent, with a range from 0.2 to 4.3 percent. Barlow and Durand, quoting the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, give 3.1 percent for a year and 5.7 percent for a lifetime. DSM-5-TR's lifetime figure is higher still, 9.0 percent, because it is a lifetime morbid risk: a projection of the proportion of people who will develop the disorder by the end of a normal lifespan, which counts the young people in a survey who have not yet reached the age of onset. (A lifetime prevalence, by contrast, counts only those who have had the disorder up to the day of the interview.) Women and girls are at least twice as likely as men and boys to have GAD; about two-thirds of the people with GAD in community surveys, and 55 to 60 percent of those presenting to clinics, are women. The 12-month prevalence among older adults, including people over 75, is 2.8 to 3.1 percent in the United States, Israel and Europe. People from high-income countries are more likely than those from low- and middle-income countries to report a lifetime history, and people of European descent meet the criteria more often than people of Asian and African descent, although the reasons are not clear.

When does it start? DSM-5-TR gives a mean age at onset of 35 years in North America, later than any other anxiety disorder, and says the disorder rarely begins before adolescence; Barlow and Durand give a median of 31, from interview data. The two figures are not in conflict. A median is the midpoint of the ages at onset, whereas a mean is pulled upwards by the long tail of people whose GAD begins in middle or late life, so in a skewed distribution like this one the mean lies above the median; the two figures also come from different studies. A deeper puzzle is that Barlow and Durand describe GAD as having an earlier and more gradual onset than most anxiety disorders, which seems to contradict DSM-5-TR. The puzzle dissolves once you ask what is being dated. Many people with GAD, like Sunita, say they have felt anxious and nervous all their lives, and DSM-5-TR explains that excessive worry may begin early in life as an anxious temperament and only later reach the threshold of the disorder. The age at which the full criteria are first met is late; the age at which the person first remembers being a worrier is early (the temperament itself, and the genes behind it, are taught in Section 6.7).

Once established, GAD is chronic. Its symptoms wax and wane across the lifespan, moving between full and subthreshold forms, and DSM-5-TR says that rates of full remission are very low. One study found only an 8 percent probability of becoming symptom-free after two years (Yonkers and colleagues 1996), and Bruce and colleagues (2005) found that twelve years after an episode began there was only a 58 percent chance of having recovered, with 45 percent of those who recovered relapsing later (both reported by Barlow and Durand). The earlier the onset, the more comorbidity and impairment. The cost is high: most adults with GAD outside institutions are moderately to seriously disabled, the disorder accounts for an estimated 110 million disability days a year in the United States, and it is linked to poorer work performance, heavier use of medical services and a raised risk of coronary disease. It is also linked to suicidal thoughts and behaviour even after other disorders are taken into account; in psychological autopsy studies GAD is the anxiety disorder most often diagnosed in people who died by suicide. In older adults the use of minor tranquillisers is strikingly high, and the benzodiazepines bring their own dangers of confusion, falls and hip fractures (Section 6.9).

Pure GAD is the exception. People who meet its criteria are likely to have met, or to meet now, the criteria for other anxiety disorders and above all for major depression, and DSM-5-TR traces this overlap to a shared temperament of negative affectivity (neuroticism) and to shared genetic and environmental risk (the genetics are taught in Section 6.7). In women the comorbidity is mostly with other anxiety disorders and depression; in men it more often extends to substance use. The closeness of GAD and depression is why some researchers wanted to group them together as "distress disorders", and it is why both classifications offer a home for people with both kinds of symptoms below full threshold: ICD-10's mixed anxiety and depressive disorder (F41.2) and DSM-5-TR's "with anxious distress" specifier for depressive episodes, both taught in Chapter 5.

Telling generalised anxiety disorder from its neighbours

Because GAD is defined partly by what it is not, the differential diagnosis is half the work. Start with normal worry (Table 6.11). Then rule out the medical and substance causes (criterion E): an overactive thyroid, a pheochromocytoma, or simply eight cups of strong coffee a day. Then ask, for each worry, whether it has a single home in another disorder (criterion F). In social anxiety disorder the anticipatory anxiety is about upcoming situations in which the person will be watched or judged, whereas a person with GAD worries whether or not anyone is evaluating them. In separation anxiety disorder the worry is only about separation from attachment figures. In illness anxiety disorder the only fear is of having a serious illness oneself, whereas in GAD health is just one item on a long list. Panic attacks can occur in GAD, but attacks that are set off by worry do not make panic disorder; only unexpected attacks followed by persistent concern about them do (Section 6.2).

Three neighbours need a closer look. The first is obsessive-compulsive disorder. GAD worries are about forthcoming real-life problems, and what is abnormal is their excess; OCD obsessions are intrusive, unwanted thoughts, urges or images, often odd or magical in content, that the person tries to neutralise, usually with a compulsion. The distinction is set out in full, along with depressive rumination and delusion, in Table 6.14 in Section 6.5. The second is depression. Depressive rumination dwells on the past (loss, failure, guilt) in a low mood, whereas GAD worry runs ahead into the future; when the worry is severe enough in a depressed person, both diagnoses may be given. The third is the stress-related group of Chapter 7. Anxiety is always present in posttraumatic stress disorder, and GAD is not diagnosed if the worry is better explained by PTSD; and an adjustment disorder with anxiety begins within three months of an identifiable stressor and does not persist more than six months after the stressor and its consequences end, whereas GAD needs no stressor and runs for years.

Clinical link

A useful interview question for suspected GAD is: "If this particular problem were solved tomorrow, would you stop worrying?" People with an adjustment problem say yes; people with GAD usually smile, as Sunita did, and say that something else would take its place. The treatments with the best evidence are cognitive behavioural therapy, applied relaxation (Book 2, Section 4.8.4) and metacognitive therapy (Book 2, Section 5.10.3), and the SSRIs and SNRIs among drugs (Section 6.9); much GAD is first treated in primary care, which is why it sits at the base of stepped-care models (Book 2, Section 5.11.5).

Separation anxiety disorder

Kunal is 8 and in Class 3 at an English-medium school in Kolkata. In the summer his grandmother, who had lived with the family since he was born, died after a short illness, and two months later his father was admitted to hospital for a heart procedure. His father is home and well now, but Kunal is not. For six weeks he has cried and clung to his mother every school morning, complaining of stomach ache and twice vomiting at the school gate; on Sundays he is perfectly well. When she goes to the market he follows her from room to room, or stands at the window until she returns. He has asked her a dozen times what would happen "if you also go to hospital". He will not sleep in his own bed and makes his way into his parents' bed every night, and twice he has woken screaming from a dream in which the house was on fire and he could not find her. His teacher reports that at school he asks to phone home, and the school counsellor has started to use a phrase the mother had not heard before: separation anxiety.

Some anxiety about separation is a normal and healthy part of growing up. Around the first birthday infants begin to protest when a parent leaves and to be wary of strangers (stranger anxiety), and DSM-5-TR points out that these periods of heightened separation anxiety may actually indicate that a secure attachment relationship is developing (attachment theory is taught in the Developmental Psychology book). The fear normally fades through the preschool years as the child learns that the parent who leaves comes back. The disorder called separation anxiety disorder is the persistence or return of this fear beyond the age at which it is expected, and at a level that disrupts life: developmentally inappropriate and excessive fear or anxiety about separation from the people to whom the person is attached, the attachment figures.

DSM-5-TR asks for at least three of eight signs. They fall naturally into three groups, set out in Table 6.12: distress and worry (distress at separation, worry about harm coming to the attachment figure, worry about some event that would separate them), avoidance (refusing to go out or to school, fearing being alone, refusing to sleep away from the attachment figure), and the signs that appear at night and in the body (nightmares about separation and physical complaints). Kunal shows seven of the eight.

Table 6.12 The eight signs of separation anxiety disorder, grouped (at least three are needed).
GroupSignHow it shows in a childHow it shows in an adult
Distress and worry1. Recurrent excessive distress when separation from home or attachment figures is expected or happensCrying, clinging, tantrums at the school gateMarked discomfort when a partner or child travels
Distress and worry2. Persistent worry about losing attachment figures or about harm to them (illness, injury, disaster, death)"What if you die?"; needs to know where the parent isRepeated calls and texts to check that loved ones are safe
Distress and worry3. Persistent worry that an untoward event (getting lost, kidnapped, having an accident, falling ill) will cause separationFear of being kidnapped on the way to schoolFear of an accident that would keep them apart
Avoidance4. Reluctance or refusal to go out, away from home, to school, to work or elsewhere, because of fear of separationSchool refusalTurning down a job or a college place away from home
Avoidance5. Persistent fear of or reluctance about being alone or without attachment figures"Shadowing" the parent from room to roomCannot stay alone at home in the evening
Avoidance6. Reluctance or refusal to sleep away from home or without being near an attachment figureComes to the parents' bed at night; no sleepoversCannot sleep in a hotel or hostel
Night and body7. Repeated nightmares about separationDreams of fire or disaster destroying the familySimilar dreams
Night and body8. Repeated physical complaints when separation happens or is anticipatedHeadache, stomach ache, nausea, vomitingPalpitations, dizziness, feeling faint (rare in young children)

The remaining criteria set the threshold. Criterion B asks that the fear, anxiety or avoidance last at least 4 weeks in children and adolescents (under 18) and typically 6 months or more in adults, the adult figure being a general guide applied with some flexibility. The short window for children is deliberate: a child who misses a month of school is already being harmed, and waiting six months would cost a school year. Criterion C asks for clinically significant distress or impairment. Criterion D rules out look-alikes: refusing to leave home because of an excessive resistance to change in autism spectrum disorder; delusions or hallucinations about separation in a psychotic disorder; refusing to go out without a trusted companion in agoraphobia, where the fear is of being trapped or helpless if panic strikes rather than of being away from a loved one; worries about ill health or harm coming to others that are only part of a wider pattern in GAD; and concerns about one's own illness in illness anxiety disorder.

Two of the associated features are easy to mistake for something worse. Alone at night, young children with the disorder may report seeing people peering into their room or feeling eyes staring at them. These are not hallucinations: they are misperceptions of real stimuli, they occur only in particular situations such as the dark, and they vanish when the attachment figure is present. And when they are forced to separate, children may become angry or even aggressive towards the adult forcing them, which can look like oppositional defiance; DSM-5-TR advises diagnosing oppositional defiant disorder only when the defiance appears in situations that have nothing to do with separation.

The most common and most costly consequence in childhood is school refusal, a child's persistent difficulty in attending school because of emotional distress. School refusal is a behaviour, not a diagnosis, and the clinician's job is to find out what the child fears. Barlow and Durand draw the classic distinction. In what used to be called school phobia the fear is of something at school (a teacher, a bully, a test, the toilets), and the child can leave the parent quite happily to go anywhere other than school; in separation anxiety the act of leaving the parent is what provokes the fear, wherever the child is going. DSM-5-TR adds two more possibilities: school avoidance driven by fear of being judged negatively, which points to social anxiety disorder (Section 6.3), and truancy in conduct disorder, where anxiety is not the reason and the child stays away from home rather than running back to it. Kunal, whose stomach aches vanished every Sunday and whose fear centred on his mother's safety, was a clear case of separation anxiety.

Until DSM-5 the disorder lived among the "disorders usually first diagnosed in infancy, childhood, or adolescence", and DSM-IV required onset before age 18. Research forced a change. In an analysis of the National Comorbidity Survey Replication, M. Katherine Shear and her colleagues (2006) found that separation anxiety, if untreated, extended into adulthood in about 35 percent of cases, that about 6.6 percent of adults met the criteria at some point in their lives, and that in some people the disorder began in adulthood rather than continuing from childhood (reported by Barlow and Durand). The focus of the adult's anxiety is the same as the child's: that harm will befall loved ones during separation. Adults with the disorder are typically over-concerned about their children, spouses, parents and even pets, text or phone them throughout the day, may be unable to sleep in a hotel or to take a job that involves travel, and often reorganise their work to stay near their attachment figures. DSM-5 accordingly moved separation anxiety disorder into the anxiety disorders chapter and dropped the age limit, and it now stands first in that chapter, which is ordered by typical age at onset (Section 6.1).

DSM-5-TR estimates the 6- to 12-month prevalence in children at about 4 percent (Barlow and Durand cite 4.1 percent from Shear's group), 1.6 percent among adolescents in the United States, and 0.9 to 1.9 percent among adults in the United States, with a mean of 1.0 percent across 18 countries. The disorder becomes less common from childhood through adolescence into adulthood. Toddler boys and girls are equally affected, school-age girls more often in community samples, and among adults women more often. Onset may be as early as the preschool years; the median age at onset reported by adults is late adolescence in high- and upper-middle-income countries and the mid-twenties in low- and lower-middle-income countries. Most adults describe a fluctuating course, yet the majority of children with the disorder are free of impairing anxiety disorders over their lifetimes. The disorder often follows a life stress, especially a loss: the death of a relative or a pet, an illness in the family, a change of school, parental divorce, a move, migration, or a disaster that separated the family, as Kunal's two losses did. Being bullied is a risk factor, and so is a history of parental overprotection and intrusiveness. The disorder runs in families and appears to be strongly heritable, and children with it are unusually sensitive to air enriched with carbon dioxide, a finding that links it to panic disorder; both findings are explained in Section 6.7.

Indian context

DSM-5-TR warns that cultures differ in how much separation they expect, and that separation anxiety disorder must be distinguished from the high value some communities place on interdependence among family members. In much of India adult children live with their parents until marriage and often after it, and a daily phone call to one's mother is unremarkable. Proximity is not the test. A 23-year-old who lives at home by choice is not disordered; a 23-year-old who has turned down a hard-won postgraduate seat in another city because she cannot bear a night away from her mother, and who phones home twenty times a day from her office in dread that something has happened, may be.

The two classifications reflect the same history. ICD-10 placed "separation anxiety disorder of childhood" (F93.0) among the emotional disorders with onset specific to childhood, and DSM-5-TR still prints that code; ICD-11 (6B05) moved it, like DSM-5, into the anxiety or fear-related disorders and applies it across the lifespan. The psychological treatments, including cognitive behavioural programmes such as the Coping Cat and work with parents and schools on getting the child back into class, are taught in Book 2, Sections 10.3.2 and 10.4.2.

Exam trap

Separation anxiety disorder needs three of eight signs lasting at least 4 weeks in children and adolescents and typically 6 months or more in adults. Since DSM-5 it is an anxiety disorder (not a childhood disorder) and it can begin in adulthood; an option saying "onset must be before 18" describes DSM-IV. School refusal is not itself a diagnosis: fear of leaving the parent points to separation anxiety, fear of being judged at school to social anxiety, and staying away from both school and home to conduct disorder.

Selective mutism

A five-year-old girl in an upper kindergarten class in Bengaluru has not said a single word at school in the eight months since term began. She is not a silent child. At home she chatters in Kannada and English with her parents and her older sister, sings along to film songs and argues about bedtime. But from the moment she reaches the school gate she falls silent. She answers her teacher by nodding, shaking her head or pointing; she joins in the action songs without singing; she will draw a picture of her family but will not name them. She does not speak even to her grandparents when they visit, or to the cousin she plays with happily. Her teacher first thought she did not understand English, but she follows every instruction, and her parents, who speak both languages at home, find the suggestion puzzling. At the parent-teacher meeting her mother said what she had been told by relatives: "She is just shy, she will grow out of it."

She may, but what she has is selective mutism: a consistent failure to speak in specific social situations in which speech is expected, such as school, despite speaking in other situations, typically at home. The first description is usually credited to the German physician Adolf Kussmaul (1822 to 1902), who in 1877 called it aphasia voluntaria, voluntary loss of speech. In 1934 the Swiss child psychiatrist Moritz Tramer renamed it elective mutism, a term that, like Kussmaul's, implied that the child was choosing not to speak. ICD-10 kept that name (F94.0) and placed it among the "disorders of social functioning with onset specific to childhood and adolescence", describing a marked, emotionally determined selectivity in speaking. DSM-IV (1994) changed the word to "selective", to stress that the silence is tied to selected situations rather than chosen by the child, while still listing it among the childhood disorders. DSM-5 (2013) made the larger change of moving it into the anxiety disorders, and ICD-11 (6B06) did the same. The reason is the evidence that the silence is driven by anxiety, and above all social anxiety, rather than by defiance or a speech problem.

The DSM-5-TR criteria are short, and each has a reason. Criterion A, consistent failure to speak where speech is expected despite speaking elsewhere, is the definition itself: the child has the capacity to speak and uses it, but not in certain settings. Criterion B asks that the silence interfere with education, work or social communication; teachers cannot assess reading, the child cannot ask for help with an assignment or to use the toilet, and peers tease or ignore her. Criterion C asks for at least one month, and adds that this month must not be limited to the first month of school, because many young children are quiet and watchful when they first start school and then settle. Criterion D excludes silence that comes from a lack of knowledge of, or comfort with, the language spoken in that setting. Criterion E excludes silence better explained by a communication disorder (DSM-5-TR names childhood-onset fluency disorder, or stuttering) and silence that occurs only during autism spectrum disorder, schizophrenia or another psychotic disorder.

Children with selective mutism do not initiate speech or reply when spoken to, but many communicate in other ways: grunting, pointing, nodding, writing, or taking non-speaking parts in a school play. They are often excessively shy and afraid of social embarrassment, and may be clingy, negative, prone to tantrums or mildly oppositional, usually only in situations that demand speech. Their language skills are generally normal, though some have subtle receptive language difficulties within the normal range. In clinical settings they are almost always given an additional diagnosis of another anxiety disorder, most often social anxiety disorder. In one series of 50 children with selective mutism studied by Dummit and colleagues (1997), nearly all also met the criteria for social anxiety disorder (reported by Barlow and Durand). Separation anxiety disorder and specific phobia are the next most common companions, and in clinics selective mutism and autism are also often seen together.

Selective mutism is rare. Point prevalence in clinic and school samples in the United States, Europe and Israel ranges from 0.03 to 1.9 percent, depending on the setting and the ages sampled; Barlow and Durand put the average at about 0.5 percent of children, with girls more often affected, although DSM-5-TR notes that some studies find an equal sex ratio. Onset is usually before the age of 5, but the disorder often comes to attention only at school entry, when the demands to speak and perform (reading aloud, answering in class) suddenly multiply. The longitudinal course is not well known. Many children appear to "outgrow" the mutism, but the social anxiety beneath it often remains. The temperamental risk factors are not well identified, but behavioural inhibition and negative affectivity may play a role, as may a parental history of shyness and social anxiety; socially inhibited parents may model reticence, and parents of these children have been described as more overprotective or controlling than other parents. Barlow and Durand note evidence that well-meaning parents may maintain the silence by readily stepping in to "do their talking for them". There is also some evidence of unusual auditory efferent activity during speaking, which might make the child's own voice sound strange to her.

Children of families who have moved to a place where a different language is spoken run a higher risk, and this is where criterion D matters most. Many children pass through a silent period when they are learning a new language, listening and understanding before they dare to speak. That is not selective mutism. DSM-5-TR advises that the diagnosis may be warranted only if the child understands the new language adequately but still refuses to speak, in both languages, in several unfamiliar settings, and for a prolonged period. In India, where a child from a Bhojpuri-speaking home may start school in English or Kannada, this distinction is practical, not academic. Table 6.13 sets selective mutism beside the conditions it is most often confused with.

Table 6.13 Selective mutism and its look-alikes.
ConditionSpeech patternThe deciding clue
Selective mutismSpeaks fluently at home; consistently silent at school or with others, for at least 1 month (not just the first month of school)Capacity to speak is established in some settings; silence is driven by anxiety and causes impairment
Ordinary shynessQuiet or slow to warm up with strangers, but speaks when neededSpeech appears once the child settles; no lasting impairment
Silent period while learning a new languageSilent only in the new language; speaks the home language freelyLack of knowledge of or comfort with the language (excluded by criterion D)
Communication disorder (language disorder, speech sound disorder, stuttering, social communication disorder)Speech is impaired in every setting, including homeThe problem is not limited to particular social situations
Autism spectrum disorder, severe intellectual disability or psychosisSocial communication is impaired across settingsSelective mutism is not diagnosed if the silence occurs only within these conditions

Treatment draws on the same behavioural principles as the treatment of social anxiety in children, with the emphasis on speech: graded steps from whispering to one familiar person to speaking in class, with modelling, shaping and rewards (Book 2, Section 10.4.2). The girl in the Bengaluru classroom is not being stubborn, and she is not simply shy. She is anxious, and the longer adults speak for her, the longer her silence is likely to last.

Exam trap

Selective mutism requires at least 1 month, not limited to the first month of school. In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR it is an anxiety disorder, not a communication disorder and not a neurodevelopmental disorder; in DSM-IV it was a childhood disorder, and in ICD-10 it is "elective mutism" (F94.0). Its closest relative is social anxiety disorder, not autism and not defiance. Keep the three duration rules of this section apart: GAD 6 months; separation anxiety disorder 4 weeks in children and typically 6 months in adults; selective mutism 1 month.

6.5 Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder: The Clinical PictureHigh yield · 8 Qs

Anjali is 24, a commerce graduate who lives with her parents and her younger brother in Indore. Three years ago, a few weeks after a neighbour's child was admitted to hospital with jaundice, a vegetable seller handed her change with what looked like wet hands. As she took the notes a thought struck her with the force of a blow: "There are germs on this money, and I will give jaundice to my brother." She washed her hands with soap ten times before she felt easier. Within a year the circle of "dirty" things had widened to all currency notes, the newspaper, the doorbell, footwear, the outer gate and anything that came in from the street. Now, whenever she returns home, she bathes for an hour and washes her clothes separately; she washes her hands forty or fifty times a day, counting twenty strokes on each finger and starting again from the beginning if anyone interrupts her; and she asks her mother over and over, "Did I touch the door? Are you sure my hands are clean?" The family has rebuilt its life around her fear. Her father changes his clothes on the verandah, her mother washes the vegetables three times in salt water, and nobody may sit on Anjali's bed in clothes worn outside. Her hands are raw and cracked, and a dermatologist treating her for "contact dermatitis" was the first to suggest that she see a psychiatrist. She left her job at a chartered accountant's office because she could not touch the files. On a bad day the washing, bathing, checking and asking take five or six hours.

Sitting in the clinic, Anjali can almost laugh at it. "I know germs cannot jump from a note to my brother. Sitting here with you, I know it is nonsense. But once I have touched something, the feeling stays in my hands, like something sticky, and the thought keeps coming that if he falls ill it will be my fault. Washing is the only thing that makes it go away, for some time." In a household where bathing before entering the kitchen or the prayer room was part of daily life, the family at first took her washing for piety. Only when it began to swallow the day did they see it as an illness. Anjali has obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD). Barlow and Durand remark that contamination themes are highly prevalent in India, and as you will see later in this section, Indian clinical studies have repeatedly found fear of contamination to be the commonest obsession. This section teaches what OCD is, how it is diagnosed, how it varies and whom it strikes, and how to tell it from the many conditions that resemble it. Its causes are taught in Sections 6.7 and 6.8 and its treatment in Section 6.9.

From the madness of doubt to a chapter of its own

Doubting, washing and checking were recognised long before anyone called them a disorder, often as the scruples of the devout. The modern clinical history begins in France. In 1838 Jean-Étienne Dominique Esquirol (1772 to 1840) described a patient tormented by ideas she knew to be absurd but could not dismiss, and classed her condition as a monomania, a partial insanity in which reason is preserved outside a single area. Later French writers spoke of folie du doute, the "madness of doubt", and in 1875 Henri Legrand du Saulle drew the earlier descriptions together under the title of the madness of doubt "with delusion of touch": in one phrase, the two great forms of the disorder, doubting and checking on one hand, fear of contamination by touch on the other. In 1877 the German psychiatrist Carl Westphal (1833 to 1890), who had named agoraphobia six years earlier (Section 6.2.6), gave the classic definition of the Zwangsvorstellung, the "compelled idea" (the German word itself was already in use), and insisted that such ideas are not delusions: they force themselves into the mind of a person whose intelligence is intact and who recognises them as abnormal. According to a common account, British psychiatry translated the German term as "obsession" and American psychiatry as "compulsion", which is one reason the disorder came to carry both words. In 1903 Pierre Janet (1859 to 1947) published a large study of obsessions within a condition he called psychasthenia, a weakness of psychological energy, and gave a vivid account of the patients' tormenting "feeling of incompleteness", the sense that an act has not been done properly. You will meet that feeling again, under the name of "just right" experiences, in the modern criteria.

Sigmund Freud's "obsessional neurosis", and his case of the Rat Man (1909), gave the disorder a psychoanalytic explanation that dominated the first half of the twentieth century; it is taught, with the defence mechanisms of isolation, undoing and reaction formation, in Section 6.8. The labels in the manuals followed: an "obsessive compulsive reaction" in DSM-I (1952), obsessive compulsive neurosis in DSM-II (1968), and from DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV obsessive-compulsive disorder, listed among the anxiety disorders. ICD-10 placed it (F42) among the "neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders". DSM-5 (2013) gave OCD a chapter of its own, the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, shared with body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder, trichotillomania and excoriation disorder, and ICD-11 (6B20) did the same; the reasons for the move are given in Section 6.1 and the related disorders are taught in Section 6.6. DSM-5-TR codes OCD as F42.2.

Obsessions and compulsions defined

Criterion A requires obsessions, compulsions or both, and defines each with two parts. The definitions are worth learning almost word for word, because examiners build their distractors from small changes to them.

An obsession is defined by two features. First, it is a recurrent and persistent thought, urge or image that is experienced, at some time during the disturbance, as intrusive and unwanted, and that in most individuals causes marked anxiety or distress. Second, the person tries to ignore or suppress it, or to neutralise it with some other thought or action, that is, by performing a compulsion. Each phrase matters. "Thoughts, urges or images" covers the three forms the intrusion can take: a thought ("my hands are contaminated"), an image (a vivid picture of stabbing one's own child) or an urge (to shout an obscenity in a temple). "Intrusive and unwanted" means that the content arrives uninvited and is not welcome; DSM-5 chose "unwanted" in place of DSM-IV's "inappropriate", a word that implied a judgement which varies from culture to culture. "At some time during the disturbance" allows for people whose insight has faded: they may no longer see the thought as senseless, but at some point they experienced it as an intrusion. "In most individuals" allows for the minority who feel disgust or a nagging sense that something is incomplete rather than anxiety. And the second part, the attempt to ignore, suppress or neutralise, is the struggle that defines the experience: the obsession is fought.

A compulsion is also defined by two features. First, it is a repetitive behaviour (hand washing, ordering, checking) or mental act (praying, counting, repeating words silently) that the person feels driven to perform in response to an obsession or according to rules that must be applied rigidly. Second, the behaviour or mental act is aimed at preventing or reducing anxiety or distress, or at preventing some dreaded event or situation, yet it is either not connected in a realistic way with what it is meant to prevent or clearly excessive. Arranging the objects on a desk symmetrically to stop harm coming to one's mother is not realistically connected to her safety; showering for hours is clearly excessive. Barlow and Durand call compulsions "magical" for this reason: they may bear no logical relation to the obsession. Compulsions are often called rituals, because they must be done in a fixed way, a set number of times, in a set order, as Anjali's twenty strokes on each finger must be. DSM-5-TR adds a note that young children may not be able to say what their compulsions are for. Barlow and Durand describe an 8-year-old who each night had to undress, put on his pyjamas and turn down the covers in a slow, set sequence, three times over; he could give no reason, he simply had to.

Most people with OCD have both obsessions and compulsions, and the two are usually linked by theme: thoughts of contamination go with washing, thoughts of harm with checking. The mental compulsion deserves special attention because it is invisible. A person who seems to have "pure obsessions" may be silently counting, praying, replacing a bad image with a good one or reviewing the day's events to make sure nothing terrible was done; these covert rituals neutralise just as hand washing does, and the clinician will miss them unless he asks. Three other behaviours surround the rituals. People with OCD avoid the people, places and things that trigger their obsessions: Anjali no longer handles money, and someone with contamination fears may stop using public toilets or eating in restaurants. They seek reassurance, which works as a ritual performed by someone else ("Are you sure my hands are clean?"). And they draw their families in. DSM-5-TR calls this involvement of family or friends in the rituals accommodation (clinicians usually say family accommodation), and warns that it can worsen or maintain the symptoms and is an important target of treatment, especially in children. Anjali's father changing clothes on the verandah is accommodation; so is the mother who answers the same question for the fortieth time.

Not everything that drives a ritual is fear. DSM-5-TR notes that up to 60 percent of people with OCD report sensory phenomena: physical sensations, "just right" feelings and feelings of incompleteness that come before the compulsion and are relieved only when things look, feel or sound exactly right. This is Janet's feeling of incompleteness, rediscovered a century later. Others report strong disgust rather than anxiety, and many hold characteristic beliefs: an inflated sense of responsibility for preventing harm, a tendency to overestimate threat, perfectionism and intolerance of uncertainty, and the belief that having a thought is as bad as acting on it. These beliefs are the raw material of the cognitive theories of OCD (Paul Salkovskis on responsibility, Stanley Rachman on thought-action fusion), which are taught in Section 6.8.

One more point in the definition separates OCD from a whole family of "compulsive" behaviours. Compulsions are not done for pleasure. Performing one brings relief, a temporary drop in anxiety or distress, but never gratification. That is the difference between a compulsion and the acts of the impulse-control disorders such as kleptomania and pyromania (Chapter 11), the urges of the paraphilic disorders (Chapter 9), and so-called compulsive gambling, drinking or drug use (Chapter 10). In all of those, DSM-5-TR explains, the person usually enjoys the activity and wishes to resist it only because of its harmful consequences. ICD-10 made the same point in its own way: carrying out the act must not in itself be pleasurable, and simple relief of tension or anxiety does not count as pleasure.

How obsessions and compulsions are recognised and recorded as signs in the mental status examination, and how they differ from phobias, preoccupations and overvalued ideas as items of descriptive psychopathology, is taught in Chapter 2 and in Book 2, Section 2.5.5. This section is concerned with the disorder.

Criterion B: the cardinal feature

If intrusive thoughts and repeated acts were enough for the diagnosis, almost everyone would qualify. Most people have occasional unwanted thoughts that are bizarre, violent or sexual, especially when bored; most people have gone back to check a lock they knew they had locked. The studies of intrusive thoughts in ordinary people, beginning with Rachman and de Silva in 1978, are told in Section 6.8; Barlow and Durand report one community survey in which 13 percent of people had moderate obsessions or compulsions that fell short of the disorder (Spinella 2005). Criterion B is what separates the disorder from this background. The obsessions or compulsions must be time-consuming (DSM-5-TR's example is more than 1 hour a day) or cause clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational or other important areas of functioning. It is the cardinal feature of the diagnosis.

Notice the word "or". A person who spends only forty minutes a day on rituals but is tormented by them, or has had to give up a job, still qualifies; so does someone who spends three hours a day on rituals and claims not to mind. The hour is an example, not a threshold to be timed with a stopwatch. DSM-5-TR describes the range: people with mild to moderate OCD spend 1 to 3 hours a day obsessing or performing compulsions, while others have nearly constant intrusive thoughts or compulsions that can be incapacitating. Anjali's five or six hours, her lost job and her damaged skin put her far beyond the threshold.

Criterion C excludes symptoms that are the physiological effect of a substance or medication (stimulants can produce repetitive behaviour) or of another medical condition (DSM-5-TR has separate diagnoses for these, taught in Section 6.6, and the sudden childhood onset linked to infection, PANDAS, is taught in Section 6.7). Criterion D excludes symptoms better explained by another mental disorder, and its long list is the subject of Table 6.15 later in this section. Notice too what criterion A does not require. Unlike DSM-IV, DSM-5 no longer asks that the person recognise, at some point, that the obsessions or compulsions are excessive or unreasonable; that question is handled by the insight specifier instead.

The WHO classifications measure the threshold differently. ICD-10 asks that obsessional symptoms or compulsive acts, or both, be present on most days for at least two successive weeks and be a source of distress or interference, and it adds four tests of the symptoms: they must be recognised as the person's own thoughts or impulses; at least one must still be resisted, even if unsuccessfully; carrying out the act must not in itself be pleasurable; and the thoughts, images or impulses must be unpleasantly repetitive. ICD-11 (6B20) drops the fixed duration and, like DSM-5, asks that the obsessions or compulsions be time-consuming (for example, more than an hour a day) or result in significant distress or impairment.

Exam trap

The cardinal DSM-5 feature of OCD is obsessions and/or compulsions that are time-consuming (more than 1 hour a day) or cause clinically significant distress or impairment. Reject: "6 hours a day" (an invented figure; even mild cases spend 1 to 3 hours); "2 weeks of continuous symptoms" (DSM has no such rule; ICD-10 asks for symptoms on most days for 2 successive weeks, which is a different thing, and is not the DSM-5 answer); "only that obsessions are present" (presence alone is not enough, since nearly everyone has intrusive thoughts). In a cardinal-feature question, "persistent depressive symptoms" is persistent depressive disorder (Chapter 5), "a pervasive pattern of grandiosity and need for admiration" is narcissistic personality disorder (Chapter 11), and "recurrent unexpected panic attacks" is panic disorder (Section 6.2). A vignette of intrusive contamination thoughts with ritual hand washing for more than an hour a day is OCD, not GAD (realistic worries, no ritual), illness anxiety disorder (fear of having a disease, Chapter 7) or specific phobia (a circumscribed external object, avoided, with no ritual).

Ego-dystonic: at war with one's own mind

To understand how an obsession feels from the inside you need a pair of terms from psychoanalytic ego psychology that are now used throughout clinical psychology. Here "ego" means, roughly, a person's conscious sense of self: values, goals and self-image. Something is ego-syntonic (in tune with the ego) when it feels acceptable, consistent with who one is, perhaps even admirable. Something is ego-dystonic (out of tune with the ego) when it feels alien, unwanted and at odds with one's values and self-concept, something one wishes to be rid of.

Obsessions are the textbook example of ego-dystonic experience, and they are ego-dystonic in a precise and rather strange way. The person recognises the thought as a product of his or her own mind. This is what separates an obsession from thought insertion in schizophrenia, where the person believes that the thought has been placed in the mind by some outside agency (Chapter 4). Yet the same person experiences the thought as foreign, as if it came from somewhere he or she does not recognise. A devout man is tormented by blasphemous phrases during prayer; a gentle new mother is horrified by images of dropping her baby from the balcony; Anjali, who loves her brother more than anyone, cannot stop the thought that she will make him ill. Notice the pattern. Obsessions fasten onto exactly what a person values most, which is why they are so distressing: the horror a gentle mother feels at the image of harming her baby is itself the measure of how foreign that image is to her. Barlow and Durand describe a young and deeply moral woman who stopped riding buses for fear that she would grab the crotch of a man sitting beside her; it was, they write, the last thing she would ever do, and that is precisely why the urge horrified her.

Ego-dystonia should not be confused with insight, which is taught next. Ego-dystonia concerns how the person relates to the thought (unwanted, resisted, foreign to the self). Insight concerns whether the person believes that the feared consequence is real (will the house really burn down?). A man with poor insight who is fairly sure that disaster will follow an unchecked stove still hates the thought and the hours of checking. That is why an option describing someone "completely unaware that their obsessions are unreasonable" does not define ego-dystonia: it describes the far end of the insight scale.

Exam trap

Applied to obsessions, ego-dystonic means that the person experiences them as intrusive, unwanted and inconsistent with his or her self-concept. The distractors: experiencing the obsessions "as a core part of one's identity" is the opposite, ego-syntonic, which is how personality traits (including those of OCPD) are felt; "deriving pleasure from the thoughts" describes paraphilic fantasies or the gratification of impulse-control acts, never obsessions; "complete unawareness that the obsessions are unreasonable" is absent insight, a specifier, not ego-dystonia. In an OCD versus OCPD question, OCD obsessions and compulsions are ego-dystonic; they are not "always reality-grounded" (OCD content is often odd or magical, which is how DSM-5-TR tells it from realistic GAD worry), and OCD is not "restricted to childhood" (mean onset 19.5 years, and it is usually chronic).

The specifiers: insight and tics

Rohit, 32, is an officer in a nationalised bank in Pune. Every morning before leaving for work he checks the knob of the gas stove, the regulator on the cylinder, the taps, the geyser switch and the lock of the main door, each a set number of times; twice a week he gets off the bus two stops along and goes back to check again, and he arrives late. His obsession is a vivid image of the building on fire and his neighbours' children trapped, all because of him. In the clinic he says, a little embarrassed, that "probably nothing would happen" if he checked only once. At six in the morning, alone in the flat, he is far less sure.

DSM-IV asked that a person with OCD recognise, at some point, that the obsessions or compulsions were excessive or unreasonable, and offered a single "with poor insight" specifier. DSM-5 dropped the requirement and replaced it with a three-level insight specifier that records how far the person believes the beliefs behind the symptoms. DSM-5-TR illustrates it with a person who checks the stove 30 times (Figure 6.8). With good or fair insight, the person recognises that the OCD beliefs are definitely or probably not true, or that they may or may not be true: the house definitely will not, probably will not, or may or may not burn down if the stove is not checked 30 times. With poor insight, the person thinks the beliefs are probably true: the house will probably burn down. With absent insight/delusional beliefs, the person is completely convinced: the house will burn down. Many people have good or fair insight, some have poor insight, and a few, 4 percent or fewer, have absent insight. Insight can change within the same person over the course of the illness, and even, as Rohit shows, over the course of a day; and poorer insight has been linked to a worse long-term outcome. (The general grading of insight in a mental status examination is taught in Book 2, Section 2.6.9.)

The far right of the figure raises an obvious question. If a person is completely convinced that the house will burn down unless the stove is checked 30 times, is that not a delusion, and should the diagnosis not be a psychotic disorder? DSM-5-TR answers no. The person still has obsessions and compulsions, which is what distinguishes the condition from delusional disorder, and has none of the other features of schizophrenia, such as hallucinations or disorganised speech. People who warrant the "absent insight/delusional beliefs" specifier are diagnosed with OCD, not with a psychotic disorder. An additional psychotic diagnosis is given only if the person holds delusions whose content goes beyond the OCD (DSM-5-TR's example, from the related disorders, is a person with body dysmorphic disorder who is also convinced that his food has been poisoned). The same three-level specifier is used for body dysmorphic disorder and hoarding disorder (Section 6.6). ICD-11 uses a two-level version: "with fair to good insight" and "with poor to absent insight".

The second specifier is tic-related: the person has a current or past history of a tic disorder. Up to 30 percent of people with OCD have a lifetime tic disorder, most often men whose OCD began in childhood. The specifier exists because this group differs from other people with OCD in the themes of its symptoms (Barlow and Durand note that the obsessions in tic-related OCD are almost always about symmetry), its comorbidity, its course and its pattern of family transmission. In children a triad of OCD, a tic disorder and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder may be seen (tic disorders and Tourette's disorder are taught in Chapter 3). Tics and compulsions can look alike, so learn DSM-5-TR's distinction. A tic is a sudden, rapid, recurrent, non-rhythmic movement or vocalisation, such as eye blinking or throat clearing, often preceded by a premonitory sensory urge; a compulsion is usually more complex, is preceded by an obsession, and is aimed at neutralising it. Compulsions are voluntary acts, although the person feels driven to perform them, which is why "involuntary" is the wrong word for them.

Exam trap

OCD with absent insight/delusional beliefs is still diagnosed as OCD, not as delusional disorder or schizophrenia. The insight specifier has three levels in DSM-5-TR (good or fair; poor; absent/delusional), and the second specifier is tic-related. "With poor insight" was the only insight specifier in DSM-IV, and DSM-IV, unlike DSM-5, required that the person at some point recognise the symptoms as excessive or unreasonable.

The symptom dimensions, and why hoarding is no longer one of them

The content of obsessions varies endlessly from one person to the next, but it clusters. DSM-5-TR names four common symptom dimensions, each pairing a kind of obsession with a kind of compulsion (Figure 6.9). In the cleaning dimension, obsessions about contamination by germs, dirt, bodily fluids or "impure" things lead to washing and cleaning, as in Anjali's case. In the symmetry dimension, a need for things to be even, exact or "just right" leads to ordering, arranging, repeating and counting, and to touching or tapping objects a set number of times or evenly on both sides; the childhood game of not stepping on the cracks in the pavement is a harmless version of it, and the person with OCD may spend an hour lining up the objects on a table. The forbidden or taboo thoughts dimension includes aggressive, sexual and religious obsessions with related compulsions; religious obsessions about sin, blasphemy or ritual impurity, with repeated confession, prayer or purification, are often called scrupulosity. In the harm dimension, fears of harm to oneself or others lead to checking, as in Rohit's case. Statistical analyses of large samples (Bloch and colleagues 2008, reported by Barlow and Durand) found symmetry to be the largest factor, followed by forbidden thoughts, cleaning and hoarding.

Three further facts about the dimensions are examined. First, most people have symptoms in more than one dimension; Anjali, for instance, also counts twenty strokes on each finger, a ritual more typical of the symmetry dimension. Second, the dimensions occur across cultures, are fairly stable over time in adults, and may involve different neural substrates. Third, their content tracks development and sex: harm obsessions (fears of death or illness striking oneself or loved ones) are commoner in children and adolescents than in adults, sexual and religious obsessions commoner in adolescents than in children, and women are more likely to have cleaning symptoms, men forbidden-thoughts and symmetry symptoms.

What about hoarding? For decades it was treated as a form of OCD, and statistical studies like Bloch's still found it as a separate factor. DSM-IV listed hoarding as a criterion of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder and, at the same time, told clinicians to consider OCD when hoarding was extreme, so it was handled as a possible symptom of OCD as well; and DSM-5-TR's OCPD criteria still include being "unable to discard worn-out or worthless objects even when they have no sentimental value". But hoarding turned out to differ from OCD in important ways. Only about one in five people with hoarding disorder also meets the criteria for OCD (DSM-5-TR); the saving is usually purposeful and felt as reasonable rather than intrusive and unwanted; and whereas OCD waxes and wanes, hoarding tends to begin early and worsen with each passing decade (Barlow and Durand). So DSM-5 made hoarding disorder a separate diagnosis within the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (taught in Section 6.6). DSM-5-TR draws the line clearly. If a person keeps things because of a typical obsession (acquiring every object in a set to achieve a sense of completeness, or refusing to throw away old newspapers because they may contain information that could prevent a disaster), that accumulation is a compulsion and the diagnosis is OCD. Primary hoarding, the persistent difficulty in discarding possessions because of a perceived need to save them and distress at parting with them, is hoarding disorder.

Exam trap

Asked which is not a compulsive behaviour of OCD, choose hoarding. Tapping (a symmetry or "just right" ritual), washing (cleaning dimension) and checking (harm dimension) are textbook compulsions. Hoarding is now its own disorder in DSM-5, so it is not a compulsion "in this classic framing", which is how the question puts it. The nuance, if an interviewer pushes: DSM-IV listed hoarding as an OCPD criterion and advised considering OCD when it was extreme, OCPD still lists the inability to discard worthless objects, and saving that is driven by a true obsession is still diagnosed as OCD.

Who develops OCD, and what course it takes

DSM-5-TR gives a 12-month prevalence of 1.2 percent in the United States and similar figures internationally, 1.1 to 1.8 percent in countries as different as Canada, Germany, Taiwan, Korea and New Zealand; Barlow and Durand give lifetime estimates of 1.6 to 2.3 percent. These are the figures behind the textbook statement that OCD affects "1 to 2 percent" of people. Unlike most anxiety disorders, which affect women about twice as often as men, OCD is nearly evenly balanced: women are affected slightly more often in adulthood, but boys more often in childhood, because boys start earlier.

The mean age at onset in the United States is 19.5 years (Barlow and Durand give a median of 19), and 25 percent of cases begin by age 14. Onset after 35 is unusual but does occur. Men begin earlier: nearly a quarter of men have their onset before age 10, whereas in girls onset is more typical in adolescence. Onset is usually gradual, although it can be acute (a sudden onset in childhood raises the question of an infection-related syndrome, Section 6.7). Untreated, the course is usually chronic, with symptoms that wax and wane; some people have an episodic course and a minority deteriorate. Remission without treatment is rare in adults: in one long follow-up only about 20 percent had remitted when they were re-examined 40 years later. Childhood onset can lead to a lifetime of OCD, yet about 40 percent of people whose OCD began in childhood or adolescence may remit by early adulthood. Two periods in a woman's life deserve mention. OCD can begin or worsen around childbirth, the peripartum period, often with aggressive obsessions such as intrusive thoughts of harming the infant that lead the mother to avoid the baby; and some women find that their symptoms worsen before menstruation.

Key study: what Indian patients obsess about (1975 and 1988)

In 1975 Salman Akhtar, Narendra Nath Wig and their colleagues published, in the British Journal of Psychiatry, a phenomenological analysis of 82 patients with obsessional neurosis seen at the Postgraduate Institute of Medical Education and Research, Chandigarh. They sorted the forms of obsession into doubts, obsessive thinking, fears, impulses and images, in that order of frequency, and found that the commonest content concerned dirt and contamination, followed by aggression, impersonal themes, religion and sex; they stressed that sociocultural factors shape what an obsession is about. A quarter of their patients had no compulsions, and those patients had the best outcome. Thirteen years later, S. Khanna and S. M. Channabasavanna at NIMHANS, Bengaluru, analysed 761 obsessions recorded from 410 patients over ten years and again found fear of contamination to be the single commonest theme. Both studies bear out Barlow and Durand's remark that contamination themes are highly prevalent in India.

Culture shapes the content of OCD more than its structure. Barlow and Durand note that in Saudi Arabia and Egypt obsessions are mainly about religious practice, especially the Muslim emphasis on cleanliness, and that contamination themes are highly prevalent in India, yet studies from England, Hong Kong, India, Egypt, Japan, Norway and many other countries have found essentially similar types and proportions of obsessions and compulsions. DSM-5-TR agrees: the sex ratio, age at onset, comorbidity and symptom structure are similar around the world, while sexual obsessions may be reported less in some religious groups and violent obsessions more where urban violence is common. People also explain their symptoms differently, as physical, social, spiritual or supernatural in origin, and these explanations can shape both the rituals and the help that is sought. Anjali's family, who first read her bathing as devotion, is a small example; in India, as elsewhere, some patients first take their symptoms to religious or traditional healers.

OCD is seldom alone. In the United States 76 percent of adults with OCD have had an anxiety disorder in their lifetime and 63 percent a depressive or bipolar disorder (41 percent major depression); impulse-control disorders (56 percent) and substance use disorders (39 percent) are also common. OCD usually begins later than most of the anxiety disorders that accompany it, but often comes before the depression. In one study of 214 treatment-seeking adults, 23 to 32 percent also had obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. Body dysmorphic disorder, trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are commoner than expected in people with OCD, and OCD is in turn much commoner than expected among people with schizophrenia or schizoaffective disorder (about 12 percent), bipolar disorder, eating disorders, body dysmorphic disorder and Tourette's disorder, which is why a clinician who diagnoses any of these should ask about obsessions.

The burden is heavy. Barlow and Durand call OCD "the devastating culmination of the anxiety disorders": among people with anxiety and related disorders, the one who needs admission is likely to have OCD, and the one referred for neurosurgery after every other treatment has failed probably does (Section 6.9). Quality of life is reduced across every domain. Harm obsessions can make relationships feel dangerous, symmetry obsessions can stop a student finishing an examination because the answer never looks right, contamination fears can keep a person away from hospitals or produce skin damage like Anjali's, and medicines themselves may be seen as contaminated. Some people impose rules on the whole family (no visitors, no outdoor shoes), and young people whose OCD began early may never achieve independence from their parents. Suicide is a real risk. DSM-5-TR reports a mean lifetime suicide attempt rate of 14.2 percent in clinical samples from many countries, lifetime suicidal ideation in 44.1 percent, and, in a Swedish national registry study of 36,788 people with OCD, a much raised risk of death by suicide (odds ratio 9.8) and of suicide attempt (odds ratio 5.5), even after other psychiatric disorders were taken into account. Severe OCD, the unacceptable-thoughts dimension and comorbid depression are among the predictors.

Clinical link

Because OCD is secret and shameful, people often wait years before telling anyone, and some first appear in a dermatology clinic with skin damaged by washing, in a general practice asking for "something for tension", or with a relative who complains about the hours spent in the bathroom. Ask directly and without embarrassment: "Some people get thoughts that they find upsetting and that keep coming back even though they try to push them away. Does that happen to you? Do you have to do anything again and again to feel all right?" Ask too about family accommodation, which treatment must address. The treatments of choice are exposure and response prevention (Book 2, Section 4.3.5), cognitive therapy built on Salkovskis' model (Book 2, Section 5.6.7) and the serotonergic drugs (Section 6.9).

Obsession, worry, rumination or delusion? The differential diagnosis

Repetitive thoughts and repetitive acts occur in many disorders, and much of the skill in diagnosing OCD lies in asking what kind of repetitive thought one is looking at. Table 6.14 compares the obsession with its six chief look-alikes. Three distinctions are examined most often. A GAD worry is usually about a real-life concern (money, health, the family), and what is abnormal is its excess; an obsession usually does not concern real-life problems, may be odd, irrational or magical in content, and is usually linked to a compulsion. Depressive rumination is mood-congruent brooding on loss, failure and guilt, not necessarily experienced as intrusive and not linked to compulsions. A delusion is a false, fixed belief that the person holds as simply true and does not resist (Chapter 4), whereas an obsession is resisted and usually recognised as excessive. Between the two lies the overvalued idea, a dominating, emotionally charged belief that the person holds with strong conviction and does not experience as senseless, such as the conviction of a disfigured nose in body dysmorphic disorder or of being fat in anorexia nervosa (Chapter 2 teaches the concept).

Table 6.14 The obsession and its look-alikes.
ExperienceTypical contentHow the person experiences itRitual?Where taught
Obsession (OCD)Contamination, harm, symmetry, taboo thoughts; often odd or magicalIntrusive, unwanted, ego-dystonic; resisted; usually seen as excessiveYes: a compulsion neutralises it6.5
Worry (GAD)Realistic everyday future problemsExcessive and hard to control, but about real matters; not felt as alienNo6.4
Depressive ruminationPast loss, failure, guilt; mood-congruentBrooding in low mood; not necessarily felt as intrusiveNoChapter 5
DelusionFalse, fixed belief (persecution, jealousy, control)Held as simply true; not resistedNo neutralising ritualChapter 4
Overvalued ideaOne dominating belief (a disfigured nose, being fat)Strong conviction, emotionally charged, felt as reasonableRelated acts (mirror checking, dieting)Chapter 2; 6.6; Chapter 8
Intrusive trauma memoryA real past traumatic event, relivedUnwanted and distressing, but a memory, not a fear of the futureAvoidance of remindersChapter 7
Restricted interest (autism)An intense special topic or routinePleasurable and sought out; ego-syntonicRoutines, not to neutralise anxietyChapter 3

Criterion D of the DSM-5-TR criteria then lists the other mental disorders whose symptoms can be mistaken for obsessions or compulsions, and its list is long because repetition is everywhere in psychopathology. Table 6.15 groups them. The common thread is simple: in OCD the repetition is driven by an intrusive, unwanted obsession and serves to neutralise it, whereas in each look-alike the repeated thought or act either has a narrower focus (appearance, possessions, hair, food, illness), brings pleasure (gambling, substances, paraphilic urges, impulsive acts), carries no neutralising purpose (tics, stereotypies, autistic routines), or belongs to another state of mind altogether (the guilt of depression, the thought insertion of psychosis). When both conditions are present, both may be diagnosed: OCD and a tic disorder, for example, often coexist.

Difficulty discarding possessionsHoarding disorderPrimary saving and distress at discarding, not driven by an obsession (6.6)
Hair pulling; skin pickingTrichotillomania; excoriation disorderBody-focused acts not triggered by obsessions (6.6)
Stereotypies; ticsStereotypic movement disorder; tic disordersSimpler movements, not aimed at neutralising; tics follow sensory urges (Chapter 3)
