OCD and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder

The most confusable pair in this whole area shares most of a name. Rohit's branch manager, a man in his fifties, is admired for his thoroughness and disliked for almost everything else. He insists that every voucher be checked three times in his own fixed order, rewrites his juniors' reports until they miss their deadlines, will not delegate anything important, keeps every circular since 1998 in labelled files because "you never know", and has not taken his earned leave in years. His family complains that holidays with him feel like inspections. Asked whether any of this troubles him, he is puzzled: "Somebody in this branch has to do things properly." He has no intrusive thoughts that he fights, no rituals that relieve anxiety, and no wish to change. If he has a disorder, it is obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (OCPD).

Despite the similar names, DSM-5-TR says, the two disorders are usually easy to tell apart: OCD is defined by true obsessions and compulsions, and OCPD is not. OCPD is an enduring and pervasive pattern, beginning by early adulthood and present across many contexts, of preoccupation with orderliness, perfectionism and mental and interpersonal control, at the expense of flexibility, openness and efficiency; its criteria (preoccupation with details and rules, perfectionism that prevents completion, devotion to work over leisure, rigid scrupulousness about morals, inability to discard worthless objects, reluctance to delegate, miserliness, rigidity and stubbornness) are taught in Chapter 11. The decisive difference is how the person experiences the symptoms. OCD is ego-dystonic: Rohit hates his checking and wants to stop. OCPD is ego-syntonic: the manager's perfectionism feels to him like the right and rational way to live, and his rigidity often distresses the people around him more than it distresses him. Table 6.16 sets the two side by side. The two are separate categories in DSM-5-TR, one among the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders and one among the personality disorders, and they can coexist: when the criteria for both are met, both diagnoses are recorded.

Table 6.16 Obsessive-compulsive disorder and obsessive-compulsive personality disorder compared.
FeatureOCDOCPD
Where classifiedObsessive-compulsive and related disorders (F42.2; ICD-11 6B20)Personality disorders, Cluster C (F60.5); Chapter 11
Core featureTrue obsessions and compulsionsPervasive perfectionism, orderliness and control, without true obsessions or compulsions
How it feels to the personEgo-dystonic: intrusive, unwanted, resistedEgo-syntonic: felt as right, rational, the proper way
Who is most distressedUsually the person, by the thoughts and ritualsOften others: family and colleagues frustrated by rigidity and delays
Repetitive behaviourRituals that neutralise an obsession and bring reliefThoroughness, rule following and rechecking for mistakes, not anxiety-relieving rituals
Saving objectsOnly when driven by an obsessionA criterion: unable to discard worn-out or worthless objects
Onset and courseUsually childhood to early adulthood; waxing and waningBy early adulthood; a stable trait pattern across contexts
PrevalenceAbout 1.2 percent in a yearAbout 2 to 8 percent in surveys (median 4.7 percent)
Together?Can coexist; 23 to 32 percent of treatment-seeking adults with OCD had OCPDBoth diagnoses are recorded when both are met
Exam trap

The feature that best distinguishes OCD from OCPD: OCD involves ego-dystonic intrusive obsessions and compulsions; OCPD is an ego-syntonic, pervasive pattern of perfectionism and control without true obsessions or compulsions. The distractors reverse this ("OCPD involves intrusive obsessions and rituals; OCD does not"), merge the two ("they are the same disorder": they are separate DSM-5 categories that can coexist), or invent a symptom ("OCPD always includes hand-washing rituals": OCPD includes no rituals at all). Remember the direction with a single image: in OCD the person is at war with the symptom; in OCPD the person is at peace with it and the family is at war with the person.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. In DSM-5-TR, generalised anxiety disorder requires excessive anxiety and worry, more days than not, for at least in adults.
  2. Adults with GAD need three of six associated symptoms, but children need only .
  3. Because they show smaller heart rate, skin conductance and breathing responses to stress than people with panic, but chronic muscle tension, people with GAD have been called autonomic .
  4. ICD-11 asks that the symptoms of GAD persist for at least , more days than not.
  5. Separation anxiety disorder must last at least in children and adolescents, and typically 6 months or more in adults.
  6. Selective mutism must last at least 1 month, not limited to the first month of ; its closest companion disorder is social anxiety disorder.
  7. The cardinal feature of OCD is obsessions or compulsions that are time-consuming, for example more than a day, or that cause clinically significant distress or impairment.
  8. Obsessions are experienced as intrusive, unwanted and inconsistent with the self-concept; in other words they are ego-.
  9. A person with OCD who is completely convinced that the house will burn down unless the stove is checked 30 times receives the specifier "with absent insight/ beliefs" and is still diagnosed with OCD.
  10. In DSM-5, is no longer an OCD compulsion but a separate disorder, although obsessive-compulsive personality disorder still lists the inability to discard worthless objects.

Answers: (1) 6 months, (2) one, (3) restrictors, (4) several months, (5) 4 weeks, (6) school, (7) 1 hour, (8) dystonic, (9) delusional, (10) hoarding.

6.6 Body Dysmorphic Disorder, Hoarding and the Body-Focused Repetitive BehavioursMedium yield · 3 Qs

Picture four people in four Indian cities. A college student in Delhi spends three hours a day examining her nose in every mirror, shop window and phone screen she passes. A retired clerk in Kolkata can no longer reach his bed, because the room is stacked to the ceiling with forty years of newspapers. A schoolgirl in Pune sits over her board-examination notes pulling out her eyebrows one hair at a time, until there are none left to pull. A nursing student in Bengaluru picks at a pimple on her cheek until it bleeds, then at the scab, then at the skin around it, until the small spot has become a scar. On the surface these four people have little in common: a worry about looks, a flat full of paper, a missing eyebrow, a wound. Yet when the American Psychiatric Association published DSM-5 in 2013 it placed all four problems in a single chapter, next to obsessive-compulsive disorder (OCD), and the World Health Organization's ICD-11 did much the same a few years later. Why should a worry about one's nose belong with a habit of pulling hair, and why should either belong with OCD? This section answers that question first and then takes the members of the family one at a time.

A new family: why these disorders sit beside OCD

Before 2013 the disorders in this section were scattered across the manual like relatives who had never met. OCD itself sat among the anxiety disorders from DSM-III (1980) to DSM-IV-TR, and in ICD-10 among the "neurotic, stress-related and somatoform disorders" (F40 to F48). Body dysmorphic disorder, the preoccupation with an imagined flaw in one's looks, had entered the American manual in 1980 only as an example of an atypical somatoform disorder, received its present name in DSM-III-R (1987), and was still listed among the somatoform disorders in DSM-IV, because its central feature is a preoccupation with the body. ICD-10 filed it under hypochondriacal disorder (F45.2), which is why DSM-5-TR, which prints ICD-10-CM codes, still gives body dysmorphic disorder the code F45.22: the number is a fossil of its old home. Hair pulling was one of DSM-IV's "impulse-control disorders not elsewhere classified", alongside kleptomania, pyromania, pathological gambling and intermittent explosive disorder, and in ICD-10 it sat with the "habit and impulse disorders" as F63.3, a code DSM-5-TR still uses. Hoarding had no category of its own at all: it was treated as a symptom of OCD and was one of the eight criteria for obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (an inability to discard worn-out or worthless objects even when they have no sentimental value, a criterion that survives in DSM-5-TR). Skin picking had no category either; excoriation disorder is new in DSM-5.

DSM-5 gathered these strays into a new chapter, the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (OCRDs). In DSM-5-TR it contains OCD; body dysmorphic disorder; hoarding disorder; trichotillomania (hair-pulling disorder); excoriation (skin-picking) disorder; substance/medication-induced obsessive-compulsive and related disorder; obsessive-compulsive and related disorder due to another medical condition; other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder; and unspecified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder. The chapter is placed immediately after the anxiety disorders, and DSM-5-TR says the sequence is deliberate: OCD remains closely related to anxiety, even though it no longer lives in the same chapter.

What binds the family? DSM-5-TR answers in two parts. The first is a shared form of symptom: repetitive thoughts or behaviours. Some members (OCD, body dysmorphic disorder and hoarding disorder) are driven by a preoccupation, an obsession about contamination, a belief that one's nose is grotesque, a felt need to save things, and the person answers that preoccupation with repetitive behaviours or mental acts. Others (trichotillomania and excoriation disorder) are driven not by a thought but by an urge located in the body: recurrent body-focused repetitive behaviours (BFRBs), such as hair pulling and skin picking, together with repeated attempts to cut down or stop. The second part of the answer is evidence on what DSM calls diagnostic validators. The disorders occur together far more often than chance would predict: body dysmorphic disorder, trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are all commoner in people with OCD than in people without it, and trichotillomania and excoriation are commoner in the first-degree relatives of people with OCD. They tend to begin in childhood or adolescence and to run a chronic course. Several respond to the same treatments, drugs that block the reuptake of serotonin and therapies built on exposure (Section 6.9), and they appear to involve overlapping genes and overlapping brain circuits (Section 6.7). Grouping them has a practical point too: DSM-5-TR tells clinicians that a person who presents with one of these conditions should be screened for the others.

The grouping is not a claim that the disorders are identical. DSM-5-TR is careful to say that there are important differences in validators and treatment across the chapter, and Barlow and Durand give examples: habit reversal, not exposure and response prevention, is the main psychological treatment for hair pulling and skin picking, and the treatments developed for hoarding have so far produced more modest results than those for OCD. Nor does every repetitive habit qualify. The chapter's disorders differ from normal preoccupations and rituals by being excessive or by persisting beyond the developmentally appropriate period. A four-year-old who insists that her bedtime story be read in exactly the same way every night, or a ten-year-old who collects every cricket card in a series, is behaving normally; the same rigidity at thirty, consuming hours and wrecking a marriage, is not. Figure 6.10 maps the family, and Table 6.17 sets its members side by side.

Table 6.17 The obsessive-compulsive and related disorders of DSM-5-TR at a glance (figures from DSM-5-TR).
DisorderCore feature and repetitive behaviourInsight specifierPrevalence and onsetOld home
Obsessive-compulsive disorderObsessions and/or compulsions (washing, checking, ordering, counting, praying)Yes12-month 1.2 percent (USA); mean onset 19.5 yearsAnxiety disorders (DSM-III to DSM-IV)
Body dysmorphic disorderPerceived flaw in appearance; mirror checking, grooming, reassurance seeking, comparingYes (plus muscle dysmorphia)Point 2.4 percent (USA); mean onset 16 to 17Somatoform disorders (DSM-IV); hypochondriacal disorder (ICD-10)
Hoarding disorderDifficulty discarding, clutter; excessive acquisition in mostYes1.5 to 6 percent; begins 15 to 19, worsens with ageAn OCD symptom; an OCPD criterion
TrichotillomaniaPulling out one's hair, with hair loss and repeated attempts to stopNo12-month 1 to 2 percent; onset around pubertyImpulse-control disorders (DSM-IV); habit and impulse disorders (ICD-10)
Excoriation disorderPicking one's skin, with lesions and repeated attempts to stopNo2.1 percent current, 3.1 percent lifetime; adolescenceNone: new in DSM-5
Substance/medication-induced OCRDOCRD symptoms during intoxication, withdrawal or medication useNoVery rareNew grouping in DSM-5
OCRD due to another medical conditionOCRD symptoms as the direct result of an illness, such as Sydenham's choreaNoNot establishedNew grouping in DSM-5

One more feature is shared by three members of the family. The disorders that have a cognitive core, OCD, body dysmorphic disorder and hoarding disorder, carry an insight specifier with three grades: good or fair insight, poor insight, and absent insight/delusional beliefs. Section 6.5 explains the specifier for OCD. The rule that matters here is that a person whose disorder-related belief is held with complete conviction is not given a second diagnosis of a psychotic disorder. A woman who is utterly certain that her nose is deformed has body dysmorphic disorder with absent insight, not delusional disorder. Only if her delusions spread beyond the disorder's own theme (DSM-5-TR's example is a person with body dysmorphic disorder who becomes convinced that her food is being poisoned) does a psychotic diagnosis come into play.

Exam trap

Know each disorder's old home, because papers set it as the wrong option. OCD was an anxiety disorder until DSM-5. Body dysmorphic disorder was a somatoform disorder in DSM-IV (and a form of hypochondriacal disorder in ICD-10). Trichotillomania was an impulse-control disorder in DSM-IV. Hoarding was a symptom of OCD and a criterion of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder. Excoriation disorder had no earlier category. In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR all five are obsessive-compulsive and related disorders.

Body dysmorphic disorder: the distress of imagined ugliness

Zoya is nineteen and in her second year of a BA course in Delhi. She is, by any ordinary standard, a good-looking young woman. Zoya does not see it that way. For three years she has been convinced that her nose bends to the left, that its tip is "fat and ugly", and that the skin on her cheeks is dark and covered with pits. She checks her face in every reflecting surface she passes: mirrors, car windows, the back of a steel spoon, the front camera of her phone, which she opens perhaps fifty times a day. Before leaving home she spends an hour and a half applying and reapplying foundation, and she will not step out without it. Several times an hour she asks her mother, "Does my nose look crooked today?" Her mother says no; Zoya feels better for a few minutes and then asks again. On the metro she is sure that other passengers are glancing at her nose and whispering about it. She compares her face, feature by feature, with her classmates' and with the women on her phone, and she always loses. She has seen three dermatologists for what one of them called "mild acne", and she has begun to save money for an operation on her nose. Since the start of the term she has stopped going to college. Her family, at first amused and then exasperated, tells her that she is vain. She is not vain. She is miserable.

Zoya has body dysmorphic disorder (BDD): a preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that other people either cannot see or regard as slight. The condition was described in 1891 by the Italian psychiatrist Enrico Morselli (1852 to 1929), who called it dysmorphophobia (some sources date his first description to 1886). The word comes from Greek roots meaning, roughly, fear of misshapenness, and Barlow and Durand gloss it simply as "fear of ugliness". The name is misleading in one important way. A person with BDD is not afraid of becoming ugly, the way a person with a phobia is afraid of meeting a dog; she is convinced that she already is ugly. Because so many sufferers cannot accept, even for a moment, that their belief might be mistaken, the condition was for decades thought to be a psychotic delusional state. The modern understanding owes most to the American psychiatrist Katharine A. Phillips, whose 1991 paper described the disorder as the distress of "imagined ugliness" and whose research since then has shaped the DSM criteria. DSM-5-TR notes that the disorder was "formerly known as dysmorphophobia".

The DSM-5-TR criteria have four parts, and each is there for a reason. First, there must be a preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. The word "preoccupation" means much more than dissatisfaction: the thoughts are intrusive, unwanted and hard to resist or control, and on average they occupy three to eight hours a day. The phrase "not observable or appear slight to others" is the heart of the diagnosis. A person with an obvious facial scar who worries about it does not have BDD; DSM-5-TR says explicitly that clearly noticeable defects are not diagnosed as BDD (an excessive, impairing preoccupation with a real and noticeable flaw is recorded as an "other specified" disorder, Section 6.6.6). Second, at some point during the disorder the person must have performed repetitive behaviours or mental acts in response to the appearance concerns. This criterion, added in DSM-5, is what makes BDD look like OCD: the mirror checking, grooming and reassurance seeking play the part that washing and checking play in OCD, and comparing one's appearance with other people's is the mental act that corresponds to a silent counting ritual. Third, the preoccupation must cause clinically significant distress or impairment; in practice, DSM-5-TR observes, usually both are present. Fourth, the preoccupation must not be better explained by concerns about body fat or weight in a person who meets criteria for an eating disorder. This last rule draws the border with anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa (Chapter 8): a young woman who believes she is fat and starves herself has an eating disorder, not BDD, although the two can coexist and then both are diagnosed.

Any part of the body can become the focus, but the commonest targets are the skin (perceived acne, scars, lines, wrinkles, paleness), the hair (thinning hair, or too much body or facial hair) and the nose. In Phillips and colleagues' series of 200 patients (2005), 80 percent were concerned about their skin, 58 percent about their hair and 39 percent about their nose, and the average person was worried about five to seven body areas, not one. Some people are troubled by a perceived asymmetry, as Zoya is by the "bend" in her nose. The concerns range in intensity from looking "unattractive" or "not right" to looking "hideous" or "like a monster".

The repetitive behaviours are varied, and DSM-5-TR's list repays careful reading because examiners draw on it. They include comparing one's appearance with that of others; repeatedly checking the perceived defect in mirrors and other reflecting surfaces, or examining it directly (some people do the opposite and avoid mirrors almost phobically); taking excessive "selfies"; excessive grooming (combing, styling, shaving, plucking or pulling hair); seeking reassurance; touching the disliked area to check it; excessive exercise or weightlifting; seeking cosmetic procedures; excessive tanning; repeatedly changing clothes; compulsive shopping for beauty products; and skin picking intended to improve the skin, which can itself cause damage, infection and scarring. Especially common is camouflaging: hiding the defect with make-up, a cap, a scarf, a hand held in front of the face, or hair arranged over the forehead. Notice the crucial difference from a habit that is enjoyed. These behaviours are not pleasurable; they are driven, time-consuming and hard to resist, and they often increase anxiety and low mood, because each glance in the mirror confirms the flaw. Zoya's checks do not reassure her. DSM-5-TR also describes a variant, body dysmorphic disorder by proxy, in which the preoccupation concerns flaws the person perceives in someone else's appearance, most often a spouse or partner.

Many people with BDD have ideas of reference (or, at the extreme, delusions of reference): the belief that other people take special notice of them, stare at them or mock them because of the way they look, as Zoya believes of the passengers on the metro. The disorder goes with high anxiety, social anxiety and avoidance, depressed mood, high negative affectivity (neuroticism), rejection sensitivity, perfectionism and low self-esteem, and also with elevated hostility. Most sufferers are deeply ashamed, both of their appearance and of how much they think about it, and reluctant to tell anyone, which is why the disorder so often goes unrecognised. Laboratory studies add a striking detail: people with BDD tend to read neutral or ambiguous faces as negative or threatening, and they process what they see in a detailed, piece-by-piece way rather than taking in the whole. If you scrutinise your own nose as a collection of millimetre-sized features, it is easy to find something wrong with it.

DSM-5-TR offers two specifiers. The first is muscle dysmorphia: preoccupation with the idea that one's body build is too small or insufficiently lean or muscular, the condition popularly called "bigorexia". It occurs almost exclusively in men and adolescent boys, whose bodies are normal-looking or even very muscular. Most diet, exercise or lift weights excessively, sometimes to the point of injuring themselves, and some take potentially dangerous anabolic-androgenic steroids and other substances to grow bigger. The specifier is used even when the person is also preoccupied with other body areas, which is often the case. The second specifier records insight into the BDD beliefs ("I look ugly", "I look deformed"): with good or fair insight (the person recognises that the beliefs are definitely or probably not true, or may or may not be true); with poor insight (the person thinks the beliefs are probably true); and with absent insight/delusional beliefs (the person is completely convinced that they are true). On average insight in BDD is poor, and one-third or more of sufferers currently hold their beliefs with delusional conviction. In Barlow and Durand's account, between 33 and 50 percent of the patients in two large clinical series were convinced that the defect was real, compared with about 10 percent of people with OCD who lack insight.

Should such a convinced patient be called psychotic? Under DSM-IV, a person who held the belief with delusional intensity could be given a second diagnosis of delusional disorder, somatic type (Chapter 4). Research by Phillips and her colleagues (2006) then compared delusional and non-delusional patients and found no meaningful difference beyond the fact that the delusional group was more severely ill (and somewhat less educated). Later work by the same group (Phillips and colleagues 2010, cited by Barlow and Durand) showed that both groups responded equally well to the treatments that work for BDD, and that the delusional group did not respond to the drug treatments used for psychotic disorders. So DSM-5 gave all of them one diagnosis: body dysmorphic disorder, with the degree of insight recorded by the specifier. DSM-5-TR adds that the greater suicidality of the delusional group seems to be explained by its more severe symptoms rather than by the delusional conviction itself.

Exam trap

Despite its old name, dysmorphophobia, body dysmorphic disorder is not a phobia: the person is not afraid of becoming ugly but convinced of being ugly. A person who is completely convinced that the flaw is real receives body dysmorphic disorder with absent insight/delusional beliefs, not delusional disorder. A noticeable, real defect does not qualify (that is "body dysmorphic-like disorder with actual flaws", an other specified diagnosis). Muscle dysmorphia is a specifier of BDD, not an eating disorder.

Body dysmorphic disorder: who, when, the cosmetic route and the look-alikes

Because sufferers hide it, BDD was long thought to be rare. It is not. In a nationwide study of American adults the point prevalence was 2.4 percent (2.5 percent in women and 2.2 percent in men), and similar studies in other countries, such as Germany, have found 1.7 to 2.9 percent. That makes BDD at least as common as OCD. Clinical series vary in their sex ratio: 68.5 percent of Phillips's 200 patients were women, whereas a majority of a large Japanese series were men. DSM-5-TR concludes that men and women are more alike than different in their disliked body areas, repetitive behaviours, severity, suicidality and course, but that men are more likely to be preoccupied with their genitals, body build and thinning hair and more likely to misuse substances, while women are more likely to worry about weight, breasts, buttocks, legs, hips and excessive body hair and more likely to have a comorbid eating disorder. Among adolescents and college students the disorder is somewhat commoner in girls and young women.

BDD is a disorder of adolescence. DSM-5-TR gives the mean age at onset as 16 to 17 years, the median as 15, and the most common age as 12 to 13; in two-thirds of sufferers it begins before 18. Milder, subclinical concerns usually appear around 12 or 13 and grow gradually into the full disorder, although some people experience an abrupt onset. Without treatment the course is usually chronic. Barlow and Durand describe a woman who had suffered for 71 years, since the age of nine, and a prospective study of 183 patients in which only 21 percent improved even somewhat over a year. Childhood neglect, abuse and trauma, and especially teasing, are commoner in the histories of people with BDD (the genetic findings are in Section 6.7).

BDD is one of the most disabling disorders in this book. Almost everyone with it has impaired social and working life, from avoiding some gatherings to being completely housebound, and about 20 percent of young people with the disorder say they dropped out of school primarily because of it. In one study of 62 outpatients, their distress, impairment and quality of life were worse on several measures than those of patients with depression, diabetes or a recent heart attack. The risk of suicide is grave. A meta-analysis of 17 studies cited in DSM-5-TR found that people with BDD were about four times more likely to have had suicidal thoughts, and about 2.6 times more likely to have attempted suicide, than healthy controls and than people with eating disorders, OCD or any anxiety disorder. In two German population studies, suicidal thoughts were reported by 19 percent and 31 percent of people with BDD against about 3 percent of those without it. In three of the clinical series reported by Barlow and Durand, between 21 and 27.5 percent of patients had already attempted suicide. DSM-5-TR lists the ways BDD differs from OCD: poorer insight, more frequent depression and higher rates of suicidal ideation. Major depression is the commonest comorbid disorder, usually beginning after the BDD; social anxiety disorder, OCD and substance-related disorders (including anabolic steroid use in muscle dysmorphia) are also common.

Where do people with BDD go for help? Not, as a rule, to a psychologist. They go to the people who can change the way they look. A majority receive cosmetic treatment, dermatological treatment and surgery most often, and DSM-5-TR's prevalence figures from these clinics are remarkable: BDD is found in 11 to 13 percent of dermatology patients, 13 to 15 percent of general cosmetic surgery patients and 20 percent of patients seeking rhinoplasty (an operation on the nose), as well as 11 percent of adults seeking jaw-correction surgery and 5 to 10 percent of adults seeking orthodontic or cosmetic dental work. The treatments rarely help. DSM-5-TR says that BDD responds poorly to cosmetic treatment and sometimes gets worse. In Barlow and Durand's summary of the research, people with BDD who have surgery seldom get the result they want: they return for more surgery on the same "defect" or shift their attention to a new one, and in one group of 50 people who sought surgery or a similar consultation, 81 percent were dissatisfied with the result. Some take legal action against their surgeons, and some become violent towards them.

Key study: Veale and the patients who operated on themselves (2000)

How desperate can the wish to change one's appearance become? The British psychiatrist David Veale collected information on 25 patients with BDD who had sought cosmetic surgery in the past. Nine of them, because they could not afford an operation or had been turned down, had tried to alter their appearance with their own hands, often with tragic results. A man who believed that the skin of his face was too "loose" used a staple gun on both sides of his face to pull it taut; the staples fell out after ten minutes, and he narrowly missed damaging his facial nerve. A woman preoccupied with the shape of her face filed down her teeth to change the line of her jaw. Another woman, who wanted liposuction but could not pay for it, cut her thighs with a knife and tried to squeeze out the fat. The study was small and descriptive, but its lesson is plain: the problem in BDD is not the nose or the skin, and changing the body does not cure it. DSM-5-TR confirms that "some individuals perform surgery on themselves".

Clinical link

Most people with BDD will never refer themselves to a psychologist, so the clinician who meets them is often a dermatologist, a cosmetic surgeon or a dentist. Liaison work with these clinics, and a few screening questions (How much time do you spend thinking about your appearance? What do you do about it? How has it affected your life?), can find patients before they undergo operations that will not help. When you see a client like Zoya, do not argue about whether her nose is crooked and do not offer endless reassurance, which works like another checking ritual. Focus instead on what the preoccupation is costing her, and always ask about suicidal thoughts. The treatments with evidence are cognitive-behavioural therapy built on exposure and response prevention (Book 2, Sections 4.3.5 and 5.6.7) and serotonin reuptake inhibitors (Section 6.9).

What causes BDD is less well understood than what causes OCD. Psychoanalytic writers proposed that an unbearable unconscious conflict is displaced onto a body part, which becomes a safer target for the person's distress; there is little evidence for this, but it is worth knowing because displacement is usually taught as the defence of the phobias (Section 6.8). The genetic and brain evidence, much of it shared with OCD, is in Section 6.7.

Culture shapes the form of the concern, though not the core of the disorder. DSM-5-TR notes that the gender ratio, the body areas of concern, the repetitive behaviours and the levels of distress are similar around the world, but that in collectivistic cultures such as Japan the fear may centre on offending other people with one's deformity, and that particular standards produce particular worries: eyelids in Japan, muscularity in Western countries. Traditional Japanese psychiatry recognises shubo-kyofu, "the phobia of a deformed body", a variant of taijin kyofusho that resembles BDD. In India, where advertisements have long sold creams that promise lighter skin, a wish for fairer skin is culturally ordinary and is not a disorder; what makes Zoya's concern about her "dark" skin part of BDD is the hours of preoccupation, the rituals, the distress and the fact that nobody else can see the flaw. BDD must also be told apart from koro, which DSM-5-TR describes as a culturally related condition, usually occurring in epidemics in South-East Asia, in which a man fears that his penis (or a woman that her labia, nipples or breasts) is shrinking or retracting into the body, often with a belief that death will follow. Koro is a fear of retraction and death, not a preoccupation with ugliness (Chapter 1 treats it, and its relative, dhat syndrome, among the cultural concepts of distress).

Because BDD involves the body, anxiety and sometimes delusional conviction, it has many look-alikes, and examiners love to set them as distractors. Table 6.18 sets them side by side; the disorders that are taught elsewhere get one line each here.

Table 6.18 Body dysmorphic disorder and the disorders it is confused with.
DisorderCore concernHow it differs from BDDTaught in
Body dysmorphic disorderA perceived flaw in appearance that others cannot see or find slightRepetitive checking, grooming, comparing; poor insight commonThis section
Somatic symptom disorderDistressing bodily symptoms (pain, fatigue)Excessive thoughts, feelings and behaviour about symptoms, not looksChapter 7
Illness anxiety disorderFear of having or getting a serious illnessFear of disease, with few or no symptoms; not about appearanceChapter 7
Conversion disorderNeurological symptoms (weakness, fits, blindness)Symptoms incompatible with recognised disease; no appearance concernChapter 7
Eating disordersBody weight and shape, fear of fatnessWeight concern counts as the eating disorder; both can coexistChapter 8
Obsessive-compulsive disorderObsessions on many themes (contamination, harm, symmetry)BDD preoccupations concern appearance onlySection 6.5
Social anxiety disorderFear of scrutiny and embarrassmentIn BDD the social fear comes from the "defect"Section 6.3
Delusional disorder, somatic typeA fixed false belief about the bodyDelusional appearance beliefs are diagnosed as BDD with absent insightChapter 4

A few further boundaries complete the picture. Ordinary concern about one's looks, which nearly everyone has, differs from BDD in the amount of time taken, the difficulty of resisting it and the distress or impairment it causes. Major depression can bring self-loathing about one's appearance, but it lacks the prominent preoccupation and rituals; and since depression so often follows BDD, the BDD should be diagnosed whenever its criteria are met. BDD is not diagnosed when the concern is discomfort with one's sex characteristics in gender dysphoria (Chapter 9), or when the belief is that one gives off a foul smell (olfactory reference disorder, Section 6.6.6). DSM-5-TR also contrasts BDD with body integrity dysphoria, a condition included in ICD-11 but not in DSM-5, in which a person persistently wants a healthy limb amputated, not because it looks ugly but because it feels as though it should not be there.

Exam trap

"Persistent preoccupation with perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable to others" is body dysmorphic disorder. The distractors are all real disorders about the body: somatic symptom disorder (distressing symptoms), illness anxiety disorder (fear of a serious illness) and conversion disorder (unexplained neurological symptoms). In DSM-5 and DSM-5-TR, BDD is classified under obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, not under somatic symptom and related disorders, anxiety disorders or personality disorders. If an option says "somatoform disorders", it is the DSM-IV answer.

Hoarding disorder

Mahesh is a 64-year-old widower who retired from a clerk's post in the railways in Kolkata. His two-room flat holds what his daughter calls "forty years of paper": every newspaper he has bought since the early 1980s, tied in bundles that rise to the ceiling, together with old bills, wedding cards, calendars, plastic bags folded into other plastic bags, three broken table fans and a cupboard of shirts he has not worn in a decade. A narrow path leads from the door to a single chair, where he now sleeps, because his bed disappeared under the bundles years ago. The kitchen cannot be used; he eats out of packets. When Mahesh was in hospital two years ago his daughter hired two men and cleared half the flat. He wept when he came home, accused her of throwing away "important information", and within a year the space had filled again, partly with second-hand books he still buys every week from the pavement stalls of College Street. Neighbours complain about cockroaches and about the risk of fire, because stacks of paper lean against the old wiring. Mahesh does not think he has a problem. "Everything here is useful," he says. "One day I will sort it."

Not long ago, Barlow and Durand recall, specialty anxiety clinics began to see patients like Mahesh, people who could not throw anything away, even a ten-year-old newspaper, for fear that they might urgently need it. At first the clinics assumed that this was a strange variant of OCD. It soon became clear that it was a major problem in its own right, and research led by the psychologists Randy O. Frost and Gail Steketee described its three main features: excessive acquisition of things, difficulty discarding them, and living amid excessive clutter and gross disorganisation. In 2013 DSM-5 made hoarding disorder a separate diagnosis.

The DSM-5-TR criteria can be read as a series of answers to the question "Why isn't this just untidiness, poverty or a hobby?" Criterion A requires persistent difficulty discarding or parting with possessions, regardless of their actual value. "Persistent" rules out a temporary mess, such as the clutter that follows inheriting a relative's house; "discarding" covers throwing away, selling, giving away and recycling; and "regardless of their actual value" matters because people who hoard save valuable things too, often in piles mixed with worthless ones. Criterion B says why the person cannot discard: because of a perceived need to save the items and distress at the thought of parting with them. This criterion makes the saving intentional, and that is what separates hoarding disorder from the passive accumulation of objects seen, for example, in severe depression, dementia or schizophrenia, where possessions pile up because the person does not care or cannot manage, and removing them causes no distress. The reasons people give are the usefulness or beauty of the items, strong sentimental attachment, a sense of responsibility for the objects' fate, a horror of waste and a fear of losing important information. The commonest items saved are newspapers, magazines, clothing, bags, books, mail and papers, but virtually anything can be saved.

Criterion C requires that the possessions congest and clutter active living areas and substantially compromise their intended use: the person cannot cook in the kitchen, sleep in the bed or sit in the chair. Here clutter has a technical meaning: a large group of usually unrelated or barely related objects piled together in a disorganised way in spaces designed for other purposes. The word "active" matters, because many people without the disorder have a cluttered attic, storeroom or garage; DSM looks at the rooms in which life is supposed to happen. The criterion ends with a clause written for cases like Mahesh's: if the living areas are uncluttered, it is only because third parties (family members, cleaners, local authorities) have intervened. A person whose home has been forcibly cleared still has hoarding disorder. Criterion D requires clinically significant distress or impairment, and DSM-5-TR adds "including maintaining a safe environment for self and others", because in hoarding the impairment often falls on the people around the hoarder. When insight is poor the person may report no distress at all, and the impairment is visible only to the family, until someone tries to clear the house, when distress is intense.

The last two criteria are exclusions. Criterion E rules out hoarding that is the direct result of another medical condition: a brain injury, cerebrovascular disease, an infection of the brain such as herpes simplex encephalitis, surgery for a tumour, or Prader-Willi syndrome (a genetic disorder of chromosome 15 marked by an insatiable appetite, short stature and intellectual disability, in which hoarding and skin picking are common). Damage to the anterior ventromedial prefrontal and cingulate cortex is particularly associated with accumulating objects; in such patients the hoarding begins abruptly after the injury. Criterion F rules out hoarding better explained by another mental disorder: obsessions in OCD, decreased energy in major depression, delusions in schizophrenia, the cognitive deficits of a major neurocognitive disorder, or the restricted interests of autism spectrum disorder.

Two specifiers complete the diagnosis. The first is excessive acquisition: the difficulty discarding is accompanied by excessive collecting, buying or stealing of items that are not needed or for which there is no space. About 80 to 90 percent of people with hoarding disorder show it. Excessive buying is the commonest form, followed by acquiring free items (leaflets, things other people have thrown out); stealing is less common. Barlow and Durand note that acquiring often brings pleasure, even euphoria, and may be a response to feeling low, the "retail therapy" of popular speech. Women tend to show more excessive acquisition, particularly buying, than men. The second specifier records insight, worded for hoarding: with good or fair insight (the person recognises that the hoarding-related beliefs and behaviours are problematic), with poor insight (the person is mostly convinced that they are not problematic, despite evidence to the contrary) and with absent insight/delusional beliefs (the person is completely convinced of this). Mahesh, who thinks that everything is useful and that nothing is wrong, has at best poor insight.

People who hoard also tend to be indecisive (a trait their first-degree relatives share), perfectionistic, prone to procrastination and avoidance, distractible, and poor at planning and organising. Barlow and Durand add an extraordinarily strong emotional attachment to possessions, an exaggerated desire for control over them, and a striking difficulty deciding whether a possession is worth keeping, as though every object were equally valuable. A special group, animal hoarding, is defined by DSM-5-TR as the accumulation of a large number of animals together with a failure to provide minimal standards of nutrition, sanitation and veterinary care, and a failure to act on the animals' deteriorating condition (disease, starvation, even death) or on the environment (severe overcrowding, extremely unsanitary conditions). Newspaper reports describe homes occupied by one owner, often a middle-aged or elderly woman, and thirty or more animals, often cats, some of them dead. Many animal hoarders also hoard objects. Compared with object hoarding, animal hoarding involves more unsanitary conditions and even poorer insight: such people typically believe they are devoted carers.

Where does hoarding end and collecting begin? A philatelist may own as many stamps as a hoarder owns newspapers. The difference, DSM-5-TR explains, is that normal collecting is organised and selective: the collector chooses, catalogues and displays, and the collection does not produce the clutter, distress or impairment of hoarding disorder. And why is hoarding not simply OCD? Barlow and Durand and DSM-5-TR give several reasons. In hoarding disorder there are no intrusive, unwanted obsessions that the saving is meant to neutralise; the urge to keep things feels natural, and the possessions are valued, sometimes loved. Acquisition can be pleasurable, whereas compulsions in OCD bring at most relief. The course differs: OCD tends to wax and wane, whereas hoarding begins early and gets worse with each passing decade, with few people reporting a waxing and waning course. And the results of treatment for hoarding have so far been more modest than those for OCD. Hoarding can occur within OCD, but then it looks different: it is driven by typical obsessions (fear of contamination or harm, or a feeling of incompleteness, such as a fear of losing one's identity unless every scrap of one's life is preserved), or by the wish to avoid onerous rituals (not throwing anything away to avoid endless washing). It is unwanted, distressing and never pleasurable, there is usually no excessive acquisition, and the hoarded items are more often bizarre (rubbish, rotten food, used nappies, nail clippings, even urine or faeces), which is very unusual in hoarding disorder. About 20 percent of people with hoarding disorder also meet criteria for OCD; when severe hoarding coexists with OCD but is independent of its obsessions, both disorders are diagnosed.

Nationally representative figures for hoarding disorder do not yet exist, but community surveys in the United States and Europe put the point prevalence of clinically significant hoarding at 1.5 to 6 percent, and a meta-analysis of 12 studies in high-income countries found 2.5 percent, with no difference between the sexes. Clinical samples, by contrast, are mostly women. Hoarding is strongly tied to age. Symptoms may first appear around ages 15 to 19, start to interfere with daily functioning by the mid-twenties and cause clinically significant impairment by the mid-thirties; participants in clinical research are usually in their fifties, and Barlow and Durand put the average age at which people come for treatment at about 50, often after many years of hoarding and often living alone. In one population study in the Netherlands, hoarding symptoms were almost three times commoner in adults over 65 than in adults aged 30 to 40. About three-quarters of people with hoarding disorder also have a mood or anxiety disorder, most often major depression (30 to 50 percent), social anxiety disorder or generalised anxiety disorder, and these comorbid conditions are often the reason they consult, because people rarely volunteer their hoarding and clinicians rarely ask about it.

The consequences reach well beyond the hoarder. Clutter impairs moving through the house, cooking, cleaning, washing and sleeping; appliances break and cannot be repaired because nobody can reach them; water and electricity may be cut off. In severe cases there is a real risk of fire, of falls (especially for elderly people) and of disease. Barlow and Durand cite a striking figure, which comes from one local data set: a ten-year review of residential fires by the Metropolitan Fire Brigade in Melbourne, Australia, found that fires in the homes of people who hoard made up only about 0.25 percent of residential fires but accounted for 24 percent of the preventable fire deaths (the figure is often quoted, as Barlow and Durand quote it, as 24 percent of all fire-related deaths, but it describes preventable deaths in one city). Families are strained, neighbours and local authorities are drawn in, and a substantial proportion of people with severe hoarding have faced eviction.

Indian context

In many Indian homes, keeping things is a virtue. Newspapers are stacked to be sold to the raddiwala, plastic bags and containers are washed and reused, and old clothes are passed down or turned into dusters. None of this is hoarding disorder. DSM-5-TR addresses exactly this situation: in cultures that place a high value on thrift and on storing possessions, the presence of distress and functional impairment should be the basis of the diagnosis. The test is not how much a family keeps but whether the keeping has defeated the use of the home and the safety of the people in it, as it has for Mahesh, who sleeps in a chair because his bed is buried. The available data from low- and middle-income countries suggest that the clinical features of hoarding are similar across cultures.

Exam trap

"Which one is not a compulsive behaviour in OCD: tapping, hoarding, washing or checking?" The answer is hoarding. Tapping, washing and checking are classic compulsions; hoarding has been a disorder in its own right since DSM-5, because it lacks intrusive obsessions, the possessions are valued rather than feared, and the course worsens with age. (Saving that is driven by OCD obsessions is diagnosed as OCD, but that is the exception.) Also know that an inability to discard worthless objects is a criterion of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, and that collecting is organised and selective, while hoarding produces clutter and impairment.

Trichotillomania and excoriation disorder: the body-focused repetitive behaviours

Swati is fifteen and in Class 10 at a school in Pune. Her mother first noticed the bald patch on the crown of her head in the summer before the board examinations and took her to a dermatologist, sure that it was a disease of the scalp. It was not. Swati pulls out her hair. It happens mostly at night, at her desk, with her chemistry notes open in front of her, and on the bed when she is scrolling on her phone. Sometimes she does not notice that she is doing it until she sees a small heap of hairs on the page. At other times she feels an itchy, tingling sensation on her scalp and a building tension, and she searches with her fingers for a hair that feels coarse or "wrong", pulls it so that the root comes out whole, looks at it, rolls it between her fingers and bites off the root. For a moment she feels relief, almost pleasure; then she feels ashamed. Her eyebrows are almost gone, so she fills them in with a pencil, and she wears a broad hairband to cover the patch on her crown. She has stopped going to swimming classes. She has promised herself, and her mother, many times that she will stop. She has not been able to.

Swati has trichotillomania (hair-pulling disorder). The term was coined in 1889 by the French dermatologist François Henri Hallopeau (1842 to 1919) from Greek roots meaning hair (thrix), to pull (tillein) and madness (mania). The condition entered the American manual in DSM-III-R (1987), was classed in DSM-IV as an impulse-control disorder, and moved in DSM-5 to the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. DSM-5-TR's criteria are short. There must be (A) recurrent pulling out of one's own hair, resulting in hair loss; (B) repeated attempts to decrease or stop the pulling; (C) clinically significant distress or impairment; (D) no other medical condition, such as a skin disease, that accounts for the pulling or the hair loss; and (E) no other mental disorder that better explains it, such as attempts to improve a perceived flaw in appearance in BDD.

Each criterion does a job. Criterion A requires hair loss, but DSM-5-TR notes that some people pull single hairs from all over a site, so that the loss is not clearly visible, and that many conceal it with make-up, scarves or wigs. Criterion B, the repeated attempts to stop, separates the disorder from a habit the person is content with, and it captures the sense of being out of control that makes the disorder so shaming. Criterion C counts as distress the feelings of loss of control, embarrassment and shame, and as impairment the avoidance of work, school and public situations, such as Swati's swimming class. Criterion D exists because many medical conditions cause hair loss without any pulling: alopecia areata, androgenic alopecia, telogen effluvium and several scarring conditions of the scalp. Most people with trichotillomania admit to pulling, but when they do not, a skin biopsy or dermoscopy of the scalp (trichoscopy) can settle the question, because pulled hair shows a characteristic pattern of reduced density and short, broken hairs of different lengths. Criterion E removes hair pulling that serves another disorder: the person with OCD who pulls hairs as part of a symmetry ritual, the person with BDD who plucks hairs that look ugly or asymmetrical, and the person with a psychosis who pulls hair in response to a delusion or a hallucination.

The commonest sites are the scalp, the eyebrows and the eyelids (pulling eyelashes); less often the armpits, face, pubic area and perianal region. The sites may change over time. Pulling may come in brief episodes scattered through the day or in long sessions lasting hours, and the disorder may endure for months or years. It is often accompanied by rituals with the hair, of exactly the kind Swati performs: searching for a particular kind of hair (a specific texture or colour), trying to pull it out in a particular way (so that the root comes out intact), and examining it, rolling it between the fingers, pulling the strand between the teeth, biting it into pieces or swallowing it. Swallowing hair, trichophagia, can produce a trichobezoar, a ball of hair in the stomach or intestine that can cause anaemia, abdominal pain, vomiting, obstruction of the bowel and even perforation. Pain does not usually accompany pulling. Some people pull in a pattern that leaves nearly complete baldness except for a narrow fringe around the edges of the scalp, called tonsure trichotillomania after the shaved crown of a monk. Pulling rarely happens in front of anyone except immediate family members; some people also pull hair from other people, from pets or dolls, or from sweaters and carpets.

The emotional sequence is what most clearly marks hair pulling off from an OCD compulsion. DSM-5-TR describes it in three steps: pulling may be triggered by anxiety or by boredom; it may be preceded by a rising sense of tension (either just before pulling or while trying to resist the urge); and it may lead to gratification, pleasure or relief. Some people report an "itch-like" or tingling sensation in the scalp that pulling relieves. Clinicians distinguish two styles. In focused pulling the person attends to the act, feels the tension beforehand and the relief afterwards; in automatic pulling the hand seems to pull on its own, while the person reads, watches television or lies in bed, without full awareness. Many people, like Swati, show both. Compare this with an OCD compulsion. The compulsion answers an obsession: a mother washes her hands because she fears she will infect her baby, and the washing brings relief from a feared consequence, never pleasure. Hair pulling answers no obsession and prevents no catastrophe; it is driven by an urge, a sensation or a mood, and it can feel good while it lasts. DSM-IV had actually required a rising tension before pulling and pleasure or relief afterwards. DSM-5 dropped both requirements, because research showed that a substantial number of people who pull do not experience the tension-relief sequence (Barlow and Durand).

Trichotillomania is commoner than once believed. DSM-5-TR estimates its 12-month prevalence in adults and adolescents at 1 to 2 percent, and Barlow and Durand cite rates of 1 to 5 percent among college students. In clinical or self-identified samples women outnumber men by 10 to 1 or more, but the ratio may be closer to 2 to 1 in community samples, and in an online survey of more than 10,000 adults aged 18 to 69, representative of the American population, 1.7 percent reported current trichotillomania with no significant gender difference (1.8 percent of men and 1.7 percent of women). Among children, boys and girls are about equally represented. Hair pulling in infants is common and usually resolves; the disorder proper most often begins with, or just after, puberty. The course is chronic, with some waxing and waning if untreated, and symptoms may worsen before menstruation. A minority of people remit within a few years of onset without relapse. Most people with trichotillomania have at least one other body-focused repetitive behaviour (skin picking, nail biting, lip chewing), and the commonest comorbid disorders are major depression and excoriation disorder.

Three more boundaries are worth knowing. Hair removal done purely for cosmetic reasons, such as plucking the eyebrows into shape, is not trichotillomania. Twisting and playing with the hair, or biting it rather than pulling it, does not qualify either. And a child with intellectual disability or autism who pulls hair along with head banging and hand biting, when frustrated or excited, is diagnosed with stereotypic movement disorder (Chapter 3) rather than trichotillomania. Stimulant drugs can worsen hair pulling, but they are rarely its main cause.

Trichotillomania's twin is excoriation disorder (skin-picking disorder). Dermatologists had long described patients who picked their skin into wounds, under names such as neurotic or psychogenic excoriation, but DSM-5 was the first edition of the manual to give the problem its own diagnosis. The criteria follow the same pattern: (A) recurrent skin picking resulting in skin lesions; (B) repeated attempts to decrease or stop; (C) clinically significant distress or impairment; (D) not attributable to the physiological effects of a substance, such as cocaine, or to another medical condition, such as scabies (a mite infestation that causes intense itching and scratching); and (E) not better explained by another mental disorder: the delusions (such as a belief in parasites under the skin) or tactile hallucinations (formication, the feeling of insects crawling) of a psychotic disorder; attempts to improve a perceived flaw in BDD; stereotypies in stereotypic movement disorder; or the intention to harm oneself in non-suicidal self-injury. That last exclusion matters: a young person who cuts or burns herself to relieve unbearable feelings is injuring herself deliberately, whereas a person with excoriation disorder picks because of an urge and is distressed by the damage.

The commonest sites are the face, the arms and the hands, though many people pick at several sites. They pick at healthy skin, at minor irregularities, at pimples or calluses, and at the scabs left by earlier picking, mostly with the fingernails but also with tweezers, pins or other objects, and they may rub, squeeze, lance or bite the skin as well. As with hair pulling, there may be rituals (searching for a particular kind of scab, examining it, playing with it, putting it in the mouth or swallowing it); there may be triggers of anxiety or boredom, rising tension and a sense of relief; and there are focused and automatic styles. The majority of people with the disorder spend at least an hour a day picking, thinking about picking and resisting the urge. The damage can be serious: scarring, infection that often needs antibiotics, occasionally surgery, and rarely a life-threatening complication. In the online survey of more than 10,000 American adults, 2.1 percent reported current and 3.1 percent lifetime excoriation disorder, and three-quarters or more of those affected in community samples are women. Picking usually begins in adolescence, around puberty, and often starts with a skin condition such as acne. When acne leads to picking that has taken on a life of its own, dermatologists speak of acne excoriée, and the clinical question is whether the picking has become independent of the skin condition. The course is chronic, with waxing and waning.

Excoriation disorder must be told apart from dermatitis artefacta, the dermatologist's term for skin lesions that are presumably self-inflicted but that the patient denies producing. If there is evidence of deception, the diagnosis is malingering (when there is an external incentive) or factitious disorder (when there is none); in the absence of deception, and with repeated attempts to stop, excoriation disorder can be diagnosed. Washing compulsions in OCD can also damage the skin, and a person with BDD may pick at her skin to remove "imperfections"; in both cases the other disorder, not excoriation disorder, is diagnosed. People with Prader-Willi syndrome often begin picking their skin early in life, and their picking may meet the criteria for stereotypic movement disorder instead.

Hair pulling and skin picking are the two named members of a larger group, the body-focused repetitive behaviours. DSM-5-TR records the others, such as nail biting, lip biting and cheek chewing, as "other body-focused repetitive behaviour disorder", one of its examples of other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, when they are accompanied by repeated attempts to stop and cause significant distress or impairment. The psychological treatment with the best evidence for the whole group is habit reversal training, in which the person learns to notice the first movement of the hand and to substitute a competing response (Book 2, Section 4.9.1); the drug evidence is in Section 6.9.

Exam trap

Trichotillomania is no longer an impulse-control disorder: DSM-5 moved it to the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, and ICD-11 calls it a body-focused repetitive behaviour disorder. DSM-5 also removed DSM-IV's requirement of rising tension before pulling and relief afterwards. Hair pulling or skin picking aimed at fixing a perceived flaw is BDD, not trichotillomania or excoriation disorder; picking meant to hurt oneself is non-suicidal self-injury; picking in response to delusional parasites belongs to a psychosis. Unlike OCD compulsions, body-focused repetitive behaviours are not triggered by obsessions.

The rest of the family: other specified, substance-induced and medical forms, and ICD-11

Every DSM chapter ends with categories for presentations that do not fit its named disorders, and the OCRD chapter's examples are unusually interesting. DSM-5-TR uses other specified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder when the clinician wants to record why the criteria are not met, and lists seven examples:

  • Body dysmorphic-like disorder with actual flaws: like BDD, except that the defects are clearly observable to others, yet the preoccupation with them is clearly excessive and impairing.
  • Body dysmorphic-like disorder without repetitive behaviours: the person meets the criteria for BDD except that she has never performed repetitive behaviours or mental acts in response to the concern.
  • Other body-focused repetitive behaviour disorder: nail biting, lip biting, cheek chewing and the like, with repeated attempts to stop and with distress or impairment.
  • Obsessional jealousy: a non-delusional preoccupation with a partner's perceived infidelity, which may lead to repetitive behaviours or mental acts (checking the partner's phone, cross-questioning, replaying events) and which is not better explained by delusional disorder, jealous type, or by paranoid personality disorder. The person with obsessional jealousy can see that the suspicion may be unfounded but cannot stop checking; in delusional jealousy (Chapter 4) the infidelity is simply known to be true.
  • Olfactory reference disorder (olfactory reference syndrome): a persistent preoccupation with the belief that one gives off a foul or offensive body odour that others cannot notice or notice only slightly, with repetitive and excessive behaviours such as checking for the odour, showering repeatedly, seeking reassurance and trying to camouflage the smell with perfumes. Traditional Japanese psychiatry knows it as jikoshu-kyofu, a variant of taijin kyofusho.
  • Shubo-kyofu: the taijin kyofusho variant, met in Section 6.6.3, marked by an excessive fear of having a bodily deformity.
  • Koro: which DSM-5-TR relates to dhat syndrome, an episode of sudden and intense anxiety that the penis (or the vulva and nipples) will recede into the body, possibly leading to death.

Unspecified obsessive-compulsive and related disorder is used when the clinician chooses not to say why the criteria are not met, or lacks the information to say, for example in an emergency department.

Two further categories mirror those found in every chapter of DSM-5-TR. In substance/medication-induced obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, obsessions, compulsions, hair pulling, skin picking or other OCRD symptoms predominate and develop during or soon after intoxication with, or withdrawal from, a substance, or after exposure to a medication. The substances DSM-5-TR names are stimulants, including cocaine and amphetamine-type drugs, together with heavy metals and toxins; its worked example is repetitive skin picking during intoxication in a man with a severe cocaine use disorder. The diagnosis is not made if the symptoms began before the substance use or persist for a substantial period (about a month) after intoxication or withdrawal ends, because then an independent disorder is more likely. The condition is very rare (Chapter 10 treats the substances).

In obsessive-compulsive and related disorder due to another medical condition, the symptoms are the direct physiological consequence of an illness, shown by the history, examination or laboratory findings. DSM-5-TR's examples are instructive. Sydenham's chorea is the neurological manifestation of rheumatic fever, which itself follows infection with group A streptococcus (the bacterium of "strep throat"); alongside its involuntary, jerky movements it can produce obsessions, compulsions, inattention and emotional lability, and a child who presents with OCD-like symptoms of this kind receives this diagnosis. DSM-5-TR also discusses PANDAS and the broader PANS, sudden-onset childhood obsessions, compulsions or tics after an infection, which Section 6.7 explains. Damage to the striatum, from a cerebral infarction (a stroke) or Huntington's disease, can likewise produce OCRD symptoms. The diagnosis carries specifiers for the predominant symptom (OCD-like symptoms, appearance preoccupations, hoarding, hair pulling or skin picking), is coded F06.8, and should be suspected whenever symptoms begin acutely, begin unusually late in life or take an atypical form. The course follows that of the underlying illness.

ICD-11, adopted by the World Health Assembly in 2019 and in use from 2022, has a grouping called obsessive-compulsive or related disorders that closely resembles DSM-5's, with three differences worth knowing. First, it gives olfactory reference disorder (6B22) full status as a named disorder, whereas DSM-5-TR mentions it only as an example of an other specified disorder. Second, it places hypochondriasis (health anxiety, 6B23) in this group, on the reasoning that, like OCD, it consists of a persistent preoccupation (with the possibility of having a serious illness) answered by repetitive behaviours such as checking the body and seeking reassurance; DSM-5-TR instead abolished hypochondriasis and replaced it with illness anxiety disorder and somatic symptom disorder, in a different chapter (Chapter 7). Third, ICD-11 names the urge-driven disorders as a group, the body-focused repetitive behaviour disorders (6B25), with trichotillomania and excoriation disorder as its two members. ICD-11 also cross-lists Tourette syndrome in this grouping, although Tourette syndrome itself is classified in the chapter on diseases of the nervous system (the tic disorders are taught in Chapter 3), and records symptoms caused by a medical condition as a "secondary obsessive-compulsive or related syndrome". One smaller difference: ICD-11's insight qualifier has two grades ("with fair to good insight" and "with poor to absent insight") where DSM-5-TR has three. Table 6.19 sets the two lists side by side.

Table 6.19 The obsessive-compulsive and related disorders in DSM-5-TR and ICD-11.
ConditionDSM-5-TR (2022)ICD-11 (in use from 2022)
Obsessive-compulsive disorderNamed disorderNamed disorder (6B20)
Body dysmorphic disorderNamed disorderNamed disorder (6B21)
Olfactory reference disorderExample of other specified OCRDNamed disorder (6B22)
Hypochondriasis (health anxiety)Not used; illness anxiety disorder and somatic symptom disorder, in another chapterNamed disorder (6B23)
Hoarding disorderNamed disorderNamed disorder (6B24)
Trichotillomania and excoriationTwo named disordersBody-focused repetitive behaviour disorders (6B25)
