Exam trap

Olfactory reference disorder is a named OCRD in ICD-11 but only an "other specified" example in DSM-5-TR. Hypochondriasis sits among the obsessive-compulsive or related disorders in ICD-11, whereas DSM-5-TR replaced it with illness anxiety disorder and somatic symptom disorder in the somatic symptom chapter. Obsessional jealousy is non-delusional and is an other specified OCRD; delusional jealousy is delusional disorder, jealous type. Koro (fear of genital retraction) and shubo-kyofu (fear of a deformed body) are cultural forms listed under other specified OCRD.

6.7 The Biology of Anxiety and ObsessionsMedium yield · 2 Qs

Two sisters grow up in the same house in Lucknow, eat the same food and go to the same school. One sails through her board examinations, sleeps well before interviews and shrugs off a near miss on the road. The other was a watchful, clinging baby, hid behind her mother's sari at every wedding, had her first panic attack at nineteen in a crowded bus and now cannot ride one alone. Why? Sections 6.2 to 6.6 described what the anxiety disorders and the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders look like. This section and the next ask what causes them. This section takes the biological evidence: the genes that run in families, the temperament that can be seen in a four-month-old, the chemical messengers that the anti-anxiety drugs act upon, and the brain circuits that sound the alarm. Section 6.8 takes the psychological evidence, conditioning, cognition and conflict, and joins the two in David Barlow's triple vulnerability theory. Keep one caution in mind throughout. Almost every biological finding in this field is a finding about vulnerability: something that makes a disorder more likely, not something that makes it inevitable.

What runs in families: genes and a general tendency to be anxious

The first question any biological theory must answer is whether a disorder runs in families, and the anxiety disorders clearly do. In family studies, investigators find people with a disorder (the probands), then interview their first-degree relatives (parents, siblings and children, who share on average half their genes) and compare the relatives' rates of illness with the rates among relatives of healthy people. Abby Fyer and her colleagues (1990), for example, found that about 31 percent of the first-degree relatives of people with a specific phobia also had a phobia, against 11 percent of the relatives of controls, and a later collaborative study with Barlow's centre replicated the finding (28 against 10 percent). Each type of phobia even seemed to "breed true": the relatives of a person with an animal phobia were most likely to fear animals. But families share kitchens and habits as well as genes. A child might learn to fear dogs simply by watching her mother scream at one. To separate genes from upbringing, researchers turn to twins, comparing identical (monozygotic) twins, who share all their genes, with fraternal (dizygotic) twins, who share about half. If identical twins are more alike in a disorder than fraternal twins, genes are probably at work, and the size of the difference yields an estimate of heritability, the proportion of the variation in a trait within a population that can be attributed to genetic differences between its members. Table 6.20 collects DSM-5-TR's figures for the disorders of this chapter.

Table 6.20 Family and twin findings for the anxiety and obsessive-compulsive and related disorders (DSM-5-TR, with one note from Barlow and Durand).
DisorderFamily and twin findingWhat it suggests
Separation anxiety disorderHeritability 73 percent in a community sample of 6-year-old twins, higher in girlsStrong genetic influence in childhood
Specific phobiaHeritability about 32 percent (animal), 33 percent (blood-injection-injury), 25 percent (situational)Blood-injection-injury phobia runs in families more strongly than any other phobia (Barlow and Durand)
Social anxiety disorderFirst-degree relatives have two to six times the riskBoth disorder-specific genes and general ones (negative affectivity)
Panic disorderMany genes; raised risk in children of parents with anxiety, depressive or bipolar disordersGenes shared with the mood disorders
AgoraphobiaHeritability 61 percentThe phobia most tied to a general proneness to phobias
Generalised anxiety disorderAbout one-third of the risk is geneticGenes overlap with neuroticism and major depression
Obsessive-compulsive disorderTwice the rate in first-degree relatives, ten times if onset was in childhood or adolescence; twin concordance 0.57 (identical) against 0.22 (fraternal); about 40 percent of symptom variance additive geneticEarly-onset OCD is the most familial form
Body dysmorphic disorderHeritability of symptoms 37 to 49 percent in adolescent and young adult twinsPartly shared with OCD
Hoarding disorderAbout 50 percent of the variability is additive genetic; more than half of those who hoard have a relative who hoardsThe rest is non-shared environment

Read down the table and two things stand out. First, every one of these disorders is moderately heritable, and none is wholly genetic. Second, and more surprising, the genes do not seem to be specific to the disorders. When the American psychiatric geneticist Kenneth S. Kendler and his colleagues (1995) analysed several disorders together in a large sample of twins, and when John Hettema and colleagues, with Kendler, returned to the question in 2005, they concluded that what is inherited is mostly a general tendency to become anxious rather than generalised anxiety disorder, panic disorder or any other particular diagnosis. DSM-5-TR names this broad tendency in almost every risk-factor section of the anxiety chapter: negative affectivity (neuroticism), a stable disposition to experience negative emotions such as fear, worry, irritability and sadness easily and intensely. DSM-5-TR says that the genes for GAD overlap with those for negative affectivity and are shared with other anxiety and mood disorders, especially major depression, which is one reason why anxiety and depression so often occur in the same person and in the same family (Chapter 5). There are a few more specific contributions too. Barlow and Durand note that the tendency to panic runs in families with a genetic contribution somewhat different from that of general anxiety, and DSM-5-TR describes agoraphobia as the phobia most strongly tied to a genetic factor for "proneness to phobias".

The clearest case of a specific inheritance is blood-injection-injury phobia, the fear that makes some people faint at the sight of blood or a needle (Section 6.3). Barlow and Durand report that as many as 60 percent of the first-degree relatives of people with this phobia share the reaction, at least to some degree, one of the highest rates of familial aggregation for any psychological disorder. What seems to be inherited is not the fear itself but an unusually strong vasovagal response: an overreaction of the baroreflex that normally corrects a sudden rise in blood pressure, so that the blood pressure drops too far and the person faints. The fear then grows around the possibility of fainting.

Which genes are involved? Here the honest answer is disappointing. As with almost every emotional trait and psychological disorder, no single gene causes anxiety or panic. Barlow and Durand describe the vulnerability as coming from collections of genes in several regions of the chromosomes, each of small effect, and DSM-5-TR says of panic disorder that "multiple genes likely confer vulnerability", but that the exact genes and their functions remain unknown. Pinel's Biopsychology goes further: researchers have been unable to locate the genes for anxiety disorders definitively, and attention has shifted to the epigenetic mechanisms through which experience switches genes on and off. DSM-5-TR mentions one tantalising lead, that a high-expressing form of the gene for the enzyme monoamine oxidase A (MAOA) may be a risk factor for panic disorder in women only, but no single gene explains much.

The genes, moreover, do not act alone. A genetic vulnerability, Barlow and Durand emphasise, does not cause anxiety or panic directly; stress and other events in the environment "turn on" the genes. DSM-5-TR gives an example in its account of social anxiety disorder: behavioural inhibition, the shy temperament described in the next subsection, is strongly genetically influenced, but the influence is subject to gene-environment interaction, because children high in behavioural inhibition are more susceptible to environmental influences such as socially anxious modelling by their parents. The same shy child may become a socially anxious adult if her parents avoid company and fret about what the neighbours think, and a merely reserved one if they gently and repeatedly encourage her to join in.

Exam trap

Heritability describes differences within a population, not the cause of one person's illness. A heritability of 61 percent for agoraphobia does not mean that 61 percent of your agoraphobia comes from your genes, nor that a heritable disorder cannot be treated. What is inherited in the anxiety disorders is mostly a general vulnerability (negative affectivity or neuroticism), not a specific disorder. The most familial phobia is blood-injection-injury, and the most familial form of OCD is the one that begins in childhood.

Born shy: Jerome Kagan and behavioural inhibition

If a general tendency to anxiety is inherited, it ought to be visible very early in life, long before a child could have learned to fear anything much. That is exactly what the Harvard developmental psychologist Jerome Kagan (1929 to 2021) set out to show. Kagan revived an idea as old as the Greek physician Galen, who sorted people into temperaments (his 1994 book was called Galen's Prophecy). By temperament developmental psychologists mean the biologically based individual differences in emotional reactivity that appear in infancy and remain fairly stable. The temperament Kagan studied was behavioural inhibition: a tendency to become quiet, cautious, watchful, withdrawn and distressed in the face of unfamiliar people, objects and situations. Some toddlers walk into a room full of strangers and new toys and start playing; others stop, cling to their mother, fall silent and watch from a distance, sometimes for many minutes.

Key study: Kagan and the high-reactive infants (1980s to 1999)

Kagan and his colleague Nancy Snidman began with four-month-old babies. Each infant was shown a series of unfamiliar stimuli (moving mobiles, new voices, new smells) while the researchers recorded how much the baby thrashed its arms and legs and cried. About 20 percent of healthy infants were "high reactive": they became agitated and cried far more than the rest (Barlow and Durand describe them becoming agitated and crying at toys and other age-appropriate stimuli). The babies were then followed for years. The high reactors were much more likely than low reactors to be fearful and avoidant of unfamiliar events and people as toddlers, and they tended to show biological signs of an easily aroused nervous system: a more reactive heart, greater activation of the right frontal cortex on the EEG, and even a narrower facial skeleton. In their 1999 review, Kagan and Snidman reported that by adolescence about one-third of the high-reactive group showed signs of serious social anxiety. A companion study by Carl Schwartz, Snidman and Kagan (1999) followed 79 children who had been classified as inhibited or uninhibited in the second year of life; at 13, the inhibited group showed more generalised social anxiety, but not more specific fears, separation anxiety or performance anxiety.

The conclusion was careful. Kagan and Snidman argued that the temperament should be seen as constraining the probability that a child will become fearless and spontaneous, not as determining an anxious personality: two-thirds of the high reactors did not develop serious social anxiety, and experience did much of the shaping. The criticisms are the usual ones for long follow-up studies: the samples were modest, mostly white and middle-class American children, and the many measures make chance findings possible.

Later research has confirmed the main point. Barlow and Durand cite evidence that children with excessive behavioural inhibition are at increased risk of developing phobic behaviour, and DSM-5-TR lists behavioural inhibition as a temperamental risk factor for nearly every disorder in this chapter, from specific phobia, social anxiety disorder, panic disorder, agoraphobia and generalised anxiety disorder to OCD. Its strongest and most specific link is with social anxiety, which makes intuitive sense: a child who is distressed by strangers has many more occasions to learn that social life is threatening. Here, then, is part of the answer to the puzzle of the two sisters. The watchful, clinging baby probably began life with a more reactive alarm system, and what happened to her afterwards determined what it became.

Exam trap

Two famous ideas share the same words. Behavioural inhibition in Jerome Kagan's sense is a temperament of children: shyness and distress at the unfamiliar, visible by four months, that raises the risk of later social anxiety and phobias. The behavioural inhibition system of Jeffrey Gray is a brain circuit (Section 6.7.4) that produces anxiety and cautious checking for danger. If a question mentions infants, temperament or shyness, it is Kagan; if it mentions the septal-hippocampal system or a circuit, it is Gray.

The chemistry of alarm: GABA, noradrenaline, serotonin and CRF

Much of what we know about the chemistry of anxiety was discovered backwards. Pinel points out that current theories of the neural basis of the anxiety disorders rest heavily on the analysis of drug effects: a drug is found by accident to calm people, researchers work out what it does in the brain, and they then guess that anxiety must involve the system the drug acts on. That reasoning has a weakness that Barlow and Durand illustrate with aspirin: the fact that aspirin cures a headache does not mean that the headache was caused by a shortage of aspirin. With that caution, four systems have the strongest claims (Table 6.21).

The first is the GABA-benzodiazepine system. Gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA) is the brain's main inhibitory neurotransmitter: when it binds to the GABA-A receptor, a channel opens and lets chloride ions into the neuron, making the neuron less likely to fire. The benzodiazepines, the family of drugs that includes chlordiazepoxide (Librium) and diazepam (Valium), act on this receptor; Pinel describes their behavioural effects as mediated by an agonistic action at GABA-A receptors. More precisely, they bind to their own site on the receptor complex and make GABA more effective, turning up the brain's natural brake. When specific binding sites for benzodiazepines were found in the brain in 1977, researchers wondered why the brain should have a receptor for a synthetic drug, and began to look for natural substances that might act on it. Two lines of evidence tie the system to anxiety. Drugs that act on the benzodiazepine site in the opposite direction, so-called inverse agonists, produce intense anxiety in animals and in human volunteers. And in a study by the British psychopharmacologist David J. Nutt and his colleagues (1990), an infusion of flumazenil, a drug that simply blocks the benzodiazepine site and has almost no effect in healthy people, provoked panic attacks in eight of ten patients with panic disorder and in none of ten controls, suggesting that the "set-point" of the receptor is altered in panic disorder. Barlow and Durand summarise the position: depleted levels of GABA are associated with increased anxiety, "although the relationship is not quite so direct." Pinel adds a caution about the animal research. The standard animal tests of anxiety (the elevated plus maze, in which rats choose between enclosed and exposed arms of a maze raised above the floor; the defensive-burying test; and the risk-assessment test) were all validated by showing that benzodiazepines reduce the measured anxiety. They may therefore be models of benzodiazepine-sensitive anxiety rather than of anxiety in general.

The second system is noradrenaline. The locus coeruleus ("blue spot"), a small nucleus in the pons of the brain stem, is the main source of the brain's noradrenaline, and its fibres reach almost the whole forebrain. Barlow and Durand describe it as part of a circuit involved in emergency and alarm reactions that may be related to panic. The classic evidence comes from monkeys. In 1976 the American psychiatrist D. Eugene Redmond Jr. and his colleagues electrically stimulated the locus coeruleus of stump-tailed macaques and observed behaviour like that which the animals show when they are threatened. In humans, DSM-5-TR lists yohimbine, a drug that increases the firing of noradrenergic neurons by blocking the alpha-2 receptors that normally restrain them, among the agents that provoke panic attacks far more readily in people with panic disorder than in others. Clonidine, which acts on the same receptors in the opposite direction, quietens the locus coeruleus.

The third system is serotonin (5-hydroxytryptamine, or 5-HT), made by the raphe nuclei of the brain stem and sent throughout the brain. Serotonin is best thought of as a modulator, a system that restrains and steadies other systems rather than producing anxiety or calm itself. The evidence again begins with treatment: the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) help most of the disorders in this chapter (Section 6.9). Jeffrey Gray and Neil McNaughton (1996) and the Brazilian researcher F. G. Graeff (1993) proposed that serotonin normally holds the brain's fight/flight system in check, so that a deficiency of serotonin activity releases it and makes panic more likely. The search for a "serotonin gene" has produced one famous lead: Klaus-Peter Lesch and his colleagues (1996) reported that a common variation in the gene for the serotonin transporter, the protein that SSRIs block, was associated with anxiety-related personality traits. The effect was small, and later studies have not been consistent. Serotonin's clearest role, as you will see, is in OCD.

The fourth system is hormonal. Increasingly, Barlow and Durand note, attention has focused on corticotropin-releasing factor (CRF), the hormone with which the hypothalamus starts the body's main stress response. CRF activates the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenocortical (HPA) axis, which ends in the release of cortisol from the adrenal glands (the axis itself is described in Book 1, Section 11.8.4). But CRF also acts within the brain, on exactly the regions implicated in anxiety: the limbic system, particularly the hippocampus and the amygdala; the locus coeruleus; the prefrontal cortex; and the dopamine system. And it interacts with the other three systems, GABA, serotonin and noradrenaline. That is why many researchers now see the CRF system as central to the expression of both anxiety and depression, and why the genes that make it easier to switch on are a target of current research.

Table 6.21 The neurotransmitter and hormone systems implicated in anxiety and obsessions.
SystemKey evidenceDrugs that act on itDisorders
GABA-benzodiazepineMain inhibitory transmitter; benzodiazepines enhance it at GABA-A receptors; low GABA goes with anxiety, though not simply; flumazenil provokes panic in panic disorderBenzodiazepines (diazepam, alprazolam); flumazenil blocks the siteAnxiety disorders broadly; little help in OCD
Noradrenaline (locus coeruleus)The brain stem's alarm centre; stimulation in monkeys mimics fear (Redmond and colleagues 1976); yohimbine provokes panicYohimbine raises firing; clonidine lowers itPanic and alarm reactions
Serotonin (5-HT)A modulator; SSRIs help most of these disorders; low serotonin may release the fight/flight system (Gray and McNaughton 1996; Graeff 1993)SSRIs; clomipramineOCD and the related disorders above all; panic, GAD, social anxiety
CRF and the HPA axisCRF starts the stress response and acts on the hippocampus, amygdala, locus coeruleus, prefrontal cortex and dopamine systemNo established anxiety drug yetAnxiety and depression generally
Dopamine and glutamateSecondary roles in OCD, for example antipsychotic augmentation in tic-related OCDAntipsychotics as add-on treatmentOCD (secondary)
Exam trap

The GABA-benzodiazepine system is the classic anxiety system; serotonin is the system most tied to OCD. Benzodiazepines do not replace GABA; they enhance its action at the GABA-A receptor. The locus coeruleus is in the brain stem (pons) and is the main source of noradrenaline, not of serotonin (that is the raphe nuclei) or dopamine. CRF is the hormone that starts the HPA axis.

The fear circuit: the amygdala, prefrontal control and Gray's two systems

Chemicals act in places, and the places that matter most for anxiety lie in the limbic system, the ring of structures that, in Barlow and Durand's phrase, acts as a mediator between the brain stem and the cortex. The brain stem monitors changes in bodily functions and relays potential danger signals upward; the cortex evaluates what is seen and heard and sends its own danger signals down. At the centre of this traffic sits the amygdala, an almond-shaped cluster of nuclei deep in each temporal lobe. Figure 6.11 shows its connections in simplified form.

Joseph LeDoux's research on the two routes by which a threat reaches the amygdala is taught in Book 1 (Section 11.4.5), and only one consequence of it is needed here. Sensory information travels from the thalamus to the amygdala by two roads: a fast, crude "low road" that goes directly from the thalamus to the amygdala, and a slower, more accurate "high road" that passes through the sensory cortex first. Because the low road bypasses the cortex, the amygdala can be set off by a threat before, or without, any conscious awareness of it. Imaging studies show the same thing in people: angry or fearful faces flashed too briefly to be consciously seen still activate the amygdala. This matters clinically. A panic attack that seems to come "out of the blue" may have had a trigger, a flicker of breathlessness, a faint smell, a glimpse of a crowded platform, that the person never consciously noticed.

From the amygdala, signals go out to the structures that produce the bodily and behavioural side of fear: the hypothalamus, which releases CRF and starts the HPA axis; the locus coeruleus, which floods the forebrain with noradrenaline and raises arousal and vigilance; the central (periaqueductal) grey of the midbrain, which organises freezing and flight; and the brain stem nuclei that drive the heart and breathing. Meanwhile, the prefrontal cortex, especially its ventromedial part, sends signals back down to the amygdala that can damp its response: this is how a person learns that a once-dangerous dog is now safe, and how she talks herself down from a scare. Brain-imaging studies of anxiety disorders, Barlow and Durand report, now agree on a pattern: the limbic system, including the amygdala, is overly responsive to stimulation or new information (abnormal bottom-up processing), while the controlling functions of the cortex that should down-regulate the hyperexcitable amygdala are deficient (abnormal top-down processing). Pinel adds two refinements. Unlike depression, in which the prefrontal cortex, hippocampus and amygdala appear to shrink, the anxiety disorders show no significant atrophy of these structures; the differences are differences of activity during emotional tasks. And each anxiety disorder seems to have its own pattern of functional connectivity between brain regions, so the "anxious brain" is not one brain but several.

Is anxiety one state or two? The British neuropsychologist Jeffrey Alan Gray (1934 to 2004), working mostly with animals, argued that it is two, with two circuits (Gray 1982, 1985; Gray and McNaughton 1996; McNaughton and Gray 2000). The first, the behavioural inhibition system (BIS), runs from the septal and hippocampal area of the limbic system to the frontal cortex. It is activated by signals from the brain stem of unexpected events, such as sudden changes in bodily functioning that might mean danger, and by danger signals that descend from the cortex when we see something threatening; it receives a big boost from the amygdala. When it is activated, Barlow and Durand write, "our tendency is to freeze, experience anxiety, and apprehensively evaluate the situation to confirm that danger is present". In Gray's wider theory of personality the BIS responds to signals of punishment, of frustrated reward and of novelty, and people with a highly reactive BIS are anxiety-prone. The second circuit, the fight/flight system (FFS), identified by Gray and by Graeff, originates in the brain stem and travels through several structures, including the amygdala (in the temporal lobe), the ventromedial nucleus of the hypothalamus and the central grey matter of the midbrain. When stimulated in animals it produces an immediate alarm-and-escape response that looks very much like panic in humans, and, as you saw, it may be held in check by serotonin. Table 6.22 sets the two systems side by side.

Table 6.22 Jeffrey Gray's two systems.
FeatureBehavioural inhibition systemFight/flight system
PathwaySeptal-hippocampal area to frontal cortex, boosted by the amygdalaBrain stem through the amygdala, ventromedial hypothalamus and central grey
Set off byUnexpected events; danger signals from the brain stem or the cortexImmediate threat; released partly by low serotonin activity
ResponseFreezing, anxiety, apprehensive checking for dangerImmediate alarm and escape
Human counterpartAnxiety and worryPanic

Gray's distinction fitted a discovery that had been made with drugs. In the early 1960s the American psychiatrist Donald Klein, whose drug studies you met in Section 6.2 and whose theory of panic comes in the next subsection, found that the antidepressant imipramine stopped the sudden attacks of his anxious patients while leaving their chronic anticipatory anxiety largely untouched. In 1964 he published the result under the title "Delineation of two drug-responsive anxiety syndromes": if one drug could block panic without relieving anxiety, panic and anxiety were probably different states with different machinery. This "pharmacological dissection" helped persuade the authors of DSM-III (1980) to separate panic disorder from generalised anxiety, the division you met in Sections 6.2 and 6.4.

Circuits are not fixed at birth. Barlow and Durand stress that factors in the environment can change the sensitivity of these circuits, making a person more or less susceptible to anxiety, and one study shows how ordinary such a factor can be. Jeffrey G. Johnson and his colleagues (2000), in the Children in the Community Study in New York State, interviewed 688 young people at an average age of 16 and again at an average age of 22. Heavy smoking in adolescence (20 or more cigarettes a day) predicted a far higher risk of anxiety disorders in early adulthood, even after the researchers had controlled for difficult childhood temperament, adolescent anxiety and depression, alcohol and drug use, and parental smoking and psychopathology: the odds of panic disorder were about 15 times higher, of generalised anxiety disorder about 5.5 times higher and of agoraphobia nearly 7 times higher. The reverse did not hold: anxious adolescents were no more likely than others to become heavy smokers. One explanation is that chronic exposure to nicotine, which itself produces bodily arousal, together with the breathing problems that smoking causes, increases biological vulnerability to anxiety and panic. DSM-5-TR now lists smoking as a risk factor for panic attacks and panic disorder. One of Johnson's co-authors was Donald Klein, and the link between breathing and panic was the centre of Klein's own theory.

Exam trap

In Gray's model the behavioural inhibition system (septal-hippocampal) produces anxiety and the fight/flight system (brain stem, amygdala, hypothalamus, central grey) produces panic. LeDoux's low road runs from the thalamus directly to the amygdala and is fast and unconscious; the high road passes through the cortex. The imaging pattern in anxiety disorders is too much bottom-up amygdala activity and too little top-down prefrontal control.

A false alarm for suffocation: the biology of panic

Ask people with panic disorder to describe their worst symptom and many will say that they could not breathe: a sudden air hunger, a smothering, a feeling that the next breath will not come. Ordinary fear, the fear of a man who meets a snake on the path, does not usually include a desperate shortage of breath. Why should panic? Donald F. Klein (1928 to 2019), the American psychiatrist whose imipramine studies had helped to define panic disorder, offered an answer in 1993 in a paper in Archives of General Psychiatry entitled "False suffocation alarms, spontaneous panics, and related conditions: an integrative hypothesis".

Klein's suffocation false alarm theory begins with evolution. Suffocation is one of the oldest threats to life, and natural selection should have equipped us with a monitor that watches for its early signs, chiefly a rise in the level of carbon dioxide in the blood (and, Klein suggested, of lactate), and an alarm that fires when the monitor detects them. The alarm's output is exactly what a suffocating animal needs: sudden respiratory distress and air hunger, rapid breathing that blows off carbon dioxide, a surge of panic and an overwhelming urge to escape to open air. In most people the monitor fires only when there is real danger, as when someone is trapped in a smoke-filled room. Klein's proposal was that in panic disorder the monitor is oversensitive: its threshold is set too low, so that it "misfires" at small and harmless rises in carbon dioxide and sounds a false alarm. A spontaneous panic attack, on this view, is not a fear reaction gone wrong but a suffocation alarm going off when there is nothing to suffocate on. Klein argued that the idea could explain several puzzles: why breathlessness is so prominent in panic but not in other fears; why panic attacks can strike during relaxation and even sleep, when breathing slows and carbon dioxide rises; why panic is associated with lung disease; and why it is linked with childhood separation anxiety. Klein also suggested that the chronic over-breathing seen in many panic patients is not a cause of the attacks but an attempt to keep carbon dioxide below the alarm's threshold.

Key study: provoking panic in the laboratory (1967 onwards)

The theory rests on a long line of challenge studies, in which a substance is given to people with and without panic disorder to see who panics. The first classic was published by Ferris N. Pitts Jr. and James N. McClure Jr. in 1967 under the title "Lactate metabolism in anxiety neurosis": when they infused sodium lactate into patients with what was then called anxiety neurosis, most of the patients had an anxiety attack, while few of the healthy controls did. Later researchers found the same with carbon dioxide: breathing air enriched with carbon dioxide, whether a 5 to 7 percent mixture for several minutes or a single deep breath of a much stronger one, provokes attacks far more readily in people with panic disorder. DSM-5-TR lists the agents that provoke panic in panic disorder to a much greater extent than in other people: sodium lactate, caffeine, isoproterenol, yohimbine, carbon dioxide and cholecystokinin. It adds that people with panic disorder, and also children with separation anxiety disorder, show "particularly enhanced sensitivity to respiratory stimulation using CO2-enriched air", and that breathing disturbances such as asthma are associated with panic disorder in history, comorbidity and family history. This respiratory sensitivity is the part of the evidence that shows some specificity for panic disorder, just as Klein's theory predicts, although DSM-5-TR stresses that none of these tests is useful for diagnosis.

The critique came from the psychologists. In 1989 William Sanderson, Ronald Rapee and David Barlow had 20 patients with panic disorder breathe 5.5 percent carbon dioxide for fifteen minutes. All were told that when a light in front of them was lit, they could reduce the carbon dioxide by turning a dial. For ten patients the light stayed on; for the other ten it never came on. In fact the dial did nothing, and everyone received the full mixture. Yet the patients who believed they had control reported fewer and milder panic symptoms, less anxiety and fewer catastrophic thoughts, and were significantly less likely to have a panic attack. The same chemical challenge, in other words, produced different amounts of panic depending on what the person believed.

What should we conclude? The challenge studies show that people with panic disorder are unusually sensitive to changes in their own breathing and body chemistry, and Klein's theory gives that sensitivity an elegant evolutionary explanation. But the fact that so many chemically unrelated substances (lactate, caffeine, yohimbine, cholecystokinin, carbon dioxide) all provoke panic suggests that what they share may be the sensations they produce rather than a single chemical lesion, and the Sanderson experiment shows that the meaning a person gives to those sensations helps decide whether they end in panic. That is the starting point of David Clark's cognitive theory of panic, the catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations, which Section 6.8 explains. The two accounts are not necessarily rivals: an oversensitive alarm may supply the sensations, and a catastrophic interpretation may turn them into an attack.

Clinical link

The challenge studies have a practical lesson. Before treating panic as purely psychological, make sure a physician has looked for medical conditions that produce similar sensations (thyroid disease, heart rhythm disorders, asthma and other lung disease), and ask about the client's caffeine intake and smoking, both of which raise the risk of attacks. The finding that harmless bodily sensations can be deliberately provoked is also the basis of interoceptive exposure in cognitive-behavioural treatment of panic, in which the client is helped to bring on breathlessness or dizziness on purpose and to learn that nothing terrible follows (Book 2, Sections 4.3 and 5.6.3).

Exam trap

"Suffocation alarm theories explain the clinical condition of" panic disorder. The distractors are unrelated conditions: Down syndrome is a chromosomal cause of intellectual disability (trisomy 21) and autism spectrum disorder a neurodevelopmental disorder (both in Chapter 3); delirium is an acute, fluctuating confusional state caused by a medical condition or a substance (Chapter 10). The theory is Donald Klein's (1993), not David Clark's: Klein's is a biological false alarm, Clark's a cognitive misinterpretation.

The obsessive brain: serotonin and the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical loop

The biology of OCD has its own story, and once again it began with a drug. In the 1960s and 1970s psychiatrists noticed that one tricyclic antidepressant, clomipramine, relieved obsessions and compulsions, while the other antidepressants of the day did little for them. Clomipramine differs from its relatives in one important way: it blocks the reuptake of serotonin much more powerfully. When the selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors arrived, fluoxetine, fluvoxamine and the rest, they too helped OCD. The comparison that made the case was a direct one. In a double-blind crossover trial published in 1989, Henrietta Leonard, Susan Swedo, Judith Rapoport and their colleagues at the National Institute of Mental Health gave children and adolescents with OCD clomipramine and desipramine, a tricyclic that acts mainly on noradrenaline, in turn; clomipramine was clearly superior. The same group found the same contrast in trichotillomania (Swedo and colleagues 1989). Meanwhile the benzodiazepines, which act on the GABA system and help many anxiety disorders, did little for obsessions. These findings produced the serotonin hypothesis of OCD: the idea that the central serotonin (5-HT) system is disturbed in the disorder.

Further evidence came from challenge studies of the kind used in panic. In 1987 Joseph Zohar, Thomas Insel and their colleagues gave patients with OCD and healthy controls a drug called m-chlorophenylpiperazine (mCPP), which stimulates serotonin receptors. In the patients, but not in the controls, it produced a temporary worsening of obsessions; and after a course of clomipramine treatment the patients' response to the same challenge was blunted (Zohar and colleagues 1988). The hypothesis has to be stated carefully, however. It is not simply that people with OCD lack serotonin, for if that were so, any drug that raised serotonin should work at once; in practice the serotonin reuptake inhibitors take weeks to help OCD and often need higher doses than in depression (Section 6.9). The better statement is that the serotonin system modulates the brain circuit that goes wrong in OCD, so that drugs acting on it can, over time, calm that circuit. And the aspirin caution applies here too: response to a drug does not prove what caused the disorder. Other transmitters play secondary parts. Dopamine is implicated because adding a low dose of an antipsychotic drug, which blocks dopamine, helps some patients who do not respond to a serotonin drug alone, especially those with tic-related OCD; glutamate, the brain's main excitatory transmitter and the working transmitter of the circuit described next, is the subject of much current research.

Exam trap

"Which biological system is implicated in obsessive-compulsive disorder?" The best single answer is the central serotonin (5-HT) system, because clomipramine and the SSRIs, which block serotonin reuptake, are the drugs that help OCD. GABA is the system of the anxiety disorders and of the benzodiazepines, which do little for obsessions. Glutamate and dopamine are implicated too, but secondarily (dopamine, for example, in antipsychotic augmentation for tic-related OCD), so they are not wrong in themselves; they are simply not the classic answer.

Where in the brain does the trouble lie? Imaging studies have converged on a loop of connections known as the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical circuit (CSTC circuit). DSM-5-TR names the orbitofrontal cortex (the underside of the frontal lobes, just above the eyes), the anterior cingulate cortex and the striatum as the regions most strongly implicated, with changes in frontolimbic, frontoparietal and cerebellar networks also reported. Barlow and Durand summarise the classic finding: although the size and structure of the brain are the same, people with OCD show increased activity in the orbital surface of the frontal cortex, increased activity in the cingulate gyrus and, to a lesser extent, in the caudate nucleus (part of the striatum), a circuit that runs from the orbital frontal cortex to parts of the thalamus. Activity in these regions is correlated, so that when one is active the others tend to be active too, and their pathways are rich in serotonin.

A simple way to understand the loop is shown in Figure 6.12. The orbitofrontal cortex and the anterior cingulate act as an error detector: they generate a signal that "something is wrong" (the hands are dirty, the door is unlocked, the picture is crooked) and a feeling that something must be done about it. Normally, the striatum, and the caudate nucleus in particular, acts as a filter or gate. It lets through the signals that deserve action and suppresses the rest, so that a passing thought about germs on a doorknob is noticed and dismissed. In OCD the filter fails. The signal passes through the striatum undamped, the thalamus relays it back to the cortex, the cortex fires the alarm again, and the person is caught in a loop: the worry about contamination keeps returning, and the urge to wash will not switch off. The compulsion brings relief for a while, but because the loop has not been broken, the alarm soon sounds again. This is a model, not a photograph of the brain, but it explains why obsessions feel so insistent, and why they are experienced as foreign, sticky signals rather than as the person's own considered judgements.

A single case shows how much this region matters. Thomas Insel (1992) retold a case first reported by Paul Eslinger and Antonio Damasio (1985): a man who had been a successful accountant, husband and father of two had an operation to remove a brain tumour. He recovered well, and his intelligence test scores were as high as ever, but within a year his business had failed and his family had separated from him. He was spending his days in lengthy, uncontrollable rituals of washing, dressing and rearranging things in the single room where he lived. The area of his brain damaged by the surgery was a small part of the orbital frontal cortex. Insel himself urged caution. It is one case; other people with the same lesion might react differently; and the orbitofrontal cortex is implicated in other anxiety and emotional disorders, so damage there may raise negative emotion in general rather than cause OCD in particular. There is also the problem of direction. An overactive circuit might be the result of hours of obsessive thinking rather than its cause, just as a runner's brain looks different from a walker's because of the running.

Key study: Baxter and the brain changed by behaviour therapy (1992)

If the circuit causes the symptoms, then successful treatment, of any kind, should change the circuit. Lewis R. Baxter Jr. and his colleagues at the University of California, Los Angeles, tested this with positron emission tomography (PET), which measures how much glucose different brain regions are using. They scanned patients with OCD before and after treatment with either the SSRI fluoxetine or behaviour therapy (exposure and response prevention). In patients who responded to either treatment, the activity of the head of the right caudate nucleus fell significantly; in non-responders and in healthy controls it did not change. The paper's title made the point: "Caudate glucose metabolic rate changes with both drug and behavior therapy for obsessive-compulsive disorder." The same team replicated the finding with a new group of patients (Schwartz and colleagues 1996).

The study, widely noted at the time, showed that a psychological treatment can change brain function as surely as a drug can, and that a brain difference is not proof that a disorder is "biological" in the sense of being beyond the reach of psychology. Its limits were the small samples and the fact that a change in the caudate after treatment still does not tell us what caused the disorder in the first place.

When every other treatment has failed, some patients with severe OCD are offered neurosurgery or deep brain stimulation aimed at parts of this circuit; those treatments, and the Indian law that governs psychosurgery, are taught in Section 6.9.

After a sore throat: PANDAS, tics and the biology of the related disorders

Ajay was a cheerful seven-year-old in Chennai, in Class 2, with no history of worry. In the second week of January he had a fever and a sore throat; the paediatrician took a throat swab, which grew streptococcus, and prescribed an antibiotic. Within days of the fever settling, Ajay changed almost overnight. He began washing his hands dozens of times a day, until the skin cracked. He refused to touch his school bag because it "had germs", insisted on going back through every doorway he had walked through, and screamed if his mother left the room. His eyes began to blink hard and repeatedly, and he cleared his throat with a little grunt every few seconds. His handwriting, which had been neat, became a scrawl. His parents, frightened, took him back to the paediatrician, who referred him to a child psychiatrist. Over the next weeks the symptoms eased; months later, after another throat infection, they returned.

Stories like Ajay's led Susan E. Swedo, an American researcher in paediatrics and neuropsychiatry, and her colleagues at the National Institute of Mental Health to propose a new syndrome. In 1998 they published a clinical description of their first 50 cases under the name PANDAS: paediatric autoimmune neuropsychiatric disorders associated with streptococcal infections. They gave five working criteria: the presence of OCD and/or a tic disorder; onset before puberty; an episodic course, with abrupt onset and dramatic flare-ups; an association between the onset or flare-ups and infection with group A beta-haemolytic streptococcus; and associated neurological abnormalities, such as hyperactivity or jerky, choreiform movements during flare-ups. The mean age at onset in their sample was 6.3 years for tics and 7.4 years for OCD, and the onset was typically "acute and dramatic". Barlow and Durand list the features that set the PANDAS group apart from other children with OCD: they are more likely to be boys; their symptoms begin dramatically, often with a fever or sore throat; they have full remissions between episodes; their symptoms remit during antibiotic treatment; they show evidence of past streptococcal infections; and they are noticeably clumsy.

The proposed mechanism borrowed from a disease that is not in doubt. Sydenham's chorea, the neurological form of rheumatic fever, is known to be caused by an autoimmune reaction: antibodies made against the streptococcus also attack the basal ganglia, the deep brain structures that include the striatum, producing involuntary movements and, often, obsessions and compulsions (Section 6.6.6). Swedo proposed that in PANDAS the same kind of misdirected immune attack on the basal ganglia, the very region at the heart of the CSTC circuit, produces OCD and tics without the other signs of rheumatic fever. The idea is attractive because it links a known infection, a known immune mechanism and a known brain circuit.

It has also been intensely controversial. There is no laboratory test that confirms PANDAS; streptococcal throat infections are so common in schoolchildren that a link with any later symptom is easy to find by chance; studies of antibodies and of antibiotic treatment have given inconsistent results; and critics worry that the label is applied too readily. Barlow and Durand note that the prevalence of the condition has yet to be determined. Partly in response, Swedo, James Leckman and Noel Rose (2012) proposed a broader category, PANS, which DSM-5-TR spells out as paediatric acute-onset neuropsychiatric syndrome: an abrupt, dramatic onset of obsessive-compulsive symptoms or severely restricted food intake, together with a range of other neuropsychiatric symptoms, whatever the trigger, because such acute onsets may follow many different infections or other insults. DSM-5-TR discusses both syndromes under obsessive-compulsive and related disorder due to another medical condition, and among the risk factors for OCD it notes that some children develop a sudden onset of obsessive-compulsive symptoms that has been associated with infectious agents and a post-infectious autoimmune syndrome. Whatever the final verdict, a child like Ajay, with an abrupt, dramatic onset of OCD and tics after an infection, needs a paediatric medical assessment as well as a psychological one.

Ajay's tics point to a second biological thread. Tic disorders and OCD are closely linked (the tic disorders and Tourette's disorder are taught in Chapter 3). DSM-5-TR reports that up to 30 percent of people with OCD have a lifetime tic disorder, most commonly men whose OCD began in childhood, and that these people differ from others with OCD in the themes of their symptoms, their comorbidity, their course and the way the disorder runs in their families; a triad of OCD, tics and attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder can also be seen in children. Barlow and Durand put the proportion of children and adolescents with OCD who have had a tic disorder at some point at 10 to 40 percent, and note that the obsessions in tic-related OCD are almost always about symmetry. This is why DSM-5 added the "tic-related" specifier to OCD (Section 6.5), and why tic-related OCD is the form in which the dopamine-blocking drugs are most useful.

What of the other members of the OCRD family? Their biology is much less studied, but what there is points back towards OCD. Body dysmorphic disorder is commoner in the first-degree relatives of people with OCD; its heritability, 37 to 49 percent, reflects both a genetic vulnerability shared with OCD and influences specific to BDD; and one imaging study found abnormal brain functioning similar to that in OCD (Rauch and colleagues 2003, cited by Barlow and Durand). Hoarding is strongly familial, with about half its variability genetic and indecisiveness common in relatives; a study by David Tolin and colleagues (2012) found that, when deciding whether to keep or discard their own possessions, people with hoarding disorder showed abnormal activity in brain regions involved in judging the emotional significance of an object, unlike people with OCD; and, as Section 6.6.4 noted, damage to the ventromedial prefrontal and cingulate cortex can produce hoarding in people who never hoarded before. Trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are commoner in people with OCD and their first-degree relatives, and there is some evidence of a genetic vulnerability to each; in one study Stephan Züchner and his colleagues (2006) found rare mutations in a gene called SLITRK1 in a small number of people with trichotillomania. And the finding that clomipramine helped hair pulling more than desipramine did suggests that serotonin plays a part there too.

Exam trap

PANDAS (Susan Swedo and colleagues, 1998) stands for paediatric autoimmune neuropsychiatric disorders associated with streptococcal infections: a sudden childhood onset of OCD or tics after a group A streptococcal infection, thought to be an autoimmune attack on the basal ganglia, on the model of Sydenham's chorea. PANS (2012) is paediatric acute-onset neuropsychiatric syndrome, the broader category with any trigger; some textbooks, Barlow and Durand among them, expand it differently, but DSM-5-TR's "acute-onset" is the standard form. Finally, none of the evidence in this section makes anxiety or OCD purely biological: the heritabilities are moderate, temperament only shifts probabilities, and behaviour therapy changes the brain. Section 6.8's triple vulnerability theory brings the biological and psychological strands together.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  2. Enrico Morselli's name for body dysmorphic disorder, coined in 1891, was .
  3. The BDD specifier for a man convinced that his body is too small or not muscular enough is "with ".
  4. In hoarding disorder, the clutter must congest living areas, unless third parties have cleared them.
  5. Trichotillomania was named in 1889 by the French dermatologist .
  6. Besides OCD, BDD, hoarding and the body-focused repetitive behaviour disorders, ICD-11's obsessive-compulsive or related group contains olfactory reference disorder and .

  8. Jeffrey Gray's circuit running from the septal-hippocampal area to the frontal cortex, which produces anxiety and cautious checking, is the system.

Answers: (1) somatoform, (2) dysmorphophobia, (3) muscle dysmorphia, (4) active, (5) François Henri Hallopeau, (6) hypochondriasis (health anxiety), (7) four, (8) behavioural inhibition, (9) suffocation, (10) caudate.

6.8 Learning, Cognition and Conflict: Psychological ExplanationsHigh yield · 7 Qs

Go back for a moment to the small boy in Vienna who would not go out into the street because a horse might bite him. Section 6.7 asked what Hans might have inherited: an anxious temperament, a jumpy alarm system, a brain that learns fear easily. That is only half the story. Two children with the same genes can grow up with very different fears, and the same child can lose a fear as quickly as he gained it. Something in experience and in thought decides which fear a vulnerable person develops, what it attaches to, and why it lasts long after any real danger has passed. Three traditions have tried to say what that something is. Learning theorists say the fear was learned and is kept alive by avoidance. Cognitive theorists say it lives in the way the person interprets danger, the body and his own mind. Psychodynamic theorists say it is a disguised expression of an inner conflict. This section takes each in turn, tests it against the evidence, and ends with the model that most researchers now use to tie the strands together: David Barlow's triple vulnerability theory. How these ideas are turned into therapy (exposure, cognitive therapy, psychoanalysis) is the business of Book 2; here they are explanations of cause.

Fear as a learned response: conditioning and Mowrer's two-factor theory

The behaviourists' starting point was a famous and ethically troubling experiment. In 1920 John B. Watson and Rosalie Rayner showed an 11-month-old infant, Albert, a white rat; each time he reached for it, a steel bar was struck with a hammer behind his head. After a handful of pairings Albert cried and crawled away at the sight of the rat alone, and the fear spread to a rabbit, a dog and a fur coat. Book 1, Section 6.4.1 tells the story in full. For this chapter, what matters is the vocabulary it gave clinicians (Book 1, Section 6.2.2). The loud noise was an unconditioned stimulus (UCS): it produces fear without any learning. The startle and crying it produced was the unconditioned response (UCR), an unlearned reflex. The rat, once neutral, became a conditioned stimulus (CS), and the fear it now produced was a conditioned response (CR). Put a phobia into the same frame and it looks simple. A boy in Pune is bitten by a street dog (UCS), feels terror (UCR), and from then on the sight of any dog (CS) makes his heart race (CR). A phobia, on this view, is a conditioned emotional response that has spread from the original stimulus to everything that resembles it.

There was an obvious problem, and it troubled learning theorists for years. Classical conditioning has a built-in cure. If a conditioned stimulus is presented again and again without the unconditioned stimulus, the conditioned response weakens and disappears; this is extinction. The boy meets dozens of dogs in the months after his bite, almost none of which bite him. By the laws of conditioning his fear should melt away. In real phobias it does not. People keep a fear of dogs, lifts or bridges for decades, even though the feared disaster almost never repeats itself. Why does extinction fail?

The answer that became standard was given by the American learning theorist Orval Hobart Mowrer (1907 to 1982), usually cited as O. Hobart Mowrer. In 1947 he proposed that a phobia is built by two different kinds of learning working one after the other, an account known as the two-factor theory (or two-process theory). The first factor is classical conditioning: a neutral stimulus is paired with something frightening and becomes a conditioned stimulus for fear. The second factor is operant conditioning (Book 1, Section 6.5.5). Once the dog has become frightening, the boy learns to cross the road, to walk the long way to school, to stay indoors when the colony dogs are out. Each of these acts is followed by a fall in fear. A fall in an unpleasant state is a powerful reinforcer, and because the reinforcement comes from removing something aversive rather than adding something pleasant, it is negative reinforcement (Book 1, Section 6.5.3). The avoidance therefore grows stronger with every use.

Here is the clever part. The very act that brings relief also stops extinction from happening. Extinction needs the person to meet the conditioned stimulus and stay with it long enough to discover that nothing terrible follows. The avoider never stays. He crosses the road before the dog is near, so he never learns that most dogs ignore passing boys, and the fear is preserved intact, ready for the next encounter. Mowrer went on, in 1948, to use the same logic to explain what he called the neurotic paradox: why people persist in self-defeating behaviour that costs them jobs, friendships and happiness. The answer is timing. The relief of avoidance is immediate and certain; its costs come later and are spread thin, and immediate consequences control behaviour far more strongly than delayed ones (Book 1, Section 6.9.7).

Two kinds of avoidance learning are worth separating because they will matter in a moment. An escape response ends an aversive state that has already begun: the woman who is already in a crowded lift and gets out at the next floor. An avoidance response prevents the aversive state from beginning at all: the woman who takes the stairs. Both are negatively reinforced, and both protect the fear from extinction. Clinicians add a third, subtler family, the safety behaviours: actions taken inside a feared situation to prevent the feared catastrophe, such as gripping a handrail, sitting near the door of the cinema hall, carrying a bottle of water or a tablet, or going out only with a trusted companion. Because the catastrophe never happens, the person credits the safety behaviour rather than learning that the situation was safe (Book 2, Section 5.6.2).

Exam trap

"Two-factor theory" in a question on phobias or OCD means Mowrer's two-factor (two-process) theory of avoidance, 1947: classical conditioning creates the fear, operant negative reinforcement of avoidance maintains it. Do not confuse it with Stanley Schachter and Jerome Singer's two-factor theory of emotion (arousal plus a cognitive label, 1962) or with Frederick Herzberg's two-factor theory of job motivation. And avoidance is maintained by negative reinforcement (an unpleasant state is removed), not by punishment: punishment weakens behaviour, whereas negative reinforcement strengthens it.

Compulsions as escape: the learning account of OCD

Hema is a 27-year-old bank clerk in Coimbatore. Every working day she handles hundreds of currency notes that have passed, she cannot stop thinking, through countless unwashed hands. On a bad day the thought arrives each time she touches a bundle: "There are germs on this; I will carry them home to my father, who has diabetes, and he will fall ill because of me." Her anxiety climbs until she can bear it no longer. She goes to the washroom and scrubs her hands, fingers first, then palms, then wrists, always in the same order, until they feel clean; if she loses count she starts again. The relief is immediate. Twenty minutes later another bundle arrives, and so does the thought. At home she washes again before she can enter the kitchen, and her evening bath has grown to over an hour. Her hands are cracked and bleeding.

Mowrer's two factors map onto Hema's day with uncomfortable precision. The thought of contamination, and the notes that trigger it, have become conditioned stimuli for anxiety (factor one). Washing ends the anxiety, and the relief negatively reinforces the washing (factor two). Because she always washes, she never discovers two things: that her anxiety would have subsided on its own if she had waited, and that her father would not have fallen ill. So the obsession returns at full strength and the ritual is needed again. In learning terms, a compulsion is simply an escape or avoidance response; washing after touching is escape, and washing in advance "just in case" before entering the kitchen is avoidance. Her refusal to touch the washroom door handle without a tissue is passive avoidance of the trigger, and it works the same way. Figure 6.13 sets the phobic loop and the obsessive-compulsive loop side by side, because they are the same machine fed with different material.

This account made a prediction that could be tested in the laboratory, and in the late 1960s and 1970s a group at the Institute of Psychiatry in London set out to test it. If compulsions are escape responses, then contact with a feared contaminant should drive anxiety up, and carrying out the ritual should bring it down.

Key study: contamination and washing (Hodgson and Rachman, 1972)

Stanley Rachman (1934 to 2021), a South African-born psychologist who worked mainly on OCD and the other anxiety disorders, and his colleague R. J. Hodgson asked obsessional patients whose rituals were washing to touch an object they regarded as contaminated, and then measured their subjective discomfort and urge to wash. Touching the contaminant made the discomfort rise sharply; being allowed to wash then brought it down. The pattern was just what the two-factor theory predicted, and companion experiments soon extended the analysis to checking rituals (Röper, Rachman and Hodgson, 1973). A further study asked a different question: what happens if the patient touches the contaminant and does not wash? Rachman, Padmal de Silva and G. Röper (1976) found that the discomfort and the urge to wash declined by themselves, more slowly than after a ritual but just as surely. That finding is the scientific foundation of exposure and response prevention: if the ritual is blocked, the anxiety extinguishes (Book 2, Section 4.3.5).

Now look closely at the words examiners use about compulsions, because two real questions turn on them. In Hema's case the conditioned stimulus is the contaminating thought or object: it comes first and signals anxiety. The washing is a response to that stimulus, so it cannot be a conditioned stimulus. An unconditioned response is an unlearned reflex, such as a startle at a loud noise, a flinch from a hot pan or salivation when food is in the mouth; Hema's elaborate, ordered, rule-bound washing is plainly learned, so it is not an unconditioned response. The washing is reinforced, but not positively: positive reinforcement adds something pleasant, and washing gives Hema no pleasure at all. It takes away distress, so it is negatively reinforced. Nor is a compulsion involuntary. Hema feels driven to wash and resists only with great difficulty, but she can delay the ritual, and in therapy she will learn to refrain from it; this is what separates a compulsion from a tic, which is sudden, rapid and largely involuntary (Chapter 3). Finally, "performance" is not a pattern of behaviour in learning theory at all; the word belongs to the distinction between learning something and showing it in performance, which is a different idea. The compulsive act, in the language of learning theory, is an escape (or avoidance) behaviour, a safety behaviour that works in the short run and keeps the disorder going in the long run.

Clinical link

Patients like Hema often say, "But the washing is the only thing that helps." The two-factor theory lets a clinician agree and then turn the point around: the washing helps for twenty minutes, and that is exactly why the thought keeps coming back. Drawing Figure 6.13(b) with the patient, using her own thoughts and rituals, is usually the first step in preparing her for exposure and response prevention, in which she touches the notes and does not wash, and watches the anxiety rise, peak and fall by itself (Book 2, Section 4.3.5).

Exam trap

A client washes to neutralise the anxiety of contamination thoughts: the washing is best seen as an avoidance response or safety behaviour (real paper, 2024). It is not a conditioned stimulus (the contaminating thought or object is the stimulus), not an unconditioned response (an unlearned reflex such as a startle) and not maintained by positive reinforcement (it removes distress; it adds nothing pleasant). In a second real question (IHBAS 2025), learning theory explains compulsive acts as patterns of escape behaviour. "Conditioning" is tempting and is defensible as the general mechanism (classical for the anxiety, operant for the ritual), but the question asks what pattern the acts show, and that pattern is escape from anxiety. "Involuntary" is wrong because compulsions can be delayed and resisted, unlike tics, and "performance" is not a learning-theory pattern of behaviour.

Why every fright does not become a phobia: preparedness and the three pathways

The two-factor theory explains why fears last. It is much less good at explaining how they start, and by the 1970s three awkward facts had piled up against the idea that every phobia begins with a frightening conditioning experience.

The first awkward fact is that many people with phobias remember no frightening event at all. Section 6.3 noted that many cannot recall how the phobia began. Some may have forgotten, but it strains belief that the many adults with a lifelong fear of snakes, in countries where few of them have ever met a snake, were all bitten in childhood. The second fact runs the other way: many people who do have a frightening experience never become phobic. When the American psychologist Peter DiNardo and his colleagues (1988) studied people with a phobia of dogs and a matched group without one, about half of the phobics recalled a frightening encounter with a dog, usually a bite. So did about half of the non-phobic group. The same terrifying event had produced a phobia in some people and nothing lasting in others. Studies of people with a phobia of driving found the same pattern: only about half of those who could date the onset had had an accident or other real fright on the road (Munjack 1984, from Barlow). And Rachman, reviewing how British civilians coped with the repeated air raids of the Second World War, concluded that short-lived fear was common but "surprisingly few persistent phobic reactions emerged" (Rachman 1991, from Barlow). If conditioning were the whole story, the bombed cities should have been full of phobias.

The third fact is the strangest. If any stimulus paired with pain can become a conditioned stimulus, phobias should be spread across all the dangerous things of modern life: electric sockets, cars, hammers, motorcycles, cigarettes. They are not. Phobias cluster around a short list of objects and situations, snakes, spiders, dogs, heights, deep water, darkness, blood and enclosed spaces, many of which harm very few people today.

The American psychologist Martin Seligman (born 1942) proposed an explanation in a 1971 paper, "Phobias and preparedness". Through evolution, he argued, our species has become biologically prepared to learn fear of the objects and situations that threatened our ancestors. For an early human, a quick, lasting, unthinking fear of snakes, cliffs and strangers was worth having; a fear of flowers was not. Seligman's idea of preparedness (or prepared learning) explains four features of phobias that ordinary conditioning does not: they are selective (they attach to some stimuli far more readily than others); they can be acquired in a single pairing or even without one; they resist extinction; and they are irrational in the sense that knowing the object is harmless does not dissolve them. You met the same principle in Book 1, Section 6.4.6, where John Garcia's rats learned in one trial to avoid a taste that was followed hours later by sickness.

Preparedness has been tested directly. The Swedish psychologist Arne Öhman and his colleagues conditioned mild fear, measured by the skin conductance response, by pairing pictures with a mild electric shock. Fear conditioned to pictures of snakes and spiders resisted extinction far better than fear conditioned to pictures of flowers or mushrooms. In 1978 Öhman and Ulf Dimberg found the same for faces: a conditioned response to an angry face outlasted one to a happy or neutral face. We seem to be prepared to fear angry, rejecting faces as well as snakes, which is one reason preparedness is invoked for social anxiety disorder (Section 6.8.6).

Key study: the monkeys who learned to fear snakes (Mineka and colleagues, 1984; Cook and Mineka, 1989)

Rhesus monkeys born in the wild are afraid of snakes; monkeys born and raised in the laboratory are not. The American psychologist Susan Mineka and her colleagues let laboratory-reared monkeys watch wild-reared monkeys reacting fearfully to a snake. After watching, the observers had acquired an intense and lasting fear of snakes themselves (Mineka, Davidson, Cook and Keir, 1984). In 1989 Michael Cook and Mineka asked whether a monkey could learn to fear anything this way. They edited videotapes so that the same fearful model monkey appeared to be reacting either to toy snakes and a toy crocodile or to flowers and a toy rabbit. Observers who watched the fear directed at snakes and crocodiles learned it; observers who watched exactly the same fear directed at flowers and the rabbit learned nothing. The conclusion: fear can be learned by watching, and what is learned depends on what evolution has prepared the learner to fear. The usual caution applies: these are monkeys, and the human evidence is less direct.

The second answer to the awkward facts came from Stanley Rachman, who in 1977 published a critical examination of the conditioning theory of fear and proposed that fears are acquired by three pathways. The first is direct conditioning, the classic route: the dog bite, the hours trapped in a lift, the choking episode (almost every person with a choking phobia has had a choking experience). The second is vicarious learning, or observational learning: acquiring a fear by watching someone else's fear or someone else's misfortune (Book 1, Section 6.8). The Swedish psychologist Lars-Göran Öst (1985) described an adolescent boy who sat in the waiting room of the school dentist, partly seeing and fully hearing his friend being treated. The friend jerked with pain, and the drill punctured his cheek. The boy in the waiting room ran out and developed a severe, long-lasting fear of dentistry, although nothing had happened to him. Closer to home, a child who has watched her mother scream and climb onto a chair every time a house lizard appears on the wall needs no bite to learn that lizards are terrifying. The third pathway is information transmission (instruction): being told, again and again, that something is dangerous. Öst described a woman with an extremely severe phobia of snakes who had never encountered one. All through childhood she had been warned about snakes in the long grass and made to wear high rubber boots, which she still wore even when walking down a city street.

Rachman's three pathways do not replace conditioning; they widen it, and they explain why so many phobics cannot recall a frightening event: no event is needed. They still leave DiNardo's question unanswered. Why did half of the people bitten by dogs go on with their lives? David H. Barlow (born 1942), whose integrated model closes this section, answers with a distinction between three kinds of alarm. A true alarm is the fear response to real danger: the bite, the near-accident. A false alarm is the same intense fear when there is no danger at all: an unexpected panic attack, often during a period of stress. A learned alarm is a true or false alarm that has become conditioned to the cues present when it happened. Those cues can be external (the bus, the bridge, the cinema hall) or internal, such as a racing heart or a change in breathing. This last idea, that the body's own sensations can become conditioned stimuli, is called interoceptive conditioning, and it is central to modern learning accounts of panic (Bouton, Mineka and Barlow, 2001). The cues need not even be conscious: experiments with animals show that fear signals can travel from the senses to the amygdala without passing through the cortex, the seat of awareness (Section 6.7; Book 1, Section 11.4.5), which is why a person with panic disorder often cannot say what set off an attack. What turns an alarm into a disorder, Barlow argues, is not the alarm itself but whether the person develops anxiety about it happening again. The dog-bitten people who did not become phobic were those who did not start dreading the next dog (Section 6.8.11).

Exam trap

Preparedness is Martin Seligman's (1971) idea that evolution makes some fears easy to learn and hard to lose; do not attach it to Seligman's other famous idea, learned helplessness (a model of depression, Chapter 5), or credit it to Garcia, whose taste-aversion work is the classic animal example of prepared learning. Rachman's (1977) three pathways are direct conditioning, vicarious (observational) learning and information or instruction: three, not four, and "information" means being told, not "cognitive appraisal". Barlow's alarms come in three kinds: true (real danger), false (no danger) and learned (conditioned to cues).

The anxious mind: overestimated danger, threat-hunting attention and catastrophic thinking

Learning theory treats fear as something that happens to a person. Cognitive theory asks what the person is thinking while it happens. Its founding figure, Aaron Temkin Beck (1921 to 2021), argued that each emotional disorder has its own characteristic thought content: loss and failure in depression, danger in anxiety (Book 2, Section 5.3). In Anxiety Disorders and Phobias: A Cognitive Perspective (1985), written with Gary Emery and Ruth Greenberg, Beck described the anxious person as caught in a systematic error of appraisal. He overestimates the probability that something bad will happen, overestimates how bad it would be, underestimates his own ability to cope, and underestimates the help that others would give. Cognitive therapists later summarised this as an "anxiety equation", usually credited to the British psychologist Paul Salkovskis (1996; you will meet his model of OCD in Section 6.8.7): anxiety rises with the perceived probability and cost of the danger and falls with the perceived ability to cope and the perceived chance of rescue. Change any term in the equation and the anxiety changes.

These appraisals are expressed through habitual distortions of thinking. Beck's full list belongs to Book 2, Section 5.3.4, but one of them, catastrophising (catastrophic thinking), is so central to anxiety that examiners ask about it directly. Catastrophising means expecting the worst possible outcome and treating it as both likely and unbearable. It usually takes the form of a chain, each link more terrible than the last. A postgraduate applicant waiting for his viva thinks: "If I fumble the first question, they will think I know nothing; I will lose the seat; I will have wasted my parents' money; I will never get into any programme; my life will be finished." Each step feels like a certainty, and the anxiety is driven by the last link, not the first. Table 6.23 sets catastrophising beside the three distortions that most often appear with it as wrong options.

Table 6.23 Catastrophising and three distortions often set beside it in exam options (Beck's full list is in Book 2, Section 5.3.4).
Thinking errorWhat it meansExample before a vivaMost typical of
Catastrophising (catastrophic thinking)Expecting the worst to happen and treating it as certain and unbearable"If I fumble one answer I will fail, lose the seat and shame my family"Anxiety: panic, GAD, health anxiety
Dichotomous (all-or-nothing) thinkingSorting every experience into one of two extremes, wholly good or wholly bad"Either I top the list or I am a complete failure"Perfectionism, depression, personality disorders
Selective abstraction (mental filter)Focusing on one negative detail and ignoring all the positive onesRemembering one examiner's frown and none of the nodsDepression
PersonalisationTaking events personally, blaming oneself for things one did not cause"The examiner looked tired; I must have bored him"Depression, social anxiety

Distorted thinking is not only a matter of conscious sentences. Anxious people also differ in what they notice, often before they are aware of noticing it. In the emotional Stroop task, words printed in coloured ink are flashed on a screen and the participant must name the ink colour as fast as possible while ignoring the word. If a word grabs attention because of its meaning, colour naming slows down. Colin MacLeod and Andrew Mathews (1991) presented threatening words for only 20 milliseconds, too briefly to be consciously read, and people with GAD were still slower than non-anxious people to name the colours of the threat words. In the dot-probe task (MacLeod, Mathews and Tata, 1986), a threatening and a neutral word appear together, one above the other, and then a dot replaces one of them; anxious participants detect the dot faster when it replaces the threat word, which shows that their attention was already there. This automatic pull towards threat is called attentional bias. It means that the anxious person lives in a world that really does look more dangerous, because the dangerous parts are the parts he sees.

Exam trap

"Catastrophic thinking is common to several anxiety disorders. The term describes:" the answer is worrying that the worst will always happen (RML 2021). The other options are other distortions: categorising experiences as extremely positive or extremely negative is dichotomous (all-or-nothing) thinking; focusing on negative events and ignoring positive ones is selective abstraction (the mental filter); taking things very personally is personalisation. Catastrophising is about the future outcome; the other three are about how the present is sorted, filtered or owned.

Panic as a misreading of the body: Clark's model and anxiety sensitivity

Nikhil is a 29-year-old software engineer in Bengaluru. Near the end of a product launch he had been sleeping four hours a night and drinking coffee all day. At two in the morning, at his desk, he felt his heart skip and then race. His uncle had died of a heart attack at 45. "This is it," he thought, "my heart is giving out." His chest tightened, he could not get his breath, his hands tingled, and the room seemed to move away from him. Certain he was dying, he woke his flatmate, who drove him to a hospital emergency department. The electrocardiogram was normal, and the doctor told him it was "only anxiety". He did not believe it. Over the next months he had several more attacks, began to check his pulse many times a day, gave up the gym and stopped climbing stairs quickly.

Nothing about Nikhil's heart was abnormal; what was abnormal was what he made of it. The British clinical psychologist David M. Clark built this observation into the most influential psychological theory of panic. In "A cognitive approach to panic" (1986), Clark proposed that panic attacks result from the catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations: perceiving normal or anxiety-produced sensations as signs of an immediate disaster. The sequence is a vicious circle (Figure 6.14). A trigger, which may be external (a crowded bus) or internal (a skipped beat after too much coffee, the breathlessness of a fast climb), is perceived as a threat. Perceived threat produces apprehension. Apprehension produces bodily sensations through the sympathetic nervous system: the heart pounds, breathing quickens, the head swims.
