If those sensations are interpreted catastrophically ("I am having a heart attack"), the perceived threat rises, apprehension increases, the sensations grow stronger, and each turn of the circle seems to confirm the catastrophe until the spiral peaks in a full panic attack. Clark described the typical pairings: palpitations read as a heart attack, breathlessness as suffocation, dizziness as fainting or losing control, and feelings of unreality as going mad.

Clark's model explains several puzzles at once. It explains "unexpected" attacks: they are not truly out of the blue but triggered by internal sensations the person barely noticed. It explains why people with panic disorder become hypervigilant about their bodies (Nikhil's pulse-checking): the more one monitors the heart, the more irregularities one finds. It explains the persistence of the disorder through safety behaviours: each time the heart attack fails to happen, Nikhil credits sitting down, or leaving the room, or the tablet in his pocket, and the belief survives. And it explains why the same laboratory challenge can provoke panic in one person and not another. Section 6.7 describes how inhaling carbon dioxide or receiving an infusion of sodium lactate sets off attacks in people with panic disorder far more readily than in others, the evidence for Donald Klein's suffocation false alarm theory. The cognitive reply is that the meaning of the sensations matters too. In the experiment of William Sanderson, Ronald Rapee and David Barlow (1989), told in Section 6.7.5, patients who wrongly believed they could turn down the carbon dioxide they were breathing had fewer catastrophic thoughts and significantly fewer panic attacks than patients given the same gas without that belief. Same gas, different belief, different outcome. The biological and cognitive theories are best seen as complementary: a sensitive alarm supplies the sensations, and the interpretation decides whether they spiral into panic. The therapy built on Clark's model, which teaches the patient to test his catastrophic interpretations, is described in Book 2, Section 5.6.3.

Why do some people interpret bodily sensations catastrophically and others shrug them off? One answer is a trait. In 1985 the American psychologists Steven Reiss and Richard J. McNally introduced the idea of anxiety sensitivity: a lasting disposition to believe that the sensations of anxiety are harmful, that a racing heart may mean a heart attack, that a wave of anxiety may mean losing one's mind, that visible trembling will lead to humiliation. It is measured with the Anxiety Sensitivity Index (Reiss, Peterson, Gursky and McNally, 1986; Assessment book). People who score high are more likely to go on to have panic attacks, and DSM-5-TR lists anxiety sensitivity, alongside negative affectivity and behavioural inhibition, among the temperamental risk factors for panic attacks and panic disorder. Where does it come from? Barlow's group points to a specific psychological vulnerability learned in childhood. In a prospective study that followed more than a thousand young women aged 18 to 24 for about a year and a half, those with a history of physical illness who were anxious about their health went on to develop panic disorder rather than other anxiety disorders such as social phobia (Rudaz, Craske, Becker, Ledermann and Margraf, 2010). They had learned, it seems, that unexplained bodily sensations can be dangerous.

Exam trap

Four panic ideas, four authors, four years. Fear of fear: Alan Goldstein and Dianne Chambless, 1978 (a learned fear of the sensations of anxiety that develops after attacks, Section 6.2). Anxiety sensitivity: Steven Reiss and Richard McNally, 1985 (a lasting trait that predates attacks and predicts them). Catastrophic misinterpretation: David M. Clark, 1986 (what happens in the moment of an attack). Suffocation false alarm: Donald Klein, 1993 (a biological alarm that misfires, Section 6.7). Clark's model is cognitive, Klein's biological; neither is about GAD, whose worry is taught in the next subsection.

Worry and scrutiny: cognitive models of GAD and social anxiety

Section 6.4 described a curious finding about people with GAD: under stress their heart rate, blood pressure, skin conductance and breathing change less than those of people with panic disorder, so that they have been called autonomic restrictors, and yet they are chronically tense. The American psychologist Thomas D. Borkovec and his colleagues explained the paradox with an avoidance theory of worry (Borkovec, Alcaine and Behar, 2004). Worry, Borkovec noticed, is mostly verbal: an anxious stream of sentences about what might go wrong, with intense activity in the frontal lobes but little vivid imagery. Images of a threat (picturing the accident, the hospital, the funeral) produce strong emotion and strong bodily arousal; thinking about the same threat in words produces much less. By worrying in words, the person keeps the frightening images, and the arousal they would bring, at a distance. That small relief negatively reinforces worrying, exactly as escape reinforces a phobic's avoidance. The cost is the same too. Because the threat is never fully pictured and felt, it is never emotionally processed, the problems are never solved, and the worrier never adapts; he simply goes on worrying, with tight muscles and a restricted, inflexible autonomic system. On this view, worry does for the person with GAD what avoidance does for the person with a phobia.

The British clinical psychologist Adrian Wells asked a different question: why does the person keep using a strategy that makes him miserable? His answer lies in metacognition, thinking about thinking. In Wells's metacognitive model of GAD (1995), people start with positive beliefs about worry: "Worrying helps me cope", "If I worry I will be prepared", "Worrying keeps bad things from happening." These beliefs lead them to use worry about everyday matters, which Wells calls Type 1 worry, as a way of coping. Over time a second set of beliefs develops, negative beliefs about worry itself: "My worrying is uncontrollable", "Worrying this much will make me ill or drive me mad." Now the person worries about his worrying. This Type 2 worry, or meta-worry, is in Wells's view what turns ordinary worry into GAD, and the person's attempts to control it (suppressing thoughts, seeking reassurance, avoiding the news) only feed it. The therapy built on the model is taught in Book 2, Section 5.10.3.

A third account comes from the Canadian psychologist Michel Dugas and his colleagues at Laval University in Quebec. They proposed that the core of GAD is intolerance of uncertainty: a tendency to find uncertain situations unacceptable and threatening in themselves, whatever the actual odds of a bad outcome. For such a person, "I do not know for sure that my daughter's bus has arrived" feels almost as bad as "the bus has crashed". Worry is an attempt to remove the uncertainty by mentally rehearsing every possibility, and since the future is always uncertain, the worry never ends. In a preliminary test of their model, which also included positive beliefs about worry, poor problem orientation and cognitive avoidance, Dugas, Gagnon, Ladouceur and Freeston (1998) compared 24 patients with GAD and 20 non-clinical participants: intolerance of uncertainty was the factor that best separated the two groups, and the four variables together classified 82 percent of participants correctly. Intolerance of uncertainty is now recognised as a feature of other disorders too, including OCD.

Social anxiety disorder has its own cognitive model, set out in 1995 by David M. Clark and Adrian Wells (Book 2, Section 5.6.4 explains the therapy built on it). Picture a young lecturer in Kolkata about to give her first departmental seminar. She believes she must always sound brilliant and that if she blushes people will think she is weak. As she stands up, she feels her face grow hot, and her attention turns inward. This self-focused attention is the heart of the model: instead of watching her audience, she watches herself, and she builds a picture of how she looks from how she feels. Because her face feels as if it is burning, she "sees" herself as scarlet and visibly shaking, an image of herself as others might see her, taken from an observer's perspective and treated as accurate. To get through, she uses safety behaviours: she avoids eye contact, reads from her notes, grips the lectern, says as little as possible in the discussion. These make her look aloof and stiff, which can provoke the very coolness she fears, and they stop her discovering that her blush was barely visible. Before the seminar she rehearsed every way it could go wrong (anticipatory processing); afterwards she replays it in a long "post-mortem", reviewing her performance through the same distorted self-image (post-event processing), so that the memory of the event is filed as another failure.

Two further strands support this picture. Preparedness again: Öhman and Dimberg's angry faces (Section 6.8.3) suggest that we are prepared to fear signs of hostility and rejection, and socially anxious people recognise angry faces more quickly than others do (Mogg, Philippot and Bradley, 2004). And learning: in one Canadian sample, 92 percent of adults with social phobia reported severe teasing or bullying in childhood, against 35 to 50 percent of those with other anxiety disorders (McCabe and colleagues, 2003, from Barlow), though memories of this kind are retrospective self-reports and must be read with care.

Exam trap

Match the model to its author. Avoidance theory of worry: Thomas Borkovec (worry is verbal and damps the imagery and arousal of fear). Metacognitive model, with Type 2 worry (worry about worry): Adrian Wells. Intolerance of uncertainty: Michel Dugas and colleagues. Self-focused attention, the observer-perspective self-image and post-event processing: Clark and Wells's model of social anxiety (1995). Do not mix up Wells's metacognitive model of GAD with the Clark and Wells model of social anxiety, and do not give Borkovec's theory to panic: panic is Clark's 1986 model.

Intrusions that feel dangerous: cognitive models of OCD

Tara is 26, and six weeks ago she gave birth to her first child in Lucknow. One morning, as she was cutting vegetables with the baby lying on the bed beside her, a picture flashed into her mind of the knife plunging into the child. She was horrified. Since then the image has come back dozens of times a day. She has hidden every knife in the house, will not bathe the baby unless her mother-in-law is present, asks her husband again and again whether he thinks she is a bad mother, and repeats a prayer whenever the image appears "to cancel it". She has never had the slightest wish to hurt her daughter; the thought is so foreign to her that she fears she is going mad. DSM-5-TR notes that intrusive violent thoughts of harming the infant, leading to avoidance of the infant, are reported in OCD that begins or worsens in the peripartum period.

Would a learning theorist or a cognitive theorist explain Tara better? The cognitive account begins with a surprising discovery. In 1978 Stanley Rachman and Padmal de Silva asked people with no psychiatric disorder whether they ever had unwanted, intrusive thoughts. Most did, and the content of their intrusions (harming a loved one, shouting an obscenity in a temple, a sudden doubt about whether the door was locked) resembled the obsessions of patients with OCD. What differed was how often the thoughts came, how intense and distressing they were, and how hard they were to dismiss. Parkinson and Rachman (1981) found that such intrusions become more frequent under stress. Unwanted thoughts, in other words, are a normal product of a normal mind. The question is why a few people cannot let them pass.

The British clinical psychologist Paul M. Salkovskis gave the best-known answer in 1985. What turns an intrusion into an obsession, he argued, is the way it is appraised. The person with OCD interprets the intrusion as meaning that he is responsible for preventing harm to himself or others: "Because I had this thought, I must make sure it does not happen, and if it does it will be my fault." This inflated responsibility produces distress and drives neutralising: any action intended to cancel the thought or prevent the harm, whether a visible ritual (washing, checking), a mental act (a prayer, a counted sequence, a "good" thought to replace the bad one), or reassurance seeking. Neutralising brings brief relief, so it is reinforced; it also prevents the person learning that the harm would not have happened anyway; and it makes the thought seem more important, so it returns more often. Tara's hidden knives, her refusal to bathe the baby alone, her questions to her husband and her cancelling prayer are all neutralising. The therapy built on Salkovskis's model is taught in Book 2, Section 5.6.7.

Rachman added a related idea, thought-action fusion: the belief that thinking about an act is the same as, or leads to, doing it. He and his colleagues distinguished two forms. In likelihood thought-action fusion, thinking about an event makes it more likely to happen ("If I picture my brother in an accident, he will have one"). In moral thought-action fusion, having an unacceptable thought is almost as wicked as acting on it; Barlow describes a patient who believed that thinking about abortion was morally equivalent to having one. Barlow also reports that it is the strength of a person's religious belief, not its type, that goes with thought-action fusion (Rassin and Koster, 2003) and with the severity of OCD (Steketee, Quay and White, 1991); most deeply religious people, of course, never develop OCD.

Why not simply push the thought away? Because pushing makes it come back. In 1987 the social psychologist Daniel M. Wegner (1948 to 2013) and his colleagues asked students to talk aloud for five minutes about whatever came into their minds, while trying not to think of a white bear and ringing a bell whenever they did. They could not stop the bear from appearing. Afterwards, when they were allowed to think about white bears, they thought about them more than students who had been asked to think about white bears from the start. Wegner described this rebound of thought suppression in his book White Bears and Other Unwanted Thoughts (1989). For Tara, every effort to banish the image makes it more frequent, and its frequency seems to prove that it means something.

DSM-5-TR sums up the cognitive research by noting that many people with OCD hold dysfunctional beliefs: an inflated sense of responsibility and a tendency to overestimate threat; perfectionism and intolerance of uncertainty; and over-importance of thoughts (for example, believing that having a forbidden thought is as bad as acting on it) together with the need to control thoughts. It adds an important caution: these beliefs are not specific to OCD. They raise the risk, but they are found in other anxiety disorders too, which is why they fit Barlow's idea of a specific psychological vulnerability (Section 6.8.11) rather than a sufficient cause.

Exam trap

Inflated responsibility and neutralising: Paul Salkovskis, 1985. Thought-action fusion: Stanley Rachman (likelihood and moral forms). Normal obsessions (most people have intrusive thoughts): Rachman and de Silva, 1978. Rebound after thought suppression (the white bear): Daniel Wegner, 1987. The cognitive models do not say that intrusive thoughts are abnormal; they say the appraisal of the thoughts is. And the DSM-5-TR beliefs list (responsibility, threat, perfectionism, uncertainty, importance and control of thoughts) is "not specific to OCD".

Freud's two theories of anxiety

Long before learning theory and cognitive science, Sigmund Freud (1856 to 1939) had made anxiety the central problem of psychopathology, and he answered it twice. His first theory came out of the "anxiety neurosis" he described in 1895 (Section 6.1). Freud noticed that many of his anxious patients had disturbed sexual lives, such as enforced abstinence or interrupted intercourse, and he proposed that sexual excitation that could not be discharged was transformed directly into anxiety. This is often called the transformed libido theory: anxiety as dammed-up sexual energy finding an outlet. On this view repression came first, and anxiety was its by-product.

Thirty years later Freud turned the theory upside down. In Inhibitions, Symptoms and Anxiety (1926; German Hemmung, Symptom und Angst) he proposed that anxiety is a warning. The ego, the part of the mind that deals with reality, senses that a dangerous impulse from the id (a forbidden sexual or aggressive wish) is pressing towards consciousness or action, and it produces a small dose of anxiety as an alarm. This signal anxiety sets the defences going, so that the wish is pushed back or disguised before it can bring about the danger it threatens: punishment, loss of love, or the condemnation of one's own conscience. Freud now said explicitly that anxiety produces repression, not the other way round, and he distinguished signal anxiety from automatic anxiety, the overwhelming state of a helpless person flooded by excitation, the prototype of which is birth. He traced a developmental series of danger situations, from birth and the loss of the mother to castration and the disapproval of the superego, and he returned in the same book to Little Hans, now concluding that the motive force behind Hans's repression was fear of castration. Freud's classification of anxiety into realistic, neurotic and moral types belongs to the Personality book; the defence mechanisms in general are tabled in Book 1, Section 10.8.4, and their place in classical analysis is in Book 2, Sections 3.2.9 and 3.4.1.

The signal theory gave every neurosis the same basic structure. A forbidden wish arouses signal anxiety; the ego defends; and when the defence partly fails, the result is a symptom, a compromise formation that both disguises the wish and expresses something of it. What distinguishes one neurosis from another, in this view, is which defences the ego relies on. Phobia, as you will see next, was built mainly on displacement; obsessional neurosis on isolation, undoing and reaction formation; hysteria on repression and conversion.

Exam trap

Signal anxiety belongs to Freud's second theory, Inhibitions, Symptoms and Anxiety (1926): anxiety is the ego's warning of internal danger and it sets off the defences. The first theory (1890s, the anxiety neurosis of 1895) held that undischarged libido is transformed into anxiety. In the second theory anxiety causes repression; in the first, repression causes anxiety. William Cullen, not Freud, coined the word "neurosis" (1769, Section 6.1).

Phobia and displacement: Little Hans as Freud read him

The opening of this chapter told what happened to Hans: the fear of horses, the father who recorded everything, the single meeting with Freud. Here is what Freud made of it, because it remains the classic psychoanalytic account of how a phobia is formed and the source of a question that appears in almost every paper.

Key study: Little Hans as Freud read him (Freud, 1909)

Freud's reading ran as follows. Hans, at about five, was in the thick of the Oedipal phase: he loved his mother possessively, enjoyed being taken into her bed, and wished his father out of the way, especially when his father was away and he had his mother to himself. Those hostile wishes brought a terrible fear of the father's revenge, in the form of castration anxiety, a fear given substance by a real threat: when Hans was about three and a half, his mother had warned him that the doctor would cut off his "widdler" if he went on touching it. But the father was also loved, lived in the same flat and could not be avoided, so the fear could not stay attached to him. It was shifted onto horses. Freud pointed to the details that betrayed the disguise: Hans was especially frightened of horses with black around their mouths and of the blinkers in front of their eyes, which Freud linked to his father's moustache and spectacles, and the fear that a horse would bite him stood for the fear that his father would punish him. The horse that fell down in the street, in this reading, expressed his wish that his father would fall down and die. The phobia, Freud concluded, was a disguised, displaced expression of an Oedipal conflict, and it eased as Hans's father, on Freud's advice, explained matters to him and reassured him.

The defence at the centre of this account is displacement: redirecting a feeling or impulse from the person or object that really arouses it onto a substitute that is safer. The everyday version is the clerk scolded by his manager who comes home and shouts at his younger brother. In a phobia the substitute has one extra advantage: it can be avoided. A boy cannot escape his father, but he can stay indoors and never see a horse. The phobia converts an inner danger, which follows you everywhere, into an outer one, which you can walk away from, and the price of the bargain is a shrunken life. Psychoanalysts gave displacement the same role in other conditions: in body dysmorphic disorder, for example, they proposed that an unbearable unconscious conflict is displaced onto a body part (Barlow; Section 6.6). Barlow's summary of Freud's view gives a further example of the phobic logic: a man's unconscious fear of being attacked and castrated may be consciously expressed as a fear of being bitten by a dog, even though he knows the dog is harmless.

The critics were not slow. Freud built his case almost entirely from the reports of a father who was a devoted follower of his and who interpreted what his son said in Freud's terms, often by asking leading questions; Freud himself saw the boy only once. In 1960 Joseph Wolpe (1915 to 1997) and Stanley Rachman published a learning-theory critique of the case (Book 2, Section 4.1.3). They pointed out that Hans's fear began after he had seen a horse pulling a bus fall down in the street, an event that, as Hans himself said, gave him a great fright. A frightening event followed by fear of the thing involved, generalising to similar things (other horses, heavily loaded carts), is exactly what conditioning predicts, and the gradual fading of the fear as Hans went out again and grew older looked more like extinction than like insight. They found nothing in the record, they argued, that would count as scientific evidence for the Oedipal interpretation. Today a third reading sits beside these two: a sensitive, anxious temperament (Section 6.7) meeting a frightening event at a vulnerable age, which is Barlow's model in miniature (Section 6.8.11).

A related psychodynamic idea has been tested more directly. Some analysts proposed that panic disorder and agoraphobia grow out of early separation and loss, so that the adult with agoraphobia, often dependent on a companion, is re-living a child's separation anxiety. Barlow's review of the evidence is blunt: despite some intriguing suggestions, little evidence indicates that people with panic disorder or agoraphobia experienced separation anxiety in childhood more often than people with other disorders or than people with no disorder (Barlow 2002; Thyer 1993). DSM-5-TR likewise notes that childhood separation anxiety may precede panic disorder but is not a consistent risk factor.

Exam trap

"Displacement is a defence mechanism typically seen in" phobias (IHBAS 2020). It is the defence of Little Hans: fear of the father displaced onto horses, which can be avoided. It is not the signature defence of hysteria (repression, with conversion of the conflict into a bodily symptom, Chapter 7), of OCD (isolation, undoing, reaction formation) or of depression (introjection and anger turned against the self, Chapter 5). Little Hans is a phobia case (1909); the Rat Man, published the same year, is the obsessional case.

Obsessional neurosis: the Rat Man and the defences of OCD

In October 1907 a young Viennese lawyer, now identified as Ernst Lanzer (1878 to 1914), came to Freud in a state of torment. For years he had been plagued by fears that something terrible would happen to the two people he loved most, his father and a lady he admired, and by impulses and prohibitions he could not resist. The crisis had come during army manoeuvres, when a fellow officer described a punishment said to be used in the East, in which a pot containing live rats was strapped to a prisoner's buttocks and the rats gnawed their way into his anus. At once the young man was seized by the idea that this was happening to the lady, and to his father, although his father had been dead for several years. To ward off the horror he had to perform protective acts and pronounce formulae, and he became entangled in an absurd, self-imposed vow about repaying a small debt that tortured him for days. Freud published the case in 1909 as "Notes upon a Case of Obsessional Neurosis" (in German, Zwangsneurose, which is why older books and Indian papers say "obsessive-compulsive neurosis"). The patient has been known ever since as the Rat Man. Freud claimed that the analysis had restored his mental health, and later lamented that he had been killed in the First World War. Scholars who compared Freud's surviving process notes with the published case, notably Patrick Mahony (1986), found that Freud implied a longer treatment than he gave (daily sessions for little more than three months, then irregular ones), presented some events out of their real order and overstated how complete the cure was.

Freud's theory of obsessional neurosis, built on this case and refined over the next two decades, went like this. The obsessional person has met the conflicts of the Oedipal phase, but instead of dealing with them he has regressed to an earlier position, the anal stage of the second and third years, when the great issues were control, dirt and aggression, and when the child's battles over toilet training taught him both defiance and submission. The result is intense ambivalence: love and hatred for the same person, held at once. Hostile, often sadistic, wishes keep breaking through as obsessional thoughts (the rats gnawing into the father), and the ego fights them with a characteristic set of defences, which Freud described in 1926 and his daughter Anna Freud (1895 to 1982) listed in The Ego and the Mechanisms of Defence (1936).

  • Isolation of affect: the forbidden idea is allowed into consciousness, but its emotion is stripped away. This is why a person with OCD can describe a horrifying thought ("I might strangle my mother") in a flat, matter-of-fact voice; the thought stays, the feeling goes. Freud also described a second kind of isolation, in which a pause or ritual is inserted to cut the thought off from its connections.
  • Undoing: performing an act, or thinking a thought, that symbolically cancels a previous forbidden thought or act, as if it had never happened. The Rat Man supplied the classic example. On the day his lady was to leave, he struck his foot against a stone in the road and felt obliged to move it aside, because her carriage would pass that way and might come to grief on it. A few minutes later it struck him as absurd, and he felt obliged to go back and put the stone back in the middle of the road. The second act undid the first: loving protection, then hostile cancellation. Washing after a "dirty" thought, saying a prayer to cancel a blasphemous one, or repeating an action until it has been done the "right" number of times are all undoing. Because the compulsion itself is an act of undoing, undoing is the defence most directly tied to compulsive rituals and is regarded as the most characteristic defence of obsessive-compulsive neurosis.
  • Reaction formation: holding an unacceptable impulse in check by exaggerating its opposite. Scrupulous cleanliness guards against a wish to soil and be messy; excessive solicitude and kindness towards a person guards against hatred of him; rigid morality guards against forbidden desire. Freud had already described in 1908 an "anal character" marked by orderliness, parsimony and obstinacy, built largely on reaction formation; it is the ancestor of the modern idea of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder (Chapter 11), not of OCD.
  • Intellectualisation: dealing with an emotional conflict by thinking about it in abstract terms, so that feeling is replaced by reasoning. The person who cannot stop debating whether a thought counts as a sin, without ever feeling the guilt, is intellectualising.

To these Freud added a style of thinking. People with obsessional neurosis, he observed, behave as if their thoughts could directly cause events in the world, so that a hostile thought is dangerous and must be cancelled. He called this omnipotence of thought, and in Totem and Taboo (1913) he said he had borrowed the phrase from a highly intelligent obsessional patient, the Rat Man. Its close relative, magical thinking, is the belief that a ritual (counting, touching, saying a word) can prevent a disaster with which it has no realistic connection. You will notice that this is the same observation that Rachman, eighty years later, called thought-action fusion (Section 6.8.7); the cognitive and psychodynamic traditions saw the same phenomenon and explained it differently.

Notice what is missing from the list: repression. In Freud's account of hysteria, the forbidden idea is repressed, pushed out of consciousness altogether and forgotten, and its energy is converted into a bodily symptom such as a paralysed arm (Chapter 7). In obsessional neurosis the forbidden idea is not forgotten at all; it sits in consciousness, which is precisely what makes it an obsession. The defences work instead on its feeling (isolation), on its consequences (undoing) and on its direction (reaction formation). That is why repression is called the defence of hysteria and the answer to "which defence is not involved in OCD?". Table 6.24 sets out the signature defences of the classic neuroses and their neighbours.

Table 6.24 The classic psychodynamic conditions and their signature defences.
ConditionSignature defenceHow it shapes the symptomTaught in
Hysteria (conversion)Repression, with conversionThe idea is forgotten; its energy becomes a bodily symptomChapter 7
PhobiaDisplacement (with projection outward and avoidance)Fear moves from an inner danger to an avoidable outer object (Little Hans)Section 6.8.9
Obsessional neurosis (OCD)Isolation of affect, undoing, reaction formation, intellectualisationThe idea stays conscious without its feeling; rituals cancel it (the Rat Man)Section 6.8.10
Depression (melancholia)Introjection, anger turned against the selfThe lost, resented person is taken into the self and attacked there (Freud, 1917)Chapter 5
ParanoiaProjection (after denial of the wish)"I love him" becomes "I hate him", then "he hates me" (Schreber, 1911)Chapter 4
Body dysmorphic disorder (analysts' view)DisplacementAn unbearable conflict is moved onto a body partSection 6.6

How well has the psychodynamic account of anxiety and obsessions stood up? Its observations are often acute: the rituals of undoing, the flat voice of isolation, the magical quality of obsessional thinking are all real and recognisable. But its causal claims, in their classic form, are very hard to test and have found little support. There is no good evidence that people with OCD had distinctive toilet training, or that the content of obsessions reveals regressed anal-sadistic wishes, and helping a patient understand the supposed meaning of his symptoms rarely makes them go away. Exposure and response prevention, built on the learning account, and cognitive therapy, built on Salkovskis's model, have far stronger evidence (Section 6.9). The psychodynamic tradition survives in clinical vocabulary and in the examination hall, which is why you must know it well.

Exam trap

"The following defence mechanisms are used in OCD: (a) isolation (b) undoing (c) reaction formation (d) displacement" (RML 2024) is keyed (b) undoing, but notice the trap: isolation, undoing and reaction formation are all OCD defences, so three options are correct. The key picks undoing because it is the defence most directly tied to compulsive acts; only displacement (the defence of phobia) is wrong. If a paper allows several answers, choose (a), (b) and (c); if it demands one, choose undoing.

Exam trap

"Which defence mechanism is not involved in OCD?" Repression (IHBAS 2020): repression is the defence of hysteria, whereas in OCD the forbidden idea stays conscious and is isolated, undone or reversed. "The defence mechanism of undoing is typically seen in" obsessive-compulsive neurosis (Amity 2023), not in depression (introjection and anger turned inward), mania (which analysts read as a denial of depressive feelings, Melanie Klein's "manic defence") or schizophrenia (the psychotic defences of regression, projection and denial of reality). The Rat Man (1909) is the obsessional case; Little Hans (1909) the phobic one; and the anal character (1908) is the ancestor of obsessive-compulsive personality disorder, not of OCD.

Putting it together: Barlow's triple vulnerability theory

Each of the theories in this section, and each in Section 6.7, explains part of the picture. Genes and temperament explain why anxiety runs in families but not which disorder appears. Conditioning explains how fears are learned and kept, but not why half of the people bitten by dogs stay fearless. Cognitive models explain how a misreading of the body or of a thought turns into panic or obsession, but not why some people misread and others do not. David H. Barlow, of the Center for Anxiety and Related Disorders at Boston University, has drawn these threads into a single account, the triple vulnerability theory (Barlow 2000, 2002; Barlow and colleagues 2013). It is a diathesis-stress model (Book 2, Section 1.7.10): three vulnerabilities, or diatheses, set the stage, and stressful life events trigger them.

The first is a generalised biological vulnerability: an inherited tendency to be uptight, high-strung and neurobiologically over-reactive to the events of daily life. This is the negative affectivity or neuroticism of Section 6.7, the general anxious tendency that twin studies show is inherited, and it includes temperaments such as Jerome Kagan's behavioural inhibition. On its own it does not produce a disorder.

The second is a generalised psychological vulnerability: a sense, built from early experience, that events are uncontrollable and unpredictable and that one may not be able to cope when things go wrong. Bruce F. Chorpita and Barlow (1998) traced it to parenting. Parents who respond to a child's needs in a positive and predictable way (attention when the child calls, food when hungry, comfort when hurt) teach the child that his actions have an effect on the world. Parents who provide a secure home base but let the child explore and meet manageable difficulties foster a healthy sense of control. Parents who are overprotective and intrusive, who "clear the way" so that the child never meets adversity, leave him without the experience of coping, and the sense of uncontrollability grows. A person with both generalised vulnerabilities is at risk of anxiety in general, and of depression too, which is one reason the two so often occur together.

The third is a specific psychological vulnerability: learning, from early experience, that particular situations, objects or sensations are dangerous, even when they are not. A child whose parent is terrified of dogs, or constantly anxious about what the neighbours will think, may learn exactly that fear. This third vulnerability decides where the anxiety will be focused, and therefore which disorder develops.

Figure 6.15 shows how the pieces fit. Stress, usually social and interpersonal (an examination, a marriage, a death, a job loss), activates the biological tendency, and the person may have a true alarm or a false alarm (an unexpected panic attack), which can become a learned alarm conditioned to internal or external cues. Whether this leads to a disorder depends on the generalised psychological vulnerability: if the person feels he cannot control or cope with such alarms, he develops anxious apprehension, a future-oriented state of tension, vigilance and dread focused on the possibility of the next one. The specific psychological vulnerability then fixes the focus of that apprehension. Push Nikhil's case through the model: an anxious temperament (biological), a lifelong sense that things could spin out of control (generalised psychological), an uncle's early death and a family that watched every twinge of the chest (specific: bodily sensations are dangerous), the stress of a product launch, and a false alarm at two in the morning. The result was not GAD or a dog phobia but panic disorder, with apprehension focused on his heart.

Barlow and his colleagues have drawn a version of the model for each disorder, and Table 6.25 sets them beside the other psychological theories of this section. For panic disorder (White and Barlow 2002), the specific vulnerability is the belief that unexplained bodily sensations are dangerous, and whether agoraphobia follows depends on cultural, social and practical factors and on the availability of safety signals such as a trusted companion. For specific phobia, a direct or vicarious experience, or simply information, teaches that one object or situation is dangerous, and inherited preparedness (and, for blood-injection-injury phobia, a vasovagal fainting tendency) makes some objects easier to fear. For social anxiety disorder, Barlow describes three routes: a person with the vulnerabilities may become anxious and self-focused under social stress even without an alarm; may have an unexpected panic attack in a social situation that becomes conditioned to social cues; or may suffer a real social trauma, such as the brutal teasing of early adolescence; in each case the specific vulnerability is having learned that social evaluation is dangerous. For OCD (Steketee and Barlow 2002), the specific vulnerability is having learned that certain thoughts are dangerous and unacceptable, as in thought-action fusion and inflated responsibility, so that apprehension is focused on recurring intrusive thoughts and rituals are used to neutralise them. GAD is the disorder in which the specific focus matters least: the generalised vulnerabilities and stress produce a diffuse anxious apprehension, carried by the worry process that Borkovec described.

Table 6.25 The disorder-specific psychological models of this section, as explanations of cause.
DisorderModel (author, year)Core ideaWhat keeps it going
Specific phobiaTwo-factor theory (Mowrer, 1947); preparedness (Seligman, 1971); three pathways (Rachman, 1977)Fear learned by conditioning, watching or being told, most easily for prepared objectsAvoidance, negatively reinforced, blocks extinction
Panic disorderFear of fear (Goldstein and Chambless, 1978); anxiety sensitivity (Reiss and McNally, 1985); catastrophic misinterpretation (Clark, 1986); learned alarms (Bouton, Mineka and Barlow, 2001)Bodily sensations are read as signs of disasterHypervigilance to the body, safety behaviours, interoceptive avoidance
Social anxiety disorderCognitive model (Clark and Wells, 1995)Fear of negative evaluation; self-focused attention and a distorted self-imageSafety behaviours, anticipatory and post-event processing
Generalised anxiety disorderAvoidance theory (Borkovec); metacognitive model (Wells, 1995); intolerance of uncertainty (Dugas and colleagues, 1998)Verbal worry as a way of avoiding imagery, arousal and uncertaintyRelief from arousal; positive and negative beliefs about worry
OCDTwo-factor theory applied (Hodgson and Rachman, 1972); inflated responsibility (Salkovskis, 1985); thought-action fusion (Rachman)Normal intrusions appraised as dangerous or as making one responsible for harmCompulsions and neutralising, negatively reinforced; thought suppression
All of themTriple vulnerability theory (Barlow, 2000, 2002)Biological and psychological vulnerabilities plus stress; a specific vulnerability sets the focusAnxious apprehension feeding on itself

The triple vulnerability theory also explains the most striking epidemiological fact of Section 6.1: comorbidity. If the anxiety disorders and depression share the same two generalised vulnerabilities and differ mainly in the focus set by a specific one, then a person who has one disorder should often have another, and this is just what Brown and Barlow's large clinical studies found. Barlow's own treatment, the Unified Protocol, is built on the same logic, targeting the shared processes rather than each disorder separately (Book 2, Section 5.6.7).

Exam trap

The triple vulnerability theory is David H. Barlow's (2000, 2002). Its three vulnerabilities are a generalised biological vulnerability (inherited reactivity), a generalised psychological vulnerability (a sense of uncontrollability from early experience) and a specific psychological vulnerability (learning that particular things are dangerous); stressful life events are the trigger, not a fourth vulnerability. Do not substitute "social vulnerability" or "cognitive, emotional and behavioural vulnerabilities". Kagan's behavioural inhibition is a temperament that belongs inside the biological vulnerability; Gray's behavioural inhibition system is a brain circuit (Section 6.7).

6.9 Drug and Physical TreatmentsLower yield · not asked yet

Most people with an anxiety disorder who get any treatment at all get it first from a general physician, and in that setting the treatment is almost always a tablet. Barlow notes that primary care physicians are usually the first clinicians to see people with panic disorder and that psychological treatment is rarely available there. Nikhil's first stop (Section 6.8.5) was a hospital emergency department, and Section 6.2 notes that the first professional a person with a panic attack meets is usually a cardiologist or an emergency physician, not a psychologist. A clinical psychologist therefore needs to know the drugs his patients are taking: what they do, what they cannot do, what their side effects feel like, what happens when they are stopped, and how they interact with psychological treatment. This section teaches the drugs used for the anxiety disorders and the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, the surgical and stimulation treatments that are reserved for the most severe OCD, and the major trials that compared drugs with psychotherapy. The antidepressant classes and their pharmacology are taught in Chapter 5, Section 5.7; here the question is how they are used for these disorders. The psychotherapies are placed alongside the drugs at the end, with pointers to Book 2.

How drugs and psychotherapy compare

Three broad findings frame everything else. First, for most of these disorders, the short-term effects of an effective drug and of an effective psychological treatment are broadly similar. For GAD, Barlow summarises, psychological treatments give about the same benefit as drugs in the short term but are more effective in the long term. Second, gains made with drugs tend to last only as long as the drug is taken. About half of the patients with panic disorder who become panic-free on an antidepressant relapse once it is stopped, and the figure is close to 90 percent for those who stop a benzodiazepine, whereas gains made with cognitive behaviour therapy (CBT) are more durable. Third, some drugs may weaken psychological treatment. Most studies, Barlow notes, suggest that drugs, particularly the benzodiazepines, can interfere with the effects of exposure-based therapy, perhaps because a person who faces his fear while sedated credits the tablet rather than learning that the situation is safe, so that the drug becomes one more safety signal. It follows, and the trials in Section 6.9.6 confirm, that combining a drug with CBT is not automatically better than either alone.

None of this makes drugs unimportant. Drugs act faster in some patients, they are available where trained therapists are not, some patients prefer them, and for severe OCD, severe depression alongside anxiety, or a crisis, they may be essential. Pinel's verdict on the anxiolytics is a fair summary of the whole field: many drugs produce slight but statistically significant improvements in groups of patients, but many patients are not helped by them and many stop because of side effects. The art lies in matching the treatment to the person.

The benzodiazepines

Vikram is a 52-year-old cloth merchant in Surat. Six years ago, after his first panic attack, a family physician prescribed alprazolam "for the nerves". It worked within half an hour, and Vikram came to rely on it. Over the years the dose crept up, and he now takes it three times a day. If a dose is late he becomes shaky and sweaty, cannot sleep and feels the old panic rising, which convinces him that he cannot manage without it. His son has noticed that he is forgetful and slow at the accounts, and last winter he fell on the shop steps. His panic attacks have never really stopped.

Vikram's drug belongs to the most famous family of anxiety drugs. Before them, physicians calmed anxious patients with bromides, alcohol and then the barbiturates, which were effective sedatives but dangerous in overdose and strongly habit-forming. In 1955 meprobamate, marketed as Miltown, arrived and is often described as the first blockbuster psychotropic drug in American history. Then, at the laboratories of the Swiss company Hoffmann-La Roche in Nutley, New Jersey, the chemist Leo Henryk Sternbach (1908 to 2005) produced the compound that became chlordiazepoxide, the first benzodiazepine, marketed as Librium in 1960; diazepam (Valium) followed in 1963. They were safer than the barbiturates and displaced them. By the 1970s, Barlow notes, Valium and Librium were among the most widely prescribed drugs in the world, and Pinel describes the benzodiazepines as the most widely prescribed psychoactive drugs, taken at any one time by about 5 percent of adult North Americans. Older books call them "minor tranquillisers", as opposed to the "major tranquillisers", the antipsychotics. The general term for a drug that reduces anxiety is an anxiolytic.

How do they work? The main inhibitory neurotransmitter of the brain is GABA (gamma-aminobutyric acid). One of its receptors, the GABA-A receptor, is an ion channel: when GABA binds, the channel opens, negatively charged chloride ions flow into the neuron, and the neuron becomes less likely to fire. Benzodiazepines bind to a separate site on the same receptor and make it respond more strongly to GABA; in Barlow's words, they make it easier for GABA molecules to attach to the receptors, so that the more benzodiazepine there is, the more GABA is attached and the calmer the person becomes, up to a point. Pinel describes them as GABA-A agonists; pharmacologists more precisely call them positive allosteric modulators, because they amplify GABA's effect rather than acting in its place. Since GABA inhibition runs through circuits all over the brain, the benzodiazepines have four main actions, and Pinel lists them as uses: they are anxiolytic, hypnotic (sleep-inducing), anticonvulsant and muscle relaxant.

The family differs mainly in potency and in how long each drug stays in the body. Diazepam and chlordiazepoxide are long-acting; chlordiazepoxide is still widely used to manage alcohol withdrawal (Chapter 10). Alprazolam (Xanax) is a high-potency, short-acting drug that Barlow singles out as the benzodiazepine commonly used for panic disorder. Clonazepam is high-potency and longer-acting and is also used for panic; lorazepam is used for acute anxiety and, by injection, for prolonged seizures. Short-acting drugs wear off between doses, which is one reason people like Vikram feel rebound anxiety several times a day.

Their proper place is narrow. There is wide agreement, Barlow writes, that the optimal use of benzodiazepines is short-term relief of anxiety during a temporary crisis or stressful event, such as a family emergency, prescribed until the crisis is resolved but for no more than a week or two. They are also used for acute, severe panic, for short-term insomnia and in alcohol withdrawal. Their problems are a long list, and every item on it is examined:

  • Sedation and impaired performance. They impair cognitive and motor functioning: people are less alert at work or in class, and driving is impaired. Pinel adds ataxia (unsteadiness), tremor and nausea, and, for the hypnotic use, next-day drowsiness and a raised risk of traffic accidents.
  • Falls. In older adults they are associated with falls and hip fractures, which is why prescribing them to the elderly is a particular worry (Section 6.4).
  • Dependence and withdrawal. They produce both psychological and physical dependence. Stopping them, especially suddenly, brings a withdrawal reaction that includes rebound anxiety, the return of anxiety at a higher level than before treatment, and insomnia; after long use of high doses, abrupt withdrawal can cause seizures, which is why they are always tapered gradually.
  • Danger in combination. Like alcohol, they are brain depressants; combined with alcohol or opioids, they can dangerously depress breathing.
  • Cognitive effects of long use. Taken over a long period, benzodiazepines are associated with cognitive impairment (Deckersbach, Moshier, Tuschen-Caffier and Otto, 2011, from Barlow), and Pinel notes that chronic use as a hypnotic has been linked with reduced life expectancy.
  • Interference with exposure therapy, as described in Section 6.9.1.

The withdrawal problem was measured early. Abby Fyer and her colleagues (1987) tapered alprazolam in 17 patients with panic disorder at 10 percent of the starting dose every three days. Only four completed the withdrawal on schedule, in four to five weeks; during withdrawal 15 of the 17 had recurrent or increased panic attacks, and nine developed new withdrawal symptoms such as malaise, weakness, insomnia, a racing heart, light-headedness and dizziness. Findings like this (15 of 17 is 88 percent) lie behind the textbook figure that relapse is close to 90 percent when benzodiazepines are stopped. Yet the benzodiazepines remain the most widely used class of drugs for panic disorder in practice (Blanco and colleagues, 2004, from Barlow), and their use has continued to rise. They work quickly, patients feel the difference within an hour, and the costs come later.

Two further points complete the picture. Benzodiazepines do not treat OCD: they may take the edge off the anxiety for a few hours, but they do not reduce obsessions or compulsions, which is one of the observations that set OCD apart from the anxiety disorders (Sections 6.1 and 6.7). And because they can be misused, DSM-5-TR includes them in the class of sedative, hypnotic or anxiolytic substances, whose use disorder, intoxication and withdrawal are taught in Chapter 10, where you will also meet flumazenil, a drug that blocks the benzodiazepine site and can reverse their effects.

Clinical link

Patients like Vikram are common in clinical practice, and the psychologist is often the person who explains why the tablet that "works" is part of the problem. Its fast relief negatively reinforces taking it, exactly as escape reinforces avoidance (Section 6.8.1), and the withdrawal symptoms between doses feel like proof that the anxiety is getting worse. A safe plan is made with the prescribing doctor: a slow, gradual taper over weeks to months, often while CBT for panic teaches the person to face the sensations that the drug has been suppressing. Never advise a patient to stop a benzodiazepine abruptly.

Exam trap

Benzodiazepines act by enhancing GABA at the GABA-A receptor; they are anxiolytic, hypnotic, anticonvulsant and muscle relaxant. They are for short-term relief (a week or two), not first-line long-term treatment; for long-term treatment the first-line drugs are the SSRIs and SNRIs. They do not treat OCD. Alprazolam is the high-potency benzodiazepine associated with panic. "Minor tranquilliser" means an anxiolytic such as a benzodiazepine; "major tranquilliser" means an antipsychotic.

Buspirone, pregabalin and the beta blockers

The problems of the benzodiazepines drove a search for anxiolytics without sedation or dependence. The first to succeed was buspirone, a drug of the azapirone class approved in the United States in 1986 for generalised anxiety disorder. It does not act on the GABA-A receptor at all. Its main action is as a partial agonist at serotonin 5-HT1A receptors: it stimulates them, but less strongly than serotonin itself, and it also has weak effects on dopamine receptors. Its profile is almost the mirror image of a benzodiazepine's. It causes little sedation and produces no significant dependence or withdrawal, so DSM-5-TR leaves it out of the sedative, hypnotic or anxiolytic class because it does not appear to be associated with significant misuse. But it has no immediate anxiolytic effect: its benefit appears only after two to four weeks of regular use. That means it cannot be taken "as needed" in a moment of panic, and a patient switched from a benzodiazepine may find it disappointing, since it neither gives the familiar rapid calm nor prevents benzodiazepine withdrawal. Its only established use among these disorders is GAD; it is not effective for panic disorder or the other anxiety disorders.

Pregabalin is one of the newest drugs prescribed for anxiety disorders, and Pinel notes that it is particularly effective for GAD. It works neither on GABA receptors nor on serotonin but by modulating voltage-gated calcium channels, thus affecting calcium levels inside nerve cells and damping the release of excitatory neurotransmitters. It is licensed for GAD in Europe, though not in the United States. Its common side effects are dizziness and sleepiness, and it has a recognised potential for misuse. An older alternative still sometimes used for short-term relief of anxiety is hydroxyzine, an antihistamine whose calming effect comes largely from sedation.

The beta blockers, such as propranolol (Inderal) and atenolol, work in the body rather than the brain. Adrenaline and noradrenaline act on beta-adrenergic receptors in the heart and muscles to produce the racing pulse and trembling hands of fear; beta blockers occupy those receptors and blunt these signs. They do nothing for the worry, the fear of evaluation or the catastrophic thought. Because a trembling bow or a shaking voice can wreck a single performance, beta blockers became popular with musicians, singers and public speakers, and clinicians long assumed they would treat social anxiety. The trials were disappointing. Michael R. Liebowitz and his colleagues (1992) gave 74 patients with social phobia eight weeks of double-blind, randomly assigned treatment with the MAOI phenelzine, the beta blocker atenolol or placebo: 64 percent responded to phenelzine, 30 percent to atenolol and 23 percent to placebo, and atenolol did not differ significantly from placebo. Samuel Turner, Deborah Beidel and Rolf Jacob (1994) found that flooding (intensive exposure) was consistently superior to placebo in 72 patients with social phobia, whereas atenolol was not. Beta blockers therefore keep a modest role for the bodily symptoms of occasional performance anxiety, and even there the controlled evidence is thin; they are not a treatment for social anxiety disorder as a whole. They are also unsuitable for some people: propranolol can provoke wheezing in asthma, and all of them slow the heart.

Exam trap

Buspirone is a 5-HT1A partial agonist for GAD: slow (two to four weeks), non-sedating, no dependence, useless "as needed" and not effective for panic or agoraphobia. Some Indian exam notes list "clomipramine, venlafaxine, buspirone" as the drug treatment of agoraphobia; the antidepressants belong on that list (Section 6.9.4), buspirone does not. Pregabalin acts on voltage-gated calcium channels and is used for GAD. Beta blockers (propranolol, atenolol) block the bodily signs of anxiety and are used before performances, but in controlled trials atenolol was no better than placebo for social anxiety disorder (Liebowitz and colleagues 1992; Turner, Beidel and Jacob 1994), whereas the MAOI phenelzine was clearly effective.

Antidepressants: the first-line drugs for anxiety and obsessions

It surprises many students that the first-line drugs for the anxiety disorders and OCD are called antidepressants. The name records the order in which their uses were discovered, not a claim that anxious people are secretly depressed, although the two conditions often coexist and Pinel points out that antidepressants are often effective against anxiety disorders and anxiolytics against depression. The classes, how they act on serotonin and noradrenaline, the lag of several weeks before they work, and their side-effect profiles are taught in Chapter 5, Section 5.7. The selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs: fluoxetine, sertraline, paroxetine, fluvoxamine, citalopram, escitalopram) and the serotonin-noradrenaline reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs: venlafaxine, duloxetine) are now the first choice across this chapter because they work for most of these disorders, they treat coexisting depression, and they do not produce the dependence of the benzodiazepines. What follows is disorder by disorder.

Panic disorder and agoraphobia. The story begins with the tricyclic imipramine, whose ability to stop panic attacks while leaving anticipatory anxiety untouched led Donald Klein to separate panic from generalised anxiety (Section 6.2), and imipramine was the drug used in the great panic trial described in Section 6.9.6. Today, Barlow writes, the SSRIs are the indicated drugs for panic disorder on all available evidence, with the closely related SNRIs such as venlafaxine. About 60 percent of patients are free of panic as long as they stay on an effective drug, but 20 percent or more stop before treatment is complete, and about half relapse once the drug is stopped. The commonest reason for stopping is sexual dysfunction, which occurred in 75 percent or more of patients in Yves Lecrubier's studies. A special problem in panic is the start of treatment. People with panic disorder are acutely sensitive to bodily sensations, and in the first days an antidepressant can cause restlessness, insomnia and a rise in anxiety, which Pohl and his colleagues (1988) described as the jitteriness syndrome in panic patients treated with antidepressants. Read through Clark's model, a new jittery sensation is just the kind of thing a panic patient will misinterpret, so clinicians start with a low dose, raise it slowly and warn the patient in advance.

Social anxiety disorder. Since 1999 the SSRIs paroxetine and sertraline and the SNRI venlafaxine have been approved by the US Food and Drug Administration for social anxiety disorder on the strength of placebo-controlled trials (Barlow). The older monoamine oxidase inhibitor (MAOI) phenelzine is also clearly effective: in Liebowitz's 1992 trial, 64 percent of patients responded to it against 23 percent on placebo. Because MAOIs require a strict low-tyramine diet to avoid a hypertensive crisis (Chapter 5, Section 5.7), they are now kept for patients who do not respond to the newer drugs.

Generalised anxiety disorder. Benzodiazepines are the drugs most often prescribed for GAD and they give some short-term relief, but few studies have followed patients beyond eight weeks and the effect is modest. There is stronger evidence for antidepressants such as paroxetine and venlafaxine, which Barlow suggests may be the better choice; other SSRIs and SNRIs, such as escitalopram and duloxetine, are also widely used. Buspirone and pregabalin (Section 6.9.3) are the alternatives.

Separation anxiety disorder and the anxious child. In a major trial with children and adolescents who had GAD, separation anxiety disorder or social phobia, CBT and the SSRI sertraline were each effective, and their combination was better still: 80 percent of children receiving both showed substantial improvement, against 24 percent on placebo (Walkup and colleagues, 2008, from Barlow). Notice that in this young group the combination did add something, unlike several adult trials in Section 6.9.6. The warning about antidepressants and suicidal thinking in young people is taught in Chapter 5.

Obsessive-compulsive disorder. OCD is where the antidepressants are most distinctive, because only those that strongly block the reuptake of serotonin work. The first was clomipramine, a tricyclic antidepressant that is far more serotonergic than the other tricyclics; its benefit in OCD was first reported by the Spanish psychiatrists Fernández-Córdoba and López-Ibor in 1967, and in 1989 it became the first drug approved by the US Food and Drug Administration for OCD. The SSRIs (fluoxetine, fluvoxamine, sertraline, paroxetine) followed. Barlow summarises the evidence: clomipramine or the SSRIs benefit up to 60 percent of patients with OCD, with no particular advantage of one drug over another, but relapse often occurs when the drug is stopped. Three features of drug treatment in OCD differ from depression. The doses are usually higher: a meta-analysis of nine fixed-dose trials with 2,268 participants found that higher doses of SSRIs were more effective in OCD than low or medium doses, though more patients dropped out because of side effects (Bloch and colleagues, 2010). The trials must be longer: experts recommend at least three months at the highest tolerated dose before concluding that the drug is not enough and adding a second one (Bloch and colleagues, 2006). And even then the response is often partial: as many as half of patients given an adequate trial continue to have significant symptoms. That selective response to serotonergic drugs, together with the failure of benzodiazepines, is the foundation of the serotonin hypothesis of OCD taught in Section 6.7.

Clomipramine's power has a price, the familiar tricyclic burden described in Chapter 5: anticholinergic effects (dry mouth, constipation, blurred vision, difficulty passing urine), sedation, weight gain, sexual dysfunction, effects on heart rhythm and danger in overdose. It also carries a higher risk of seizures than most other tricyclics, rising with dose. For these reasons the SSRIs are usually tried first and clomipramine kept for those who do not respond.

Body dysmorphic disorder. Barlow notes that only two treatments for BDD have real evidence of effectiveness, and they are the same as for OCD: drugs that block the reuptake of serotonin and exposure-based CBT. Clomipramine (Anafranil) and fluvoxamine (Luvox) relieve at least some patients (Hadley, Kim, Priday and Hollander, 2006). In a controlled crossover trial, Eric Hollander and his colleagues (1999) found clomipramine significantly more effective than desipramine, a tricyclic that does not specifically block serotonin reuptake, even in patients whose BDD was of the delusional type; and in a placebo-controlled trial of fluoxetine, 53 percent responded well against 18 percent on placebo (Phillips, Albertini and Rasmussen, 2002). That even delusional BDD responds to serotonergic drugs is one reason DSM-5 keeps such patients in the OCD family rather than among the psychoses (Section 6.6). Cosmetic surgery is not a treatment.

Hoarding disorder and the body-focused repetitive behaviours. Here drugs have least to offer. There is no established drug treatment for hoarding; the main approach is a specialised CBT that teaches people to assign different values to objects and to tolerate discarding them, and even its results are more modest than those achieved with OCD (Barlow). For trichotillomania and excoriation disorder the first-line treatment is psychological, habit reversal training (Book 2, Section 4.9.1). SSRIs hold some promise, particularly for trichotillomania (Chamberlain and colleagues, 2007), but the results have been mixed for excoriation (Grant and colleagues, 2012), both from Barlow. A newer candidate is N-acetylcysteine, an amino acid derivative that modulates the excitatory transmitter glutamate: in a 12-week double-blind trial with 50 adults with trichotillomania, it reduced hair pulling significantly more than placebo (Grant, Odlaug and Kim, 2009).

Exam trap

Clomipramine is a tricyclic antidepressant, not an SSRI, though it is the most serotonergic of the tricyclics; it was the first drug approved for OCD in the United States (1989). The SSRIs and SNRIs are first-line for long-term drug treatment of panic, social anxiety, GAD and OCD. OCD usually needs higher doses and longer trials than depression, and only serotonergic drugs help it; benzodiazepines do not. The MAOI phenelzine works for social anxiety disorder but needs the tyramine diet. Imipramine is the tricyclic of Klein's panic discovery.

When the first drug is not enough: augmentation and D-cycloserine

What happens when an adequate trial of a serotonergic drug leaves a person with OCD still ill? The best-studied step is augmentation: adding a second drug to the first rather than replacing it. In OCD the second drug is usually a low dose of an antipsychotic. Michael Bloch and his colleagues (2006) pooled nine double-blind trials involving 278 adults with OCD that had not responded to a serotonin reuptake inhibitor. Antipsychotic augmentation helped significantly, but only about one-third of these treatment-resistant patients showed a meaningful response. The evidence was clearest for haloperidol and risperidone, and patients with a coexisting tic disorder did particularly well. That last finding goes back to a trial by Christopher McDougle and his colleagues (1994), who added haloperidol or placebo for four weeks to the treatment of 34 patients who had not responded to fluvoxamine, comparing those with and without chronic tics. It fits the idea, taught in Section 6.7, that dopamine plays a secondary part in OCD, especially in its tic-related form (Chapter 3 teaches the tic disorders). The same review recommended at least three months of a serotonergic drug at the highest tolerated dose before augmentation, because a quarter of patients go on improving with the first drug alone.

A very different idea has come from the laboratory study of extinction. Exposure therapy works, as Section 6.8 explained, because the person learns that the feared stimulus is safe; that new learning is a form of extinction, and extinction is an active learning process in the amygdala that depends on the excitatory transmitter glutamate acting at NMDA receptors. The neuroscientist Michael Davis and his colleagues at Emory University found that D-cycloserine, a partial agonist at the NMDA receptor (it acts at the receptor's glycine site and so boosts the receptor's response to glutamate), enhanced the extinction of conditioned fear in rats, so that fewer extinction trials were needed (Walker, Ressler, Lu and Davis, 2002). D-cycloserine is not new: it is an old antibiotic, used in India and elsewhere as a second-line drug for drug-resistant tuberculosis. In anxiety treatment it is used in a completely different way. It is given once, about an hour before an exposure session, and not taken on an ongoing basis, so that it can boost the learning that happens in that session. In double-blind trials it improved the results of CBT in panic disorder (Otto and colleagues, 2010) and in social anxiety disorder (Hofmann and colleagues, 2006), and a review by Hofmann, Sawyer and Asnaani (2012) summarised it as a promising augmentation strategy for CBT. A later and larger trial was more sobering: D-cycloserine made patients with social anxiety disorder improve 24 to 33 percent faster during a 12-week course of CBT, but by the end they were no more likely to respond or remit than those on placebo (Hofmann and colleagues, 2013). It seems, as Hofmann and his colleagues concluded, to start the treatment effect sooner rather than to make a full course of CBT more effective. Note what kind of drug it is: D-cycloserine is not an anxiolytic and does nothing for anxiety on its own; it is a learning enhancer that works only together with exposure.

Exam trap

In treatment-resistant OCD the usual augmenting drugs are antipsychotics (best evidence for haloperidol and risperidone), and they help about one patient in three, especially those with tics. D-cycloserine is a glutamate (NMDA) receptor enhancer and antibiotic given about an hour before exposure sessions to speed extinction learning; it is an enhancer of CBT, not an anxiolytic, and it is not taken daily.

Table 6.26 gathers the drugs of this section in one place.

Landmark trials: drugs against, and with, psychotherapy

Clinicians argued for decades about whether anxious patients should get tablets, therapy or both. The argument was finally put to the test in large randomised trials, which is why their results appear in examinations. The most influential was a panic trial sponsored by the National Institute of Mental Health (NIMH) in the United States.

Key study: CBT, imipramine or both for panic disorder (Barlow, Gorman, Shear and Woods, 2000)

In four anxiety research clinics between 1991 and 1998, 312 patients with panic disorder were randomly assigned, double-blind, to one of five conditions: imipramine alone; CBT alone; placebo alone; CBT plus imipramine; and CBT plus placebo, the last included to check whether any advantage of the combination came simply from taking a pill. Imipramine was used because the study began before the SSRIs were established for panic. Patients were treated weekly for three months, responders were seen monthly for six more months, and everyone was then followed for six months after treatment stopped. At the end of the first three months both imipramine and CBT were significantly better than placebo, with similar response rates (about 46 percent for imipramine and 49 percent for CBT, against 22 percent for placebo, on the main panic measure), and the combination was not significantly better than either. After the maintenance phase, the combination showed some advantage. The decisive result came six months after all treatment had ended: on the clinicians' global rating, the response rate was 41 percent for CBT plus placebo and 32 percent for CBT alone, but only 20 percent for imipramine alone and 26 percent for the combination, against 13 percent for placebo. Patients who had taken the drug, with or without CBT, had lost ground; those who had CBT without the drug kept most of their gains. The lesson: drug and psychological treatment work about equally well at first, combining them adds little, and CBT lasts.

The same pattern appeared in OCD. Edna B. Foa and her colleagues (2005) randomly assigned 122 adults with OCD to intensive exposure and ritual prevention (four weeks of intensive sessions followed by eight weekly maintenance sessions), the tricyclic clomipramine (12 weeks, up to 250 mg a day), the two combined, or a pill placebo, at three centres chosen for their expertise in drugs, in exposure, or in both. All active treatments beat placebo. Among patients who completed treatment, 86 percent responded to exposure and ritual prevention alone, 79 percent to the combination, 48 percent to clomipramine alone and 10 percent to placebo. Exposure, with or without the drug, was superior to the drug alone, and adding the drug to exposure gave no further advantage; when the drug was withdrawn, relapse was high (Barlow). In social anxiety disorder, David Clark and his colleagues (2003) compared cognitive therapy, the SSRI fluoxetine plus instructions to seek out social situations (self-exposure), and placebo plus self-exposure. Both active treatments helped, but cognitive therapy was substantially better at every assessment, and its advantage held at a 12-month follow-up; in a separate Swedish trial (Mörtberg, Clark, Sundin and Åberg Wistedt, 2007), the gains of individual and of intensive group cognitive therapy for social phobia were not only kept but had grown further when patients were followed up five years later (Mörtberg, Clark and Bejerot, 2011). And Jonathan Davidson, Edna Foa, Jonathan Huppert and their colleagues (2004) found that CBT and fluoxetine were comparably effective for social anxiety disorder, while the combination was no better than either. Table 6.27 summarises the four trials.

Table 6.27 Four landmark trials comparing drugs and psychological treatment.
TrialDisorderComparedMain result
Barlow, Gorman, Shear and Woods (2000)Panic disorderCBT, imipramine, both, placebo, CBT plus placeboAll active treatments beat placebo; combination no better at first; CBT without the drug held up best after treatment stopped
Foa and colleagues (2005)OCDExposure and ritual prevention, clomipramine, both, placeboCompleter response 86 percent for exposure, 79 percent combined, 48 percent clomipramine, 10 percent placebo
