Exam trap

"Drug plus CBT is always best" is the tempting wrong answer. In the adult trials above, the combination was no better than CBT alone at the end of acute treatment, and in panic the drug-containing conditions relapsed more after treatment stopped. In OCD, exposure and ritual prevention outperformed clomipramine (86 versus 48 percent of completers responding; Foa and colleagues 2005). The child anxiety trial (Walkup and colleagues 2008) is the exception in which the combination did best.

Neurosurgery and deep brain stimulation for severe OCD

A small number of people with OCD remain severely ill after years of every drug and of expert exposure and response prevention. They may spend most of their waking hours on rituals, be unable to work or leave the house, and be at serious risk of suicide. For them, two physical treatments are considered as a last resort.

The first is neurosurgery. The word psychosurgery is still used, though Barlow calls it a misnomer: what is meant is neurosurgery for a psychological disorder. It carries the shadow of the prefrontal leucotomies of the 1930s to 1950s (Chapter 4), which destroyed large areas of brain in thousands of patients. Modern procedures are very different: small lesions placed precisely with stereotactic guidance in the circuits implicated in OCD (Section 6.7). In an anterior cingulotomy the lesion is made in the cingulate bundle, in the cingulate region above the corpus callosum. Michael A. Jenike and his colleagues (1991) reviewed the records of the patients with OCD who had undergone cingulotomy at Massachusetts General Hospital over 25 years, most of them extremely severe cases who had failed drug and psychological treatment. Using very conservative criteria, they estimated that at least 25 to 30 percent of the 33 patients benefited substantially; side effects included seizures, easily controlled, in 9 percent and transient mania in 6 percent, and six patients had died since surgery, four of them by suicide, a reminder of how severe the illness was. In an anterior capsulotomy the lesion is made in the front part of the internal capsule, interrupting fibres that link the frontal cortex and the thalamus. Christian Rück and his colleagues (2008) followed 25 consecutive Swedish patients for an average of almost 11 years after capsulotomy. Their mean score on the Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale (Assessment book) fell from 34 to 18, and nine patients (about a third) were in remission; but only three of those nine had no adverse effects, and ten patients overall had significant problems with executive functioning, apathy or disinhibition, more often after high radiation doses or repeated operations. Barlow judges these results typical of the surgical procedures.

The second is deep brain stimulation (DBS). Instead of destroying tissue, the surgeon passes thin electrodes through small holes drilled in the skull into a target deep in the brain and connects them to a pacemaker-like pulse generator implanted under the skin of the chest, which delivers continuous electrical stimulation. The usual target for OCD lies in the same circuit as capsulotomy, the ventral part of the anterior limb of the internal capsule and the adjoining ventral striatum, including the nucleus accumbens; a French trial stimulated the subthalamic nucleus instead (Mallet and colleagues, 2008). Its great advantages are that the stimulation can be adjusted and, if necessary, switched off: it is reversible. Barlow reports that its results are similar to those of the surgical procedures. In February 2009 the US Food and Drug Administration permitted its use for severe, treatment-resistant OCD under a humanitarian device exemption, a pathway for rare conditions that does not require full proof of effectiveness. The risks include those of any brain operation (bleeding, infection) and stimulation effects on mood. Both surgery and DBS remain treatments of last resort, offered only in specialised centres after careful assessment and alongside continued medication and therapy.

Indian context

Indian law treats psychosurgery as a special case. Under section 96 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017, psychosurgery may not be performed as a treatment for mental illness unless the informed consent of the person on whom it is performed and the approval of the concerned Mental Health Review Board have both been obtained. The preceding section, 95, prohibits outright certain other procedures, including electroconvulsive therapy without anaesthesia and muscle relaxants, and chaining (Book 2, Section 1.10.7).

Exam trap

"Psychosurgery" means neurosurgery for a psychological disorder; for OCD the procedures are cingulotomy (cingulate bundle; Jenike and colleagues 1991, about 30 percent of 33 patients helped substantially) and capsulotomy (anterior limb of the internal capsule; Rück and colleagues 2008, 9 of 25 in remission, most with adverse effects). Deep brain stimulation gives similar results but is adjustable and reversible. All are last resorts. In India, psychosurgery needs the person's own informed consent plus Mental Health Review Board approval (MHCA 2017, section 96).

Where the psychotherapies fit

For every disorder in this chapter there is a psychological treatment with evidence at least as good as that for any drug, and for several there is better evidence. How these therapies are actually carried out is taught in Book 2; Table 6.28 places them beside the first-line drugs so that you can see the whole treatment picture at once.

For panic disorder and agoraphobia, the treatment of choice is CBT that combines cognitive therapy for catastrophic misinterpretations with interoceptive exposure (deliberately bringing on the feared sensations, by spinning in a chair or running on the spot, until they lose their power) and graded exposure to avoided situations; Barlow's panic control treatment is one version (Book 2, Sections 5.6.3 and 4.3), and breathing retraining is sometimes added (Book 2, Section 4.8.3). Specific phobia rarely needs drugs at all: exposure, which can be completed in a single session of a few hours, systematic desensitisation and participant modelling are highly effective (Book 2, Sections 4.2, 4.3 and 4.7.2), and blood-injection-injury phobia adds applied tension to prevent fainting (Section 6.3). Social anxiety disorder responds best to cognitive therapy based on the Clark and Wells model, with social skills training where skills are genuinely lacking (Book 2, Sections 5.6.4 and 4.7.5). GAD is treated with CBT, applied relaxation, metacognitive therapy and acceptance-based therapies (Book 2, Sections 4.8.4, 5.10.3 and 5.9). Separation anxiety disorder and selective mutism are treated with child-focused CBT and work with parents and schools (Book 2, Sections 10.3.2 and 10.4.2).

For OCD, exposure and response prevention is the psychological treatment with the strongest evidence (Book 2, Section 4.3.5), and cognitive therapy aimed at inflated responsibility and the other beliefs of Section 6.8.7 is an effective alternative or addition (Book 2, Section 5.6.7). Thought stopping, once popular, is now discouraged, because suppressing a thought tends to make it rebound, as Wegner's white bears showed (Book 2, Section 4.9.4). BDD is treated with CBT that includes exposure and response prevention adapted to appearance concerns; cosmetic procedures are not a treatment and often lead to further operations (Section 6.6). Hoarding disorder has its own specialised CBT, and trichotillomania and excoriation disorder are treated with habit reversal training (Book 2, Section 4.9.1). Because these disorders share vulnerabilities and processes (Section 6.8.11), Barlow's group developed the Unified Protocol, a single transdiagnostic CBT for anxiety and related disorders (Book 2, Section 5.6.7), and services increasingly organise care in steps, starting with the least intensive effective treatment and stepping up when it fails (stepped care, Book 2, Section 5.11.5).

Table 6.28 First-line drug and psychological treatment for each disorder in this chapter.

Exam trap

The treatment traps of this chapter in one place. SSRIs and SNRIs, not benzodiazepines, are first-line for long-term drug treatment. Benzodiazepines do not treat OCD. Buspirone is for GAD, not panic. Beta blockers damp the bodily signs of performance anxiety but do not treat social anxiety disorder. Clomipramine is a tricyclic. Drug plus CBT is not automatically better than either. Exposure and ritual prevention outperformed clomipramine in OCD. Neurosurgery and deep brain stimulation are last resorts. Cosmetic surgery does not treat BDD, and thought stopping is no longer recommended for obsessions.

Scan Your Brain

Fill in the blanks. The answers are below; cover them first.

  1. Mowrer's two-factor theory (1947) holds that a fear is acquired by conditioning and maintained because avoidance is strengthened by reinforcement.
  2. From the perspective of learning theory, compulsive acts such as hand-washing are patterns of (or avoidance) behaviour.
  3. Martin Seligman's (1971) idea that evolution makes fears of snakes and heights easy to learn and hard to extinguish is called .
  4. Rachman's (1977) three pathways to fear are direct conditioning, vicarious learning and .
  5. David M. Clark's (1986) model traces panic to the catastrophic of bodily sensations.
  6. In Adrian Wells's metacognitive model of GAD, worry about worry is called worry.
  7. Paul Salkovskis (1985) argued that intrusions become obsessions when they are appraised as signs of responsibility for harm.
  8. The defence typically seen in phobias is ; the defence not involved in OCD is , the defence of hysteria.
  9. Benzodiazepines enhance the action of the neurotransmitter at its type A receptor.
  10. Buspirone is a partial agonist at serotonin receptors and is used for .

Answers: (1) classical, negative, (2) escape, (3) preparedness, (4) information (instruction), (5) misinterpretation, (6) Type 2, (7) inflated, (8) displacement, repression, (9) GABA, (10) 5-HT1A, generalised anxiety disorder.

The case of Little Hans, revisited

Go back to the front door of the flat in Vienna, beyond which a boy of four and three-quarters would not go because a horse might bite him. You can now name what Hans had. His fear was intense, out of proportion to the danger, tied to one kind of animal and handled by avoidance; in DSM-5-TR terms it would be a specific phobia of the animal type (Section 6.3), not agoraphobia, because he stayed indoors to avoid horses, not because escape from the street might be difficult. Freud's reading (Section 6.8.9) made the horse a disguised father. The Oedipal fear of the father, who was loved and could not be avoided, was displaced onto horses, which could be; that is why displacement is still the answer when a paper asks which defence is typical of phobias, why Freud called phobias like this one anxiety hysteria (Section 6.1.3), and why in 1926 he returned to Hans to argue that fear of castration drove the repression (Section 6.8.8). Joseph Wolpe and Stanley Rachman's reading of 1960, also told in Section 6.8.9, needed none of this. Hans's fear began after he watched a bus horse fall and lie kicking in the street; a frightening event, followed by fear of the thing involved and its spread to other horses and to heavily loaded carts, is exactly what conditioning predicts (Section 6.8.1), and Mowrer's two-factor theory adds why staying indoors, rewarded by relief, would have kept the fear from extinguishing.

Neither story on its own explains why Hans, and not every child in Vienna who saw a horse fall, became phobic. DiNardo found frightening encounters with dogs as often in people without a dog phobia as in people with one, and Rachman's three pathways (1977) allow a fear to be learned by watching or by being told, with no fright at all (Section 6.8.3). The modern answer is the third story of the opening, Barlow's triple vulnerability (Section 6.8.11): an inherited, reactive temperament of the kind Kagan found in high-reactive infants (Section 6.7.2), a sense that events are uncontrollable, and a specific lesson that this one thing is dangerous, brought together at a vulnerable age by a real fright. The ending fits as well. Phobias that begin in childhood often wax and wane (Section 6.3.4), and Hans's passed: the strapping youth of nineteen who called on Freud in 1922 was well and, having read his own case history, remembered nothing of it. A specific phobia today rarely needs a drug at all; exposure, the very contact with the feared thing that staying indoors prevented, is highly effective (Section 6.9.8). And the case Freud published beside his in 1909, the Rat Man, set out the other half of this chapter, the obsessions, together with the defences of isolation, undoing and reaction formation that still decide exam questions (Section 6.8.10), even though the classic causal claims of both case histories have found little support.

Chapter summary

6.1 Fear, Anxiety and the Neuroses: Classifying the Anxiety Disorders. DSM-5-TR defines fear as the response to real or perceived imminent threat and anxiety as the anticipation of future threat. Either becomes a disorder when it is excessive, persists beyond the developmentally appropriate age, lasts (typically 6 months, less for two childhood disorders) and causes distress or impairment. William Cullen coined neurosis in 1769, Freud carved out anxiety neurosis in 1895, and DSM-III (1980), under Robert Spitzer, dropped the term. DSM-5 (2013) split the old group into anxiety disorders, obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, and trauma- and stressor-related disorders. ICD-10 keeps the neurotic block F40 to F48; ICD-11 has anxiety or fear-related disorders and asks for GAD symptoms over "at least several months". Comorbidity is the rule (Brown and colleagues, 2001), and anxiety caused by a substance or a medical illness is diagnosed separately.

6.2 Panic Attacks, Panic Disorder and Agoraphobia. A panic attack is an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, with four or more of thirteen symptoms. Attacks may be expected or unexpected, and an attack is not itself a disorder: elsewhere it is recorded as a specifier. Donald Klein's imipramine studies (1959 to 1964) separated panic from anticipatory anxiety. Panic disorder needs recurrent unexpected attacks, at least one followed by a month or more of persistent concern or maladaptive change in behaviour; a single attack is not enough. Goldstein and Chambless (1978) traced its persistence to fear of fear, shown as anticipatory anxiety, interoceptive avoidance and safety behaviours. Agoraphobia, named by Westphal in 1871, is marked fear of two or more of five situations because escape might be difficult or help unavailable, typically for 6 months, and since DSM-5 it is diagnosed independently of panic disorder.

6.3 Specific Phobia, Social Anxiety Disorder and the Names of the Phobias. A phobia is a persistent, irrational fear of a specific object or situation, out of proportion to the danger, almost always provoked, and avoided or endured with dread; DSM-5-TR asks that it typically last 6 months, and the clinician judges it excessive. The five types are animal, natural environment, blood-injection-injury, situational and other. Blood-injection-injury phobia alone produces a diphasic vasovagal response with fainting, runs strongly in families and is treated with Öst and Sterner's applied tension (1987). Phobia names decode from their Greek roots: algophobia is fear of pain, nosophobia of disease, cynophobia of dogs, haematophobia of blood, and agoraphobia literally of the marketplace. Social anxiety disorder is fear of scrutiny and negative evaluation, typically for 6 months, with a performance-only specifier, a median onset of 13 and a cultural relative in taijin kyofusho.

6.4 Generalised Anxiety Disorder, Separation Anxiety Disorder and Selective Mutism. Generalised anxiety disorder grew from Freud's anxious expectation (1895); DSM-III (1980) named it with a one-month rule and DSM-III-R set six months. DSM-5-TR requires excessive, hard-to-control worry about a number of events, more days than not for at least 6 months, with three of six symptoms (restlessness, easy fatigue, poor concentration, irritability, muscle tension, sleep disturbance; one in children). GAD is continued apprehension about unspecified things, not fear of fear, flashbacks or checking, and its sufferers are chronically tense autonomic restrictors. ICD-10's guidelines ask for several weeks, usually several months, and ICD-11 for at least several months. Separation anxiety disorder needs three of eight signs for 4 weeks in children or typically 6 months in adults, and can begin in adulthood; selective mutism (Kussmaul, 1877) needs 1 month, not the first month of school.

6.5 Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder: The Clinical Picture. Esquirol, Westphal and Janet described obsessions long before DSM-5 gave OCD a chapter of its own. An obsession is a recurrent, intrusive, unwanted thought, urge or image that the person tries to ignore, suppress or neutralise; a compulsion is a repetitive behaviour or mental act performed to reduce anxiety or prevent a dreaded event, never for pleasure. The cardinal feature is that they take more than an hour a day or cause significant distress or impairment. Obsessions are ego-dystonic. Specifiers record insight (good or fair, poor, absent/delusional) and a tic history. Four symptom dimensions pair obsessions with compulsions, and hoarding is no longer one of them. Twelve-month prevalence is about 1.2 percent, mean onset 19.5 years, and contamination leads Indian series (Akhtar and Wig, 1975). Obsessions differ from worries, ruminations and delusions, and OCD from ego-syntonic obsessive-compulsive personality disorder.

6.6 Body Dysmorphic Disorder, Hoarding and the Body-Focused Repetitive Behaviours. DSM-5 gathered OCD, body dysmorphic disorder, hoarding disorder, trichotillomania and excoriation disorder, with substance-induced, medical and other specified forms, into the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. Body dysmorphic disorder, Morselli's dysmorphophobia (1891) and Phillips's "imagined ugliness", is preoccupation with perceived flaws in appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others, with repetitive mirror checking, grooming, comparing or reassurance seeking. It has muscle dysmorphia and insight specifiers, usually begins in adolescence, carries a grave suicide risk, and is an obsessive-compulsive and related disorder, not a somatic symptom, anxiety or personality disorder. Hoarding disorder (Frost and Steketee) is persistent difficulty discarding possessions, whatever their value, until clutter blocks the use of living areas. Trichotillomania (Hallopeau, 1889) and excoriation disorder are body-focused repetitive behaviours. ICD-11 adds olfactory reference disorder and hypochondriasis to the group.

6.7 The Biology of Anxiety and Obsessions. Every disorder in this chapter is moderately heritable, but what is inherited is mostly a general tendency to anxiety (Kendler, 1995; Hettema, 2005); blood-injection-injury phobia is the most familial. Jerome Kagan and Nancy Snidman found about 20 percent of infants high reactive, and about a third of these socially anxious by adolescence. The GABA-benzodiazepine system, noradrenaline from the locus coeruleus, serotonin and CRF are implicated. The amygdala drives fear while the prefrontal cortex restrains it, and Jeffrey Gray separated a behavioural inhibition system (anxiety) from a fight/flight system (panic). Donald Klein's suffocation false alarm theory (1993) explains panic disorder as an oversensitive suffocation monitor, supported by lactate and carbon dioxide challenges. OCD is tied to the central serotonin system (clomipramine, the SSRIs, mCPP) and the cortico-striato-thalamo-cortical loop (orbitofrontal cortex, anterior cingulate, caudate; Baxter, 1992), and PANDAS (Swedo, 1998) to streptococcal infection.

6.8 Learning, Cognition and Conflict: Psychological Explanations. Mowrer's two-factor theory (1947) holds that fear is acquired by classical conditioning and maintained because avoidance is negatively reinforced, which blocks extinction. Compulsions are escape or avoidance responses, as Hodgson and Rachman's washing experiments (1972) showed. Seligman's preparedness (1971) and Rachman's three pathways (1977) explain why phobias cluster on ancient dangers and need no frightening event. Beck traced anxiety to overestimated danger and catastrophising, worrying that the worst will happen. Clark (1986) explained panic as the catastrophic misinterpretation of bodily sensations, Reiss and McNally described anxiety sensitivity, and Borkovec, Wells and Dugas modelled the worry of GAD. Salkovskis's inflated responsibility (1985) and Rachman's thought-action fusion explain obsessions. Freud's signal anxiety (1926) sets off the defences: displacement in phobia (Little Hans), and isolation, undoing and reaction formation, not repression, in OCD (the Rat Man). Barlow's triple vulnerability theory brings these strands together.

6.9 Drug and Physical Treatments. Drugs and psychological treatments work about equally well at first, but drug gains tend to last only while the drug is taken. The benzodiazepines (Sternbach's chlordiazepoxide, 1960) enhance GABA at the GABA-A receptor and act fast but bring sedation, falls, dependence and withdrawal, so they suit only short-term relief; they do not treat OCD. Buspirone, a 5-HT1A partial agonist, is slow and non-sedating and is used for GAD; beta blockers blunt the bodily signs but do not treat social anxiety disorder (Liebowitz, 1992). The SSRIs and SNRIs are first-line, and in OCD only strongly serotonergic drugs work, clomipramine first among them. Large trials in panic disorder (2000) and OCD (Foa, 2005) found the combination no better than psychological treatment alone. Cingulotomy, capsulotomy and deep brain stimulation are last resorts for severe OCD; in India, section 96 of the Mental Healthcare Act 2017 governs psychosurgery.

From the past papers

  1. Someone diagnosed as suffering from Agoraphobia would be (JEMAS 2025)

    (a) hearing voices telling him or her to do certain things (b) feeling very nervous in an elevator (c) feeling very fearful of tall trees or buildings (d) feeling an irrational fear of public places

    Answer: (d) feeling an irrational fear of public places. Agoraphobia (Westphal, 1871; literally fear of the marketplace) is marked fear of two or more of five situations, public transport, open spaces, enclosed places, queues or crowds and being outside the home alone, because escape might be difficult or help unavailable if panic-like symptoms strike. Hearing voices is a psychotic symptom, nervousness in a lift on its own is claustrophobia (a situational specific phobia), and fear of tall trees or buildings is acrophobia (natural environment type).

  2. Panic disorder is where: (RML 2021)

    (a) An individual experiences a singular panic attack (b) There is excessive anxiety and worry about many different aspects of life (c) There is a fear of panicking in enclosed spaces (d) There are recurrent and unexpected panic attacks

    Answer: (d) There are recurrent and unexpected panic attacks. DSM-5-TR requires recurrent unexpected attacks, at least one followed by a month or more of persistent worry about further attacks or a maladaptive change in behaviour. A single attack is common and is not a disorder; worry about many aspects of life is generalised anxiety disorder; and a fear of panicking in enclosed spaces is a situational specific phobia, or agoraphobia if two or more of the five situations are feared.

  3. A phobia is best described as a: (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Persistent irrational fear of a specific object or situation (b) Mild stress reaction (c) Personality disorder (d) Temporary hallucination

    Answer: (a) Persistent irrational fear of a specific object or situation. The fear is out of proportion to the real danger, is set off almost every time the object or situation is met, and is avoided or endured with dread; DSM-5-TR asks that it typically last 6 months. A mild stress reaction fades as the person adapts, a personality disorder is a pervasive pattern of traits, and a hallucination is a perceptual symptom, not a fear.

  4. The specific phobia of pain is known as: (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Nosophobia (b) Algophobia (c) Cynophobia (d) Hematophobia

    Answer: (b) Algophobia. Algos is Greek for pain (as in analgesic) and phobos is fear. The distractors are real names for other fears: nosophobia is fear of disease (nosos, as in nosology), cynophobia fear of dogs (kyon, kynos) and haematophobia fear of blood (haima).

  5. Which of the following is a symptom of generalised anxiety disorder (GAD)? (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Mania (b) Persistent, excessive worry (c) Hallucinations (d) Mood swings

    Answer: (b) Persistent, excessive worry. GAD is defined by excessive anxiety and worry about a number of events, more days than not for at least 6 months, that the person finds hard to control, with three of six symptoms such as restlessness, muscle tension and sleep disturbance. Mania and mood swings are mood symptoms (Chapter 5) and hallucinations are psychotic (Chapter 4); none is a criterion of GAD.

  6. Generalized Anxiety Disorder (GAD) is a pervasive condition in which the sufferer experiences: (IHBAS 2024)

    (a) Continued apprehension about unspecified things (b) Fear of fear (c) Continual flashbacks to past events (d) A desire to check that the environment is safe

    Answer: (a) Continued apprehension about unspecified things. The anxiety of GAD is free-floating: no single object or theme defines it, and the worry moves from topic to topic. Fear of fear (Goldstein and Chambless, 1978) belongs to panic disorder and agoraphobia, continual flashbacks are the re-experiencing of PTSD (Chapter 7), and a desire to check that the environment is safe is best read as the checking of OCD.

  7. Which one is NOT a compulsive behaviour in obsessive-compulsive disorder? (Amity 2023)

    (a) Tapping (b) Hoarding (c) Washing (d) Checking

    Answer: (b) Hoarding. Tapping (a symmetry or "just right" ritual), washing (the cleaning dimension) and checking (the harm dimension) are textbook compulsions. Hoarding has been a separate obsessive-compulsive and related disorder since DSM-5, because it lacks intrusive obsessions and the possessions are valued rather than feared; saving that is driven by a true obsession is still diagnosed as OCD, but that is the exception.

  8. Which of the following best describes the concept of "ego-dystonic" as it applies to obsessions in OCD? (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) The individual experiences the obsessions as a core part of their identity (b) The individual experiences the obsessions as intrusive, unwanted, and inconsistent with their self-concept (c) The individual derives pleasure from the obsessive thoughts (d) The individual is completely unaware that their obsessions are unreasonable

    Answer: (b) intrusive, unwanted, and inconsistent with their self-concept. The person recognises the obsession as a product of his own mind yet feels it as alien and resists it. Feeling it as part of one's identity is ego-syntonic, the way OCPD traits are felt; pleasure belongs to paraphilic fantasies and impulse-control acts, never to obsessions; and complete unawareness that the obsessions are unreasonable is absent insight, a specifier, not ego-dystonia.

  9. Persistent preoccupation with perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable to others is characteristic of which disorder? (Real PYQ 2025)

    (a) Somatic Symptom Disorder (b) Body Dysmorphic Disorder (c) Illness Anxiety Disorder (d) Conversion Disorder

    Answer: (b) Body Dysmorphic Disorder. BDD, Morselli's dysmorphophobia (1891), is preoccupation with perceived flaws that are not observable or appear slight to others, with repetitive mirror checking, grooming, comparing or reassurance seeking, and DSM-5 placed it among the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders. The distractors are real disorders about the body (Chapter 7): somatic symptom disorder is distress about bodily symptoms, illness anxiety disorder fear of having a serious illness, and conversion disorder unexplained neurological symptoms.

  10. Suffocation Alarm Theories explain the clinical condition of: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Down Syndrome (b) Autistic Spectrum Disorder (c) Delirium (d) Panic Disorder

    Answer: (d) Panic Disorder. Donald Klein's suffocation false alarm theory (1993) proposes that people prone to panic have an oversensitive suffocation monitor that fires at harmless rises in carbon dioxide, which is why carbon dioxide and lactate challenges provoke attacks in them far more readily than in others. Down syndrome (trisomy 21) and autism spectrum disorder are neurodevelopmental (Chapter 3), and delirium is an acute, fluctuating confusional state (Chapter 10).

  11. A client with OCD washes their hands excessively to neutralize the anxiety caused by intrusive thoughts of contamination. This hand-washing behavior is BEST conceptualized as a(n): (Real PYQ 2024)

    (a) Conditioned stimulus (b) Unconditioned response (c) Avoidance response / Safety behavior (d) Operant behavior maintained by positive reinforcement

    Answer: (c) Avoidance response / Safety behavior. In Mowrer's two-factor theory applied to OCD, the contamination thought becomes a conditioned stimulus for anxiety, and washing, which brings relief, is an escape or avoidance response strengthened by negative reinforcement that also stops the person from learning that nothing bad would happen. It is not the conditioned stimulus (the thought or object is), not an unconditioned response (an unlearned reflex such as a startle), and not positively reinforced, because it removes distress rather than adding anything pleasant.

  12. From the perspective of learning theory 'compulsive acts' related to OCD are explained as patterns of ___________ behaviours. (IHBAS 2025)

    (a) Performance (b) Conditioning (c) Escape (d) Involuntary

    Answer: (c) Escape. The ritual ends the anxiety raised by the obsession, and the relief negatively reinforces it, as Hodgson and Rachman's washing experiments (1972) showed. "Conditioning" is defensible as the general mechanism but does not name the pattern the acts show; compulsions are not involuntary (they can be delayed and resisted, unlike tics), and "performance" is not a learning-theory pattern of behaviour.

  13. Catastrophic thinking is common to several anxiety disorders. The term describes: (RML 2021)

    (a) Categorising all experiences as either extremely positive or extremely negative (b) Focusing on negative events and ignoring positive ones (c) Worrying that the worst will always happen (d) Taking things very personally

    Answer: (c) Worrying that the worst will always happen. Catastrophising expects the worst possible outcome and treats it as likely and unbearable, usually as a chain of ever worse consequences; Beck made overestimated danger the core of anxiety. The other options are different distortions: dichotomous (all-or-nothing) thinking, selective abstraction (the mental filter) and personalisation.

  14. 'Displacement' is a defense mechanism typically seen in: (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Hysteria (b) Phobias (c) Obsessive-compulsive disorder (d) Depression

    Answer: (b) Phobias. In Freud's reading of Little Hans (1909), the boy's fear of his father, whom he loved and could not avoid, was displaced onto horses, which he could avoid. Hysteria rests on repression and conversion, OCD on isolation, undoing and reaction formation, and depression, in the psychoanalytic account, on introjection and anger turned against the self.

  15. The following defense mechanisms are used in obsessive-compulsive disorder: (RML 2024)

    (a) Isolation (b) Undoing (c) Reaction formation (d) Displacement

    Answer: (b) Undoing, as keyed; but (a) and (c) are also correct. Undoing, an act that symbolically cancels a forbidden thought (the Rat Man moving a stone off the road and then putting it back), is the defence most directly tied to compulsive rituals, which is why the key picks it. Isolation of affect and reaction formation are OCD defences too, so only displacement, the defence of phobia, is wrong; if a paper allows several answers, choose (a), (b) and (c).

  16. Which of the following defense mechanisms is not involved in obsessive-compulsive disorder? (IHBAS 2020)

    (a) Reaction formation (b) Repression (c) Isolation (d) Undoing

    Answer: (b) Repression. Repression, which pushes the forbidden idea out of consciousness and turns its energy into a bodily symptom, is the defence of hysteria. In OCD the forbidden idea stays conscious: its feeling is isolated, it is cancelled by undoing, or it is held in check by its exaggerated opposite in reaction formation.

  17. The defence mechanism of Undoing is typically seen in: (Amity 2023)

    (a) Depression (b) Mania (c) Obsessive compulsive neurosis (d) Schizophrenia

    Answer: (c) Obsessive compulsive neurosis. Undoing is the hallmark defence of obsessional neurosis, and washing after a "dirty" thought or praying to cancel a blasphemous one are its everyday forms. Depression is linked with introjection and anger turned inward, mania with Melanie Klein's "manic defence" against depressive feelings, and schizophrenia with the psychotic defences of regression, projection and denial of reality.

High-yield sheet

The facts this chapter's questions ask most, most-asked first. Last read before the exam.

  1. The defences of OCD (Freud's obsessional neurosis, the Rat Man, 1909) are isolation of affect, undoing and reaction formation, with intellectualisation; undoing, an act that symbolically cancels a forbidden thought, is the most characteristic and is "typically seen in obsessive-compulsive neurosis". Repression is the defence of hysteria and is not involved in OCD. asked 3 times
  2. In learning theory (Mowrer's two-factor theory, 1947, applied to OCD) compulsive acts are escape or avoidance responses (safety behaviours), maintained by negative reinforcement as anxiety falls; they are not conditioned stimuli, unconditioned responses, positively reinforced operants or involuntary acts. asked 2 times
  3. The cardinal DSM-5 feature of OCD: obsessions and/or compulsions that are time-consuming (more than 1 hour a day) or cause clinically significant distress or impairment. Contamination thoughts with ritual washing for over an hour a day are OCD, not GAD, illness anxiety disorder or specific phobia. asked 3 times
  4. Ego-dystonic obsessions are experienced as intrusive, unwanted and inconsistent with one's self-concept, although recognised as one's own thoughts; not as part of one's identity (ego-syntonic), not as pleasurable, and not the same as lacking insight, which is a specifier. asked 2 times
  5. Body dysmorphic disorder: preoccupation with perceived defects or flaws in appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others, with repetitive mirror checking, grooming, comparing or reassurance seeking (Morselli's dysmorphophobia, 1891); not somatic symptom, illness anxiety or conversion disorder. asked 2 times
  6. Agoraphobia (Westphal, 1871; "fear of the marketplace") is an irrational fear of public places: marked fear of two or more of five situations (public transport, open spaces, enclosed places, queues or crowds, being outside the home alone) because escape might be difficult or help unavailable, typically for 6 months, diagnosed independently of panic disorder since DSM-5. A lift alone is claustrophobia; tall buildings, acrophobia. asked 2 times
  7. OCD versus OCPD: OCD has ego-dystonic intrusive obsessions and compulsions; obsessive-compulsive personality disorder is an ego-syntonic, pervasive pattern of perfectionism and control without true obsessions or compulsions. They are separate disorders that can coexist. asked 2 times
  8. GAD duration: DSM-5-TR requires excessive worry more days than not for at least 6 months in adults; ICD-11 asks for "at least several months", not a fixed 6, and ICD-10's guidelines for several weeks, usually several months. asked 2 times
  9. A panic attack is an abrupt surge of intense fear or discomfort that peaks within minutes, with four or more of thirteen symptoms; it is not itself a disorder and is recorded as a specifier in other disorders. asked 2 times
  10. The defining symptom of GAD is persistent, excessive, hard-to-control worry about many events, with three of six symptoms (restlessness, easy fatigue, poor concentration, irritability, muscle tension, sleep disturbance; one in children). Mania, mood swings and hallucinations are not GAD symptoms. asked once
  11. GAD is continued apprehension about unspecified things (free-floating anxiety). Fear of fear (Goldstein and Chambless, 1978) belongs to panic disorder and agoraphobia, continual flashbacks to PTSD, and checking to OCD. asked once
  12. Panic disorder: recurrent unexpected panic attacks, at least one followed by 1 month or more of persistent concern or maladaptive change in behaviour; a single attack is not panic disorder. asked once
  13. Donald Klein's suffocation false alarm theory (1993) explains panic disorder: an oversensitive suffocation monitor fires at harmless rises in carbon dioxide (and lactate). It has nothing to do with Down syndrome, autism or delirium. asked once
  14. Catastrophic thinking (catastrophising) is worrying that the worst will always happen. All-or-nothing categorising is dichotomous thinking, focusing on negatives is selective abstraction, and taking things personally is personalisation. asked once
  15. Hoarding is not an OCD compulsion: since DSM-5 it is a separate obsessive-compulsive and related disorder. Tapping, washing and checking are compulsions. asked once
  16. Displacement is the defence typically seen in phobias: Little Hans's fear of his father displaced onto horses (Freud, 1909); not hysteria (repression), OCD or depression. asked once
  17. A phobia is a persistent, irrational fear of a specific object or situation, out of proportion to the danger and avoided or endured with dread; not a mild stress reaction, a personality disorder or a hallucination. asked once
  18. Algophobia is the fear of pain (algos); nosophobia is fear of disease, cynophobia of dogs and haematophobia of blood. asked once
  19. DSM-5 classifies body dysmorphic disorder under the obsessive-compulsive and related disorders, not under somatic symptom and related disorders (its somatoform home in DSM-IV), anxiety disorders or personality disorders. asked once
  20. Specific phobia needs fear typically lasting 6 months or more, as do social anxiety disorder and agoraphobia; panic disorder needs 1 month of worry, separation anxiety disorder 4 weeks in children, and selective mutism 1 month. asked once
  21. The biological system most implicated in OCD is the central serotonin (5-HT) system: clomipramine and the SSRIs help. GABA is the system of the anxiety disorders and the benzodiazepines; glutamate and dopamine are involved only secondarily. asked once
